Applicant	Amivas, LLC
Dosage form	Intravenous
Applicant Proposed Dosing Regimen	(b) (4)
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	Initial treatment of severe malaria in adults and children. (b) (4)
Applicant Proposed SNOMED CT Indication Disease Term for each Proposed Indication	61462000 | Malaria (disorder)
Regulatory Action	APPROVAL
Indication(s)/Population(s)	Initial treatment of severe malaria in adult and pediatric patients
SNOMED CT Indication Disease Term for each Indication	61462000 | Malaria (disorder)
Recommended Dosing Regimen	2.4 mg/kg administered intravenously at 0 hours, 12 hours and 24 hours, and thereafter, administered once daily until the patient is able to tolerate oral antimalarial therapy

L58: ## 1 Executive Summary
L59: ## 1.1 Product Introduction
L60: Artesunate for Injection is a semi-synthetic anti-malarial drug in the class of artemisinins. Each single-dose vial contains 110 mg of artesunate for constitution with sodium phosphate buffer. Artesunate for Injection is to be administered at 2.4 mg/kg/dose as a slow intravenous bolus infusion over 1 to 2 minutes.
L61: (b) (4)
L62: Derived from
L63: (b) (4)
L64: Study of artemisinins for the treatment of severe malaria began over 30 years ago. (Myint and Shwe 1987) The mode of action involves iron-catalyzed cleavage of the endoperoxide bridge with generation of free radicals and alkylation of malaria-specific proteins, inhibiting growth and replication of *Plasmodium*. (Kamchonwongpaisan and Meshnick 1996) Artesunate, a succinic acid ester of artemisinin, is water soluble and is rapidly hydrolyzed to dihydroartemisinin (DHA), its active metabolite.
L65: Artesunate and DHA are active against the erythrocytic asexual blood stage *Plasmodium* parasites, affecting rapid clearance of parasitemia generally within 48 to 72 hours.
L66: ## 1.2 Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L67: The Applicant has provided substantial evidence of effectiveness and sufficient safety data to support the approval of Artesunate for Injection for the initial treatment of severe malaria in adult and pediatric patients. IV artesunate treatment resulted in an improved survival over the previous standard of care (quinine) in a single, randomized, active-controlled trial in adults and pediatric patients with severe malaria in Asia (SEAQUAMAT), with supportive evidence from a published, randomized, active-controlled trial in pediatric patients with severe malaria in Africa (AQUAMAT). Both trials were conducted by the Wellcome Trust using parenteral artesunate manufactured by Guilin Pharmaceuticals. The Applicant submitted patient level data from SEAQUAMAT and sufficient information to support the scientific bridge based on comparative Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls information and comparable pharmacokinetics of the proposed drug product from the Phase 1 and Phase 2 studies (1128, 1142 and 1168) and the Guilin product used in the published clinical trials.
L68: SEAQUAMAT, which enrolled 1461 adults and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older, had a primary efficacy endpoint of in-hospital mortality. The in-hospital mortality in the IV artesunate group (13.2%) was significantly lower than in the comparator group receiving IV quinine (20.5%), OR 0.59 (95% CI: 0.44, 0.79, p=0.0002). AQUAMAT, which enrolled pediatric patients younger than 15 years of age, evaluated IV artesunate at a similar dose and regimen as SEAQUAMAT except that a total of 35.5% of patients received artesunate or quinine
L69: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L70: intramuscularly. Treatment with parenteral artesunate in AQUAMAT resulted in a similar effect on reducing in-hospital mortality compared to IV quinine as observed in SEAQUAMAT. The in-hospital mortality in the artesunate group (8.5%) was significantly lower than in the comparator group receiving quinine (10.9%), OR 0.75 (95% CI: 0.63, 0.90, p=0.0022).
L71: Although patient-level data were only available from SEAQUAMAT, the data were considered adequate to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness based on the persuasive finding of mortality benefit in this trial with a similarly robust mortality benefit reported in the AQUAMAT publication. Additional supportive data were provided from a study of two artesunate dosing regimens in pediatric patients with severe malaria (EDCPT-MMV07-01).
L72: ## 1.3 Benefit-Risk Assessment
L73: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L74: In NDA 213036, the Applicant is seeking approval for Artesunate for Injection for intravenous (IV) use for the initial treatment of severe malaria in adults and pediatric patients. Artesunate for Injection is intended to be administered intravenously at 0 hours, 12 hours, 24 hours and thereafter once daily until oral therapy is tolerated and must be followed by a complete course of oral antimalarial therapy.
L75: Severe malaria is a life-threatening infection and is a medical emergency. Most cases are due to *Plasmodium falciparum*. There are currently no approved drugs in the US for treatment of severe malaria. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the World Health Organization (WHO) currently recommend IV artesunate as the first-line treatment of severe malaria in adults, children and pregnant women. IV artesunate is currently available in the US from the CDC under an Investigational New Drug (IND) expanded access treatment protocol.
L76: IV artesunate was superior to IV quinine, the standard of care when the study was conducted, for the treatment of severe malaria in a single, randomized, open-label, active-controlled trial in adults and pediatric patients in Southeast Asia (SEAQUAMAT), with supportive evidence from a published, randomized, open-label, active-controlled trial in pediatric patients in Africa (AQUAMAT). The primary efficacy endpoint for both trials was in-hospital mortality. In SEAQUAMAT, which enrolled patients two years of age and older with severe malaria, in-hospital mortality was 13% in artesunate recipients compared to 21% in quinine recipients [OR 0.59, 95% CI (0.44, 0.79), p=0.0002]. AQUAMAT, which enrolled pediatric patients less than 15 years of age, demonstrated a similar significant survival benefit for parenteral artesunate compared to parenteral quinine. The in-hospital mortality in the artesunate group (8.5%) was significantly lower than in the comparator group receiving quinine (10.9%), OR 0.75 (95% CI: 0.63, 0.90, p=0.0022).
L77: Safety data for IV artesunate were obtained from SEAQUAMAT and an uncontrolled, open-label, retrospective study in U.S. patients with severe malaria treated with Artesunate for Injection under the IND expanded access treatment protocol (CDC-060). Additional supportive evidence for safety was provided by AQUAMAT, a study of artesunate dosing regimens in pediatric patients with severe malaria, EDCPT-MMV07-01, a phase 2 trial of two dosing regimens of IV artesunate in pediatric patients with severe malaria, two phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria, and two phase 1 studies in healthy adults. In SEAQUAMAT, the most common adverse reactions in patients receiving IV artesunate included acute renal failure requiring dialysis, hemoglobinuria, and jaundice. The most frequently observed adverse reactions in CDC-060 were anemia, elevation of transaminases, thrombocytopenia, hyperbilirubinemia, and acute renal failure. In SEAQUAMAT, the incidence of neurological impairment at the time of hospital discharge was higher in the artesunate group compared to the quinine; similar
L78: findings were reported in AQUAMAT.
L79: Post-artemisinin delayed hemolysis (PADH), defined as a 10% decrease in hemoglobin associated with laboratory findings of hemolysis occurring more than one week after treatment initiation, has been reported following IV artesunate therapy and is associated with hyperparasitemia. Because severe PADH may require transfusions, patients should be monitored with weekly blood counts for up to four weeks after treatment.
L80: Hypersensitivity reactions including anaphylaxis has been reported following IV artesunate administration. In the phase 1 clinical development of IV artesunate, one case of urticaria was observed in a healthy volunteer. In a phase 2 study of IV artesunate in uncomplicated malaria, Stevens Johnson Syndrome was documented in a single patient. Transient neutropenia and a decrease in reticulocyte count were observed following Artesunate for Injection in phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers and phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria.
L81: In nonclinical studies, embryo-fetal toxicity (embryolethality and malformations) was observed in three different animal species administered intravenous or oral artesunate in early gestation. However, the clinical relevance of these findings is unclear since extensive experience with oral artesunate and other artemisinin class drugs (via various routes of administration) in pregnant women has not identified a drug-associated risk of adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. The Applicant is required to conduct a postmarketing study to collect data on women exposed to artesunate during pregnancy to assess the risk of maternal and fetal complications.
L82: The Artesunate for Injection labeling includes warnings for post-treatment hemolysis, hypersensitivity, and embryo-fetal toxicity in animals.
L83: In summary, the studies submitted demonstrated a clinically meaningful survival benefit of IV artesunate treatment in patients with severe malaria when compared to quinine the previous standard of care in well-controlled trials. The results were robust and consistent in adults and pediatric patients across different geographic regions. The primary safety concern with artesunate is the risk of delayed hemolysis, though the risk can be mitigated with post-treatment laboratory monitoring.
L84: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Severe malaria is a life-threatening infection and is a medical emergency.
Most cases of severe malaria are due to P. falciparum, but the condition may occur with other species such as P. vivax.
Approximately 2000 cases of malaria are diagnosed each year in the U.S. with about 300 classified as severe. Most cases occur in travelers and immigrants from malaria-endemic
Severe malaria is associated with significant morbidity and mortality.
Higher morbidity and mortality are found in pregnant women, children, and
L85: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L86: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
regions of the world.
Under the CDC IND expanded access protocol a total of 477 patients have been treated with IV artesunate in the US from 2007 to 2019; 31 deaths have been reported (6.5% of total treated).
immunocompromised patients.
Malaria can progress to severe or life-threatening disease if diagnosis is delayed or overlooked; timely access to effective therapy is essential.

Current Treatment Options	Quinidine, the only FDA-approved drug for severe malaria, is no longer marketed for use in the US, and IV quinine, the prior standard of care treatment worldwide, is not approved for use in the U.S.
Oral antimalarials approved by FDA for uncomplicated malaria such as artemether/lumefantrine (Coartem), mefloquine hydrochloride, atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone), and quinine sulfate are sometimes administered off-label to patients with severe malaria until IV artesunate is available; however, oral therapy is often not tolerated due to the severity of clinical illness and may be associated with treatment failures in patients with marked hyperparasitemia.
The CDC and the WHO currently recommend parenteral artesunate as the first-line treatment of severe malaria in adults, children and pregnant women.
IV artesunate is currently available in the US from the CDC under an IND expanded access protocol.
There are currently no FDA-approved drugs for treatment of severe malaria in the U.S.

Benefit	IV artesunate was evaluated in a single, randomized, open-label, active-controlled trial in Asia in adults and pediatric patients with severe malaria (SEAQUAMAT), with supportive evidence from a published, randomized, open-label, active-controlled trial in Africa in pediatric patients younger than 15 years of age (AQUAMAT).
The primary efficacy endpoint for both SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT was in-hospital mortality.
In SEAQUAMAT, in-hospital mortality in artesunate recipients was 13.2% (96 of 730) compared with 20.5% (150 of 731) in quinine recipients; odds ratio 0.59 (95% CI 0.44, 0.79, p=0.0002). AQUAMAT demonstrated a similar advantage with respect to in-hospital mortality.
Substantial evidence of artesunate's effectiveness for the treatment of severe malaria has been demonstrated in adults and children. Significant survival benefit was observed over quinine, the previous standard of care.
L87: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L88: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
These efficacy findings were robust across age, gender and geographic regions.
Limitations of these studies included limited follow up which ended upon death or hospital discharge, and the absence of data on malarial species other than P. falciparum.

Risk and Risk Management	The safety database consisted of 832 patients who received IV artesunate at 2.4 mg/kg/dose in SEAQUAMAT and CDC-060, an uncontrolled, open-label, retrospective study from U.S. patients receiving IV artesunate under an expanded access IND, with supportive evidence for safety from published studies AQUAMAT and EDCPT-MMV07-01, a controlled trial in pediatric patients comparing two dose regimens, two phase 2 studies of uncomplicated malaria, and two phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers.
Most common adverse reactions with IV artesunate in SEAQUAMAT included acute renal failure requiring dialysis, hemoglobinuria and jaundice. The most frequently observed adverse reactions with Artesunate for Injection in CDC-060 were anemia, elevation of transaminases, thrombocytopenia, hyperbilirubinemia, and acute renal failure.
In SEAQUAMAT, a higher incidence of neurological impairment at the time of hospital discharge was reported in artesunate recipients compared to the comparator; similar findings were reported in AQUAMAT. As neurological sequelae can occur following severe malaria, particularly patients with cerebral malaria, the higher incidence in artesunate recipients may be due to improved survival in these artesunate-treated patients.
Delayed hemolysis following administration of artemisinins has been reported in published literature. This condition, defined as a 10% decrease in hemoglobin associated with laboratory findings of hemolysis occurring more than one week after treatment initiation, occurs more frequently in patients hyperparasitemia, and may require transfusions.
Hypersensitivity reactions including anaphylaxis have been reported following IV artesunate administration.
Transient neutropenia and a decrease in reticulocyte count were observed following Artesunate for Injection in phase 1 studies of healthy volunteers and phase 2 studies of patients with uncomplicated malaria.
Artesunate demonstrated an acceptable safety profile for the treatment of severe malaria.
As the anemia associated with delayed hemolysis may be significant enough to require transfusions, patients should be monitored with weekly blood counts for 4 weeks after initiating IV artesunate therapy. The labeling includes a warning for delayed hemolysis following Artesunate for Injection.
The Applicant is required to conduct postmarketing studies to evaluate the potential development of artesunate resistance and to collect data on women exposed to artesunate during pregnancy to assess the risk of maternal and fetal complications.
The labeling includes a warning for hypersensitivity and a contraindication for patients with known serious hypersensitivity to artesunate.
The labeling includes a warning for embryo-
L89: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L90: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
In nonclinical studies, embryo-fetal toxicity was observed in animals administered intravenous or oral artesunate. However, the clinical relevance of this finding is unclear since experience with oral artesunate and other artemisinin class drugs (via various routes of administration) in pregnant women over several decades has not identified a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes.
fetal toxicity in animals that also cautions providers that delaying treatment of severe malaria in pregnancy may result in serious morbidity and mortality to the mother and fetus.
The Applicant is required to conduct postmarket nonclinical studies to assess artesunate effects on male and female fertility in rats, in an in vivo Micronucleus Assay and in a bacterial reverse mutation test.

L94: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L95: ## 2.1 Analysis of Condition
L96: In 2017, malaria was associated with an estimated 219 million cases and 435,000 deaths worldwide. Approximately 92% to 93% of malaria cases and deaths occurred in Africa. *P. falciparum* was associated with 99.7% of malaria cases in Africa, 62.8% in South-East Asia, 69% in the Eastern Mediterranean, and 71.9% in the Western Pacific. (World Health Organization 2018) In the U.S., most cases of malaria occur in travelers and immigrants returning from malaria-endemic regions in sub-Saharan Africa or South Asia. In 2016, 2078 cases of confirmed malaria were reported in the U.S. *P. falciparum* was found in 1,419 of 1,853 (76.6%) with an identified species. (Mace et al. 2019)
L97: Patients with malaria caused by *P. falciparum* can progress to severe or life-threatening disease if diagnosis is delayed or overlooked. (Pasvol 2006) In 2016, 305 of 2078 (14.7%) U.S. cases were classified as severe malaria; of these 7 (2.3%) died. (Mace et al. 2019) Severe malaria is characterized by hyperparasitemia, destruction of parasitized red cells, and sequestration of red cells in small blood vessels causing end-organ dysfunction or failure. The World Health Organization (WHO) defines severe falciparum malaria as having one or more of the following: impaired consciousness, prostration, multiple seizures, shock, pulmonary edema, recurrent or prolonged bleeding, hypoglycemia, acidosis, severe anemia, hyperparasitemia, renal impairment, and/or jaundice. (World Health Organization 2015)
L98: Following the publication of SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT, in 2010, the WHO recommended parenteral artesunate as preferred treatment for severe malaria in adults, children, and pregnant women in the second and third trimester, due to its reported superior efficacy, reduced mortality, and better adverse event profile compared to quinine. (World Health Organization 2010) In 2015, the WHO extended the preference for parenteral artesunate for the treatment of severe malaria in pregnant women during all trimesters. (World Health Organization 2015)
L99: ## 2.2 Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L100: Parenteral antimalarial therapy is recommended in patients with severe malaria.
L101: The only approved parenteral antimalarial therapy for severe malaria in the US was quinidine gluconate, an anti-arrhythmic drug. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2019) Parenteral quinidine gluconate is cardiotoxic and may cause ventricular arrhythmia, hypotension, hypoglycemia, and prolongation of the QTc interval; administration in an intensive care setting is recommended. In 2017, the manufacturer discontinued production of intravenous quinidine gluconate and distribution in the U.S. was discontinued in March 2019. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2017).
L102: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L103: Both WHO and CDC recommend parenteral artesunate as the first line therapy for severe malaria. Intravenous artesunate has been available in the U.S. since 2007 through the CDC IND expanded access protocol (IND 076725). (Paczkowski et al. 2014) Under the CDC IND protocol, a total of 477 patients have been treated with IV artesunate from 2007 to 2018, averaging 48 patients per year with 31 deaths reported (6.5% of total treated). During the last full reporting period from June 2018 to June 2019, a total of 63 patients were enrolled with two deaths documented; all patients received the U.S. Army formulation of IV artesunate.1 IV artesunate (U.S. Army formulation) is also available to U.S. military personnel under IND 064769.2
L104: IV quinine is not commercially available in the US. FDA-approved oral antimalarials such as artemether/lumefantrine (Coartem), mefloquine hydrochloride, atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone), and quinine sulfate are indicated for treatment of uncomplicated malaria. While these antimalarials may be used off-label for severe malaria or until IV artesunate is available, oral therapy is often not tolerated due to the severity of clinical illness and may be associated with treatment failures in patients with marked hyperparasitemia.
L105: In its 2015 guidelines for treatment of malaria, the WHO revised its pediatric dose recommendation for parenteral artesunate from 2.4 mg/kg to 3 mg/kg (World Health Organization 2015); CDC's current dosing recommendation in children mirrors the WHO recommendations. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2019) The WHO based its revision on a review of PK data on artesunate and DHA in published studies of 71 adults and 265 children and simulations of exposures in various age groups which predicted underexposure of younger children. (Hendriksen et al. 2013) (Zaloumis et al. 2014) In its review, the WHO noted that parenteral artesunate doses between 1.75 and 4 mg/kg have been evaluated without any toxicity observed. (World Health Organization 2015) (See Section 12, Pediatrics and 18.4 Pharmacometrics review of the proposed artesunate dosing in pediatric patients, for additional discussion of pediatric dosing.)

L116: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L117: ## 3.1 U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L118: Artesunate for injection (IV artesunate) is being developed for the initial treatment of severe malaria in adult and pediatric patients. IV artesunate is a new molecular entity and is not currently marketed in the United States. The Department of the Army sponsored the IND and Amivas is the applicant of the NDA.
L119: ## 3.2 Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L120: The Office of the Surgeon General, Department of the Army (US Army), submitted an IND for the development of IV artesunate for the treatment of severe (b) (4) malaria. While under development multiple submissions were received and meetings held with the Agency and the IND received fast track, breakthrough and orphan designations.
L121: In 2017, the US Army entered into a Cooperative Research and Development Agreement with Amivas, LLC (Amivas). Amivas was designated as the co-development partner and commercial partner responsible for the submission of the New Drug Application for Artesunate for Injection for the immediate treatment of malaria. The US Army also provided Amivas with a letter of authorization to allow the US Food and Drug Administration review of all pertinent information in their IND in support of the NDA.
L122: During the Pre-NDA discussions in 2019, both the US Army and Amivas participated in multiple meetings with the Agency, and a request for NDA rolling review was submitted and granted. On August 12, 2019, Amivas, LLC, began their rolling review submission of NDA 213036 for Artesunate for Injection for the initial treatment of severe (b) (4) malaria in adults and children; NDA submission was completed on September 26, 2019. The submission included a request for Priority Review and for issuance of a Tropical Disease Priority Review Voucher at the time of approval.

The microbiology review had identified deficiencies with the Container Closure Integrity Test (CCIT) and the drug product review identified a deficiency with the analytical method used in the leachables study. Following review of the IR responses, both the microbiology and drug product reviews recommended this NDA for approval; however, an overall “Withhold” recommendation for the facilities was entered into Panorama on 04/17/2020. An overall “Approve” recommendation for the facilities was entered into Panorama on 05/19/2020. For further details, refer to the CMC Integrated Quality Assessment submitted to DARRTS on 5/21/2020.

L131: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L132: ## 5.1 Executive Summary
L133: Artesunate is a semi-synthetic derivative of artemisinin, [REDACTED](b) (4). The nonclinical safety profile consists of pharmacology, pharmacokinetics and toxicology studies *in vitro* and *in vivo* in rats, dogs, rabbits and monkeys. Pivotal studies with artesunate discussed in this review include repeated-dose IV studies (up to 28 days), embryo-fetal development studies conducted via oral administration, and genetic toxicology studies.
L134: Although the subject of this NDA is IV artesunate, some of the submitted supporting literature and nonclinical studies were conducted using artemisinin and other artemisinin derivatives (i.e., arteether and artemether) to illustrate how artesunate compares across areas of known artemisinin class effects. Oral artemether, the only FDA approved artemisinin, is part of a fixed oral combination therapy with lumefantrine (Coartem®, approved 2009; Novartis Pharmaceuticals Corp) for treatment of uncomplicated malaria. Studies early in the drug development phase with artesunate were conducted primarily using a non-GMP formulation of IV artesunate produced in China by Guilin Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd. More recent nonclinical studies were conducted using a GMP Walter Reed Army Institute of Research (WRAIR) formulation of artesunate [REDACTED](b) (4). The pivotal repeated-dose toxicity studies in rat and dog were conducted using the to-be-marketed formulation of semi-synthetic artesunate in 0.3 M phosphate buffer. No nonclinical bioequivalence studies have been conducted between the semi-synthetic formulation and the [REDACTED](b) (4) WRAIR or Guilin formulations. In general, animal studies conducted using the [REDACTED](b) (4) WRAIR and semi-synthetic IV artesunate formulations show a consistent pattern of toxicity across species (described below).
L135: ## Pharmacokinetics
L136: Artesunate (AS) is rapidly hydrolyzed to its metabolite dihydroartemisin (DHA) following intravenous administration, with a half-life of less than 0.5 hr for AS and DHA across all species. A mass balance study in rats indicate artesunate has a biphasic pattern of distribution, with an initial peak concentration of radioactivity present in plasma and whole blood 5 min after dosing, with a secondary drug plasma peak concentration occurring 3 to 4 hr after dosing, suggesting enterohepatic circulation or the presence of a second absorption phase. In rats, artesunate is present in whole blood at concentrations 2 to 4-fold that of plasma, with plasma protein binding capacities of 66 to 81% (comparable to human plasma protein binding of 63 to 75%). The rate and extent of artesunate metabolism to DHA varies by species, with extent of DHA formation in liver microsomes following artesunate incubation greatest in humans (85%), followed by rat (76%), cynomolgus monkey (59%), then rhesus monkey and beagle dog (both at 44%). Glucuronidation is a major metabolic pathway for both DHA, mainly via UGT1A9 and
L137: UGT2B7. After IV administration, AS was mainly excreted via the urine (56%), with approximately 38% of the administered dose excreted via the feces. No nonclinical studies evaluating the excretion of artesunate in milk have been conducted.
L138: ## Safety Pharmacology
L139: No studies have been conducted with AS on the inhibition of the tail current in human embryonic kidney 293 cells (transfected with hERG cDNA). The IC_{50} for DHA on hERG current was 7.7 \pm 0.9 mcM. No adverse effects on cardiovascular function were observed in conscious beagle dogs following single or repeated (up to 27 days) IV dosing up to 40 mg/kg, approximately nine times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparison.
L140: Neurobehavioral assessments in rats conducted after 26 days of repeated IV dosing of AS (3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) showed no biologically significant effects on parameters evaluated in a standard functional observational battery (e.g., gait and posture, motor movements, piloerection, etc.). Neurotoxicity is a class effect of artemisinins, with artemisinin and artemisinin-derived compounds (i.e., artemether and arteether) displaying a pattern of damage to specific brainstem nuclei in experimental animals when administered in intoxication or lethal doses. The extent of neurotoxicity seen following administration of artemisinins appears to be dependent on the specific compound, dose, vehicle, and route of administration. Intramuscular administration of arteether causes widespread neuronal necrosis and gliosis in monkey and degenerative lesions in the trapezoid nucleus in rat. Brain histopathologic evaluations were unremarkable in monkeys and rats following repeated IV artesunate exposure (7 days) at doses that were 4.3 and 10-fold clinically relevant doses based on body surface area comparisons. Additional comparative studies reported in the literature report no behavioral or histopathological changes in brainstem nuclei of rats administered artesunate IM daily for seven days whereas rats receiving an equivalent molar concentration of arteether intramuscularly displayed significant behavioral deficits with histopathological evidence of chromatolysis, pyknosis and gliosis in brainstem nuclei.
L141: ## Repeat dose Toxicology Studies
L142: The hematopoietic system was the primary target organ of toxicity in rats and dogs. A dose-dependent anemia characterized by reductions in hematocrit and red blood cells, increased mean corpuscular volume, reticulocyte count and percentage, and a moderate to marked leukocytosis was observed after 28 days of repeated IV dosing in rats at doses 0.7 to 2-fold the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons. Hematologic changes were accompanied by erythroid hyperplasia in the spleen, bone marrow and kidneys. Reductions in body weight were also observed, with deaths occurring in 1 animal each from the mid and high dose groups. Histopathologic and hematologic changes were comparable to controls at the end of recovery, indicating reversibility of effects. The NOAEL was 3 mg/kg/day, a dose equivalent to 0.2 that of the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons. Differences in IV artesunate formulation (i.e., [REDACTED] (b) (4) versus semi-synthetic) are not likely to impact toxicity given similar findings were observed in dogs administered the WRAIR formulation and the
L143: semi-synthetic formulation. Repeated intravenous administration of 20 to 50 mg/kg IV artesunate to dogs for 14 days resulted in a reduction of red blood cells, hemoglobin, hematocrit and absolute reticulocyte count with no changes in histopathology. The NOAEL for this study was 10 mg/kg (approximately 2-fold the clinical dose based on body surface area comparison). Artesunate hematologic toxicities were dose dependent in the 28-day study, with anemia occurring at lower doses (5 mg/kg) compared with the 14-day study. In addition to reductions in red blood cells and hemoglobin, animals also had increased leukocytes and fibrinogen, indicating an inflammatory response. Histopathologic changes included extramedullary hematopoiesis in liver and spleen and erythropoietic hyperplasia in the bone marrow of the sternum and femur. Reversibility of hematologic and histopathologic changes was evident following a 28-day recovery period. No NOAEL was determined given hematologic and histopathologic changes were observed in all dose groups in the 28-day dog study. The lack of a NOAEL in the 28-day study is not of appreciable concern since this product is indicated for short duration of use (3 to 7 days) and hematologic changes observed were characteristic of artemisinin antimalarial drugs and findings were reversible.
L144: ## Genetic Toxicology and Carcinogenicity
L145: In studies conducted by the Applicant, artesunate was negative in the Ames test, mammalian chromosome aberration assay (CHO), and in vivo micronucleus assay (up to 500 mg/kg, highest concentration tested). Conflicting findings reported in the published literature showed artesunate induced DNA damage in human lymphocytes and Hep2G liver cells in a Comet assay and increased micronuclei formation in human lymphocytes. The published literature also indicates that in vivo, artesunate is positive for micronucleus formation but negative for DNA damage in peripheral blood cells in mice following oral administration. No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with artesunate. Given the short duration of treatment (up to 7 days) carcinogenicity studies are not indicated.
L146: ## Reproductive Toxicology
L147: No fertility studies with IV artesunate have been conducted. No significant changes in testicular histology, sperm motility, counts or morphology were observed in rats and dogs following 28 days of repeated dosing with IV artesunate. Histopathological changes of the seminiferous tubules and altered spermatogenesis (increased percentage of abnormal sperm and/or decreased sperm motility and viability) in rats and mice have been reported in the literature following repeated oral or intraperitoneal artesunate administration (3 days to 6 weeks) at less than clinically relevant doses. Given the conflicting findings in animals and lack of any formal fertility study(ies), the risk that IV artesunate administration poses to human fertility is uncertain.
L148: Pivotal GLP embryofetal developmental toxicity studies have not been conducted with IV artesunate. A single intravenous administration of artesunate to pregnant rats on day 11 of pregnancy displayed a steep dose response curve for embryotoxicity, with a NOAEL of 0.75 mg/kg and the next highest dose tested of 1.5 mg/kg (0.1 times the recommended human dose based on body surface area comparison) inducing complete embryolethality in the absence of
L149: ## Artesunate for Injection
L150: maternal toxicity. A mass balance study conducted in pregnant rats demonstrated accumulation of artesunate in feto-placental tissues following a single administration of [^{14}\text{C}]-artesunate on day 11 of pregnancy at a dose 0.3 times the recommended human dose based on body surface area comparison. When given intramuscularly during organogenesis in rat, artesunate induced high levels of postimplantation loss, with complete resorption of litters seen at 0.75 mg/kg, 0.05-fold the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons (fetal \text{LD}_{50} = 0.61 mg/kg/day; 95% CI: 0.59-0.63). Given the extent of embryolethality observed following a single administration, embryo-fetal development studies conducted with IV AS cannot feasibly be conducted at doses that would be clinically relevant. Embryo-fetal development studies have been conducted using oral artesunate, which has a bioavailability of less than 1% in the pregnant rat. Oral administration of artesunate during organogenesis caused a significant increase in post-implantation loss in pregnant rats, rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys at significantly less than clinically relevant doses based on body surface area comparisons. Surviving rat and rabbit fetuses displayed a spectrum of cardiovascular (ventricular septal defects, retro-esophageal right subclavian artery arising from the descending aorta) and skeletal malformations (e.g., ribs, long bone, and scapulae). Rabbit fetuses also displayed an increased incidence of brain abnormalities, including severely dilated brain ventricles and pons with absent areas. Embryolethality and/or fetal malformations following oral artesunate administration occurred in the absence of maternal toxicity. In rats and monkeys, the critical window for oral artesunate exposure associated embryolethality and developmental toxicity occurs early during organogenesis, at time periods roughly equivalent to the human first trimester.
L151: Pre- and postnatal developmental toxicity studies have not been conducted with IV artesunate. The extent of embryolethality and fetal malformations observed in the oral embryo-fetal development studies indicate that pre-and postnatal development study(ies) conducted with artesunate, by any route of administration route, would not yield any clinically meaningful information.
L152: ## Excipients/Impurities
L153: Artesunate for Injection contains no excipients and there are no significant differences between the (b) (4) and semi-synthetic batches (see Quality Assessment review). The drug product is reconstituted with 0.3 M sodium phosphate buffer (b) (4)
L154: The same sodium phosphate buffer in the to be marketed formulation has been used in the PK, safety and efficacy studies conducted under IND 64769. The use of the 0.3 M phosphate buffer is considered to be qualified from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective based on prior extensive clinical use. The drug product specification includes an acceptance criteria of NMT (b) (4) % for artemisinin and (b) (4) % for (b) (4). The proposed acceptance criteria for (b) (4) % artemisinin and (b) (4) % (b) (4) are adequately qualified in the 28-day toxicity study in dog.
L155: ## Leachables:
L156: Per communication with the CMC review team, there are no leachables associated with IV AS present that warrant Pharmacology/Toxicology involvement; all leachables were below the safety concern threshold of 10^{(b)}/(4) mcg/day. (For details of product quality assessment refer to Office of Product Quality review by Dr. George Lunn in Panorama).
L157: ## Recommendation
L158: The submitted nonclinical safety information on IV artesunate lacks adequate characterization of the in vivo genotoxic potential and the potential risks on female fertility. However, given the severity of the disease and the unmet need for drugs intended to treat severe malaria the current nonclinical package supports approval for marketing in the U.S. The Applicant will be conducting the following nonclinical studies as postmarketing requirements: a GLP-in vivo micronucleus test and a female fertility study with IV artesunate.
L159: ## 5.2 Referenced INDs, NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L160: IND 64769
L161: ## 5.3 Pharmacology
L162: ## Primary Pharmacology
L163: No formal studies investigating the mechanism of action of artesunate have been performed. The endoperoxide bridge in artesunate is thought to confer its antimalarial properties through a two-step process: activation involving an iron-mediated cleavage of the endoperoxide bridge to generate an unstable organic free radical followed by alkylation of malarial proteins. Details of the primary pharmacology studies conducted with Artesunate, including efficacy studies can be found in Nonclinical Microbiology Section 9.1.
L164: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L165: No secondary pharmacology studies with artesunate have been performed.
L166: ## Safety Pharmacology
L167: ## *Cardiovascular and Respiratory Safety*
L168: Artesunate has not been evaluated in GLP studies for effects on human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) tail currents. The Applicant was not requested to conduct a hERG study using artesunate as no significant QTc prolongation of artesunate was detected in the QT assessment (see Section 10.4.9).
L169: The Applicant provided the following information on cardiovascular effects with the artesunate active metabolite, DHA:
L170: ## **In Vitro Cardiovascular Effects of Dihydroartemisinin-Piperaquine Combination Compared with Other Antimalarials.** *Antimicrobial Agents and Chemotherapy* 2012. 56(6): 3261-3271.
L171: DHA was assessed for effects on hERG blockage and trafficking in HEK-293 cells stably transfected with the hERG clone and effects on electrophysiological properties of the isolated rabbit ventricular wedge preparation (including I_{Na} and I_K ion current interference). The IC_{50} for DHA on hERG current was 7.7 \pm 0.9 mcM. DHA also produced a significant inhibition of hERG trafficking, but only at a concentration that caused &gt;75% cell death (i.e., 100 mcM). DHA was without effect on QT, T_{p-e} (interval between the end and peak of the T wave), endocardial action potential duration measured at 90% repolarization (ENDO APD_{90}) and QRS in rabbit heart ventricular wedge preparations at concentrations up to 30 mcM.
L172: *Reviewer comment: Other antimalarials (e.g., chloroquine, lumefantrine, DHA in combination with piperaquine) were tested but only results for assays in which DHA was tested alone are described. The authors of the paper indicate the IC_{50} for DHA on hERG current was approximately 30 times the reported mean therapeutic C_{max} of free DHA (0.265 mcM) in patients receiving Eurartesim, an oral piperaquine phosphate and DHA fixed-combination antimalarial treatment. The estimated C_{max} of DHA following 3 days of IV artesunate is approximately 9.8 mcM (2,789.6 ng/mL), levels that are comparable to the IC_{50} value reported for DHA on hERG current in this study but without effect on QT in rabbit heart ventricular wedge preparations. Although the GLP status is unknown, there is sufficient detail regarding methodology, mean \pm SEM values, and statistical analysis to have confidence in the reported findings.*
L173: ## **Cardiovascular Safety Pharmacology Study of Artesunate in Beagle Dogs/Study No. 1466-103**
L174: Telemetered beagle dogs aged 6-7 months (2 each per sex) were administered sterile 0.3M phosphate buffer (vehicle control) via intravenous injection on study day 1. Beginning study day 2, animals received a dose of 10, 20, and 50 mg/kg via a single slow intravenous dose (i.e., 3 min bolus) and 50 mg/kg administered via a single fast intravenous dose (i.e., 1 min bolus) with at least a one-week wash out period between each escalating dose level. Artesunate (single slow IV dose of 0, 10, 20 and 50 mg/kg or single fast IV dose of 50 mg/kg) did not have any effects on mortality, clinical observations, body weights, ECGs, QT intervals, heart rate, blood pressure, or respiratory rate.
L175: ## **A 28-day Repeated Intravenous Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of Artesunate in Beagle Dogs Followed by a 28-day Recovery Period/Study No. 352638**
L176: Artesunate was administered intravenously to dogs (3/sex/group at 0(vehicle), 5, 15, or 40 mg/kg/day for 28 days). Electrocardiograms (6 limb leads) and indirect blood pressure measurements were obtained for all animals (1 hr \pm 30 min after end of dosing) on Day 1 and Day 27 and on recovery animals on Day 56. Systolic pressure, diastolic pressure, heart rate, P-duration, PR-interval, QRS duration, and QT-interval were reported. All ECG parameters remained within normal physiologic limits during the measured study periods and there were no significant pathologic changes in the development of arrhythmias for artesunate at the dosing intervals tested.
L177: ## Artesunate for Injection
L178: ## *Central Nervous System*
L179: ## **A 28-day repeated intravenous dose toxicity and toxicokinetic study of artesunate in Sprague Dawley rats followed by a 28-day recovery period/Study No. 351852**
L180: Neurological examinations performed in rats after approximately 26 days of repeated IV dosing found no effects of artesunate on behavior (gait and posture, clonic/toxin involuntary movement, vocalization, motor activity, etc) or functional observational battery parameters.
L181: ## **Dose Range Finding Study of Injectable Artelinate and Artesunate in Rats/Study No. SR01-1**
L182: Artesunate was administered intravenously to Sprague Dawley rats daily for 7 days with 0, 9.4, 18.8, 37.5, 75, or 150 mg/kg artesunate or intramuscular injection of 25 mg/kg arteether daily for seven days followed by histopathological examination of hind brain tissues. No macroscopic or microscopic lesions observed in hindbrain from artesunate-treated animals were observed at any dose level, whereas degenerative lesions in trapezoid nucleus were noted in animals treated with arteether (see Nonclinical Appendix for full study review).
L183: ## **Toxicity of Subacute, 7-day Dosing of Candidate Intravenous Artemisinins in Rhesus Monkeys/Study Nos. AQ0008_02_AF and PN02-01.**
L184: Animals administered IV Artesunate (Guilin formulation) at doses of 4, 8, 16, 32, and 128 mg/kg/day for 7 days displayed depressed motor activity (\ge 32 mg/kg) lasting up to 6h post-dosing. Three out of 4 animals treated with a higher artesunate dose (128 mg/kg/day) died prematurely or were humanely euthanized, displaying persistent depressed motor activity, slight truncal ataxia, and altered urinalysis parameters prior to death. The surviving animal showed neuronal chromatolysis in the cranial nerve nucleus. Necropsies in the control, 4, 8, 16 and 32 mg/kg group were unremarkable for brain histological findings. In a separate group, 2 animals treated with 16 mg/kg IM arteether for 14 days (positive control) displayed motor depression and/or piloerection with foot and arm tremor and exhibited histological findings consistent with widespread neuronal necrosis and gliosis in brain stem nuclei.

L205: ## 5.5 Toxicology
L206: ## 5.5.1 General Toxicology
L207: ## A 28-day repeated intravenous dose toxicity and toxicokinetic study of artesunate in Sprague Dawley rats followed by a 28-day recovery period/Study No. 351852
L208: - Artesunate treatment caused a moderate to marked regenerative anemia, with a dose-dependent decrease in hemoglobin, RBC, and MCH with increase in MCV and reticulocytes. This was accompanied by splenomegaly and erythroid hyperplasia in spleen, liver, and bone marrow in all animals from the high dose group (30 mg/kg). While evidence of persistent anemia was present at the of recovery in the high dose animals, the noted histopathological changes were comparable to controls at the end of recovery.
- Artesunate was rapidly converted to DHA, such that plasma concentrations were &lt; LOQ (55 ng/mL) at 1 hr post dosing in the high dose group, and earlier for the low and mid dose groups. Maximum plasma concentrations for DHA peaked at 5 min post dosing for most dose groups and the AUC0-Last for DHA decreased with repeated dosing, suggesting alterations in artesunate metabolism and/or tissue distribution.
- The NOAEL for this study was 3 mg/kg, approximately 0.2-fold the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons.
L209: (b) (4)
L210: Conducting laboratory and location:
L211: GLP compliance: Yes (study was conducted in accordance with 21 CFR Part 58 and OECD Principles of Good Laboratory Practice).
L212: ## Methods
L213: Dose and frequency of dosing:	3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day for 28 days with 28-day recovery period
Route of administration:	Intravenous (slow injection over 2-3 min)
Formulation/Vehicle:	0.3 M phosphate buffer, pH 7.4 (dose formulations within \pm 15% nominal values)
Species/Strain:	Rat/CD®[Crl:CD®(SD)] (Sprague-Dawley)
Number/Sex/Group:	10M + 10F/group main study animals; 5M + 5F/group recovery animals
Age:	7-8 weeks
Satellite groups/ unique design:	TK: 6M + 6F/group (3M + 3F/control)
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:	No
L214: ## Observations and Results: changes from control
L215: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	2 animals found dead during study:
MD: 1 male on day 16
HD: 1 female day 21
Gross findings included congestion of lung lobes, soft consistency of kidney (M) and hyperemia of mandibular lymph nodes and hemorrhagic ovaries (F).
Clinical Signs	MD and HD: Pallor on the ears of animals during last week of treatment (41% of animals in MD and 100% of animals in HD)
Body Weights	MD: decrease in weight gain
HD: decrease in weight gain, mean body weight (males only), food consumption (females only)
Ophthalmoscopy	No treatment-related findings
Hematology	Dose-dependent anemia as indicated by decreased hemoglobin and RBC (up to 47% in HD), decreased MCHC (up 17% in HD), and increased MCV and reticulocytes (up to 29% and 1569%, respectively, in HD). Anemia persisted through recovery in HD males (evidenced by higher MCV and MCH).
MD and HD: Increased platelet concentration and volume, moderate to marked leukocytosis.
The hematological findings were comparable to controls at the end of the recovery period.
Clinical Chemistry	LD, MD and HD (males only): Increased albumin levels (up to 11%), Increased ALT (23%) and AST (up to 44%)
MD and HD (males and females): Increased total bilirubin (up to 145%) and increased phosphorus levels (up to 15%).
Urinalysis	No treatment-related changes
Gross Pathology	Enlarged spleen in all MD and HD females and all treated males
One HD male had hydronephrosis in the right kidney and another HD male had enlarged left seminal vesicle
Organ Weights	Increased mean spleen weight and spleen/brain weight ratios in MD and HD males and females. Increased spleen weight in HD animals compared with controls in HD animals was greater in males (448% increase organ/body weight in males vs. 270% increase organ/body weight in females).
Increased mean heart weight and heart/brain weight ratios in MD females (10 and 15%, respectively) and 13% in heart/brain weight ratios in HD males and females (13-14%).
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	HD (all animals): splenomegaly and erythroid hyperplasia in spleen, liver, and bone marrow. Erythroid proliferation indistinguishable from control at end of recovery.
Findings related to dosing procedure observed in control and treated groups included minimal to mild mixed cell perivenous infiltrates in and/or around the caudal veins associated with active or resolving thrombosis.
L216: Neurological assessment	A complete functional observation battery in Main Study male rats found no treatment-related findings.
Male reproductive assessment	No significant changes in cauda epididymal sperm motility, counts, and morphology.
L217: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose.
L218: ## A 28-day repeated intravenous dose toxicity and toxicokinetic study of artesunate in Beagle Dogs followed by a 28-day recovery period. Study No. 352638
L219: ## Key findings:
L220: - Artesunate treatment induced a dose-dependent regenerative anemia associated with erythroid hyperplasia in the spleen, liver and bone marrow. Changes in measured hematologic parameters and erythroid hyperplasia had resolved by the end of the recovery period. Since changes in hematological parameters and evidence of extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen were observed in the low dose (5 mg/kg), a NOAEL could not be determined.
- Artesunate quickly converted to DHA following IV dosing, resulting in a rapid drop of plasma concentrations after first sample collection. DHA plasma levels also dropped rapidly post dosing, as evidenced by short half-life (&lt;0.5 hr). Measured TK parameters were not markedly different between males and females on Day 1 and 28 of dosing. Plasma exposure to DHA was 20-24% that of artesunate following administration at the high dose (40 mg/kg). For DHA, a linear relationship was observed between dose and C_{max} or AUC_{0-Tlast} which was either dose proportional or close to dose proportional.
- The LOAEL of this study was 5 mg/kg, equivalent to the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons.
L221: (b) (4)
L222: Conducting laboratory and location:
L223: GLP compliance: Yes (study was conducted in accordance with 21 CFR Part 58 and OECD Principles of Good Laboratory Practice).
L224: ## Methods
L225: Dose and frequency of dosing:	5, 15, and 40 mg/kg/day for 28 days with 28-day recovery period
Route of administration:	Intravenous; slow bolus injection over 2-3 min via cephalic or saphenous vein (IV catheter).
Formulation/Vehicle:	0.3 M phosphate buffer, pH 8.1 (dose formulations within \pm 15% nominal values)
Species/Strain:	Dog/Beagle
Number/Sex/Group:	3M + 3F/group main study animals; 2M + 2F/group recovery animals
Age:	11-12 months (M) and 7-8 months (F)
Satellite groups/ unique design:	None
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:	No
L226: ## **Observations and Results: changes from control**
L227: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	None. All animals completed the 28-day treatment period and survived until scheduled termination for necropsy.
Clinical Signs	LD and MD: soft/mucoid feces in one male each dose group, one female (MD) exhibited mild vomiting
HD: mild vomiting, soft feces, swelling at injection site, pale mucous membranes and prolonged capillary refill time, which resolved during the recovery period.
Body Weights	No treatment-related changes
Ophthalmoscopy	No abnormalities observed
ECG and indirect blood pressure	None. PR interval, wave duration, QRS duration, and QT interval were within normal limits for all groups and no pathologic arrhythmias were noted at any of the time points.
No treatment-related changes in systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure, and mean heart rate during the study.
Hematology	Decreased MCV (2-5%) and increased leukocytes (100-1800%) in all treatment groups
Dose-dependent reduction in RBC, hemoglobin concentration and hematocrit (up to 35% in HD)
MD and HD: Increased platelet concentration (26-65%)
Increased fibrinogen concentration (up to 174%)
Anemia and leukocyte changes resolved by end of recovery period.
Clinical Chemistry	Increased LDH in MD (females) and HD (males and females), though still within normal range. LDH activity was reduced by end of recovery.
Urinalysis	No treatment-related changes
Gross Pathology	Pale bone marrow in femoral metaphysis in one MD male.
Injection site related findings of perivascular hemorrhage in one HD male and one LD female.
Organ Weights	LD males: 14% reduction in heart weight as a ratio to body weight.
L228: Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	
Increased amounts of extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen and erythropoietic hyperplasia with increased megakaryocytes and overall cellularity in bone marrow of sternum and femur were observed in animals from all treatment groups. A few foci of erythroid extramedullary hematopoiesis were also observed in the liver of treated animals. These findings returned to control level by the end of the recovery period.
Minimal or mild increases in hemosiderin deposits were noted in histiocytes in the splenic red pulp of LD (1 of 4), MD (3 of 4) and HD (4 of 4) recovery animals, likely indicative of increased residual iron deposition after erythrocyte degradation during treatment.
Findings related to dosing procedure observed in control and treated groups included perivascular inflammation characterized by focal fibrosis and mixed cell infiltrates at site of injection.

Male reproductive assessment	
No treatment-related changes observed following histological evaluation of cellular stages of spermatogenesis. No significant changes in cauda epididymal sperm motility, counts, and morphology.
L229: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose.
L230: *Reviewer comment: In Module 2.6.6, Applicant notes that while the low dose is not a no-effect dose, the effects are considered to be mild and non-adverse in nature and that 5 mg/kg could be considered a NOAEL. However, based on the changes in hematological parameters observed at all doses (including 5 mg/kg), a NOAEL cannot be determined for this study.*
L231: ## General toxicology; additional studies
L232: Additional repeated-dose toxicity studies previously reviewed under IND 64769 can be found below. A similar pattern of toxicity was observed in dogs following a 14-day repeated IV dosing with the WRAIR formulation (Study No. 1466-102) and the semi-synthetic artesunate formulation in the 28-day toxicity study (352638) albeit toxicity was more pronounced following the longer duration of exposure. Similarly, the findings in rat following 28-day repeated IV dosing using the semi-synthetic formulation are consistent with findings from a 7-day dose-range finding study conducted using the WRAIR formulation (Study No. SR01-01; see Nonclinical Appendix), again with extent of toxicity greater following the longer duration of exposure. Unless otherwise indicated, the additional nonclinical studies reviewed below were conducted with the WRAIR formulation.
L233: ## 14-Day Toxicity Study of Artesunate in Beagle Dogs/Study No. 1466-102
L234: IV administration of Artesunate at 10, 20, 35, or 50 mg/kg/day for 14 days resulted in changes in hematological parameters including significant reductions in RBC, HCT, and RETIC at mid- and high-doses that resolved after a 14-day recovery period. Changes in clinical observations, body weight, gross pathology and histopathology were unremarkable. The NOAEL for this study was 10 mg/kg (approximately 2.25-times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparison)
L235: ## **Toxicity of Subacute, 7-Day Dosing of Candidate Intravenous Artemisinins in Rhesus Monkey/Study No. AQ0008-02-AF and PN02-01**
L236: IV administration of Artesunate 4, 8, 16, 32 and 128 mg/kg/day for 7 days resulted in hematological changes (leukopenia, neutropenia, RBC loss, and reticulocytopenia), decreased body weight (5-10%), and microscopic hemoglobinuria that resolved after a 7-day recovery period. Depressed motor activity was observed in mid- and high-dose groups was observed that lasted up to 6h post-dosing. Three out of 4 animals treated with a higher artesunate dose (128 mg/kg/day) died prematurely or were humanely euthanized. Animals in the 128 mg/kg group displayed persistent depressed motor activity, slight truncal ataxia, altered urinalysis parameters (ketonuria, hemoglobinuria, pyuria and proteinuria), and hepatic vacuolation prior to death/at necropsy. Animals who died prematurely also had hypocellular marrow consistent with the peripheral neutropenia and reticulocytopenia. Hepatic vacuolation was observed the 3 animals that died but was not observed in the surviving animal. Moderate-to-severe congestion and hemorrhage of the stomach and intestines were also observed, and 2 of the 3 animals that died had moderate adrenocortical congestion and hemorrhage. The males in the 128 mg/kg dose group had noted spermatid degeneration. The surviving animal in the 128 mg/kg dose group showed neuronal chromatolysis in the cranial nerve nucleus. Necropsies in the control, 4, 8, 16 and 32 mg/kg were unremarkable for brain or other organ abnormalities. A separate group of monkeys were administered IM with 16 mg/kg arteether for 14 days as a positive control for the neurotoxicity assessment. Animals treated with arteether exhibited histological findings consistent with widespread neuronal necrosis and gliosis in brain stem nuclei.
L237: *Reviewer comment: Study was not conducted in compliance with GLP regulations.*

L254: ## In Vivo Clastogenicity Assay in Rodent (Micronucleus Assay)
L255: ## **Evaluation of Artesunic Acid in the Mouse Bone Marrow Micronucleus Assay/Study No. G307-08**
L256: Key Study Findings:
L257: - Artesunic Acid did not induce micronucleated erythrocytes in the bone marrow of mice that received single oral doses of up to 500 mg/kg under the conditions of this study
L258: GLP compliance: Yes
L259: Test system: Mice (male Swiss-Webster) were exposed to a single oral (gavage) dose of Artesunic Acid (125, 250 and 500 mg/kg) or 0.5% methyl cellulose (vehicle control). Positive control was urethane (300 mg/kg). Femoral bone marrow was harvested at 24 and 48 hours post dosing and evaluated for presence of micronuclei.
L260: Study is valid: Yes*
L261: **Reviewer comment: The in vivo micronucleus assay appeared to be well conducted. However, the plasma levels of artesunate and DHA were not evaluated in this study and the oral bioavailability of artesunate in mice is unknown, limiting the interpretation of the negative findings. It is of note that in pregnant rats and nonpregnant female cynomolgus monkeys the oral bioavailability of artesunate is &lt; 1-2%. In addition, no significant suppression of polychromatic erythrocytes among red blood cells in mice of either sex at any dose level were observed in this study. It is therefore unlikely that sufficient artesunate and DHA exposures were achieved at the target tissues.*
L262: ## Other Genetic Toxicity Studies
L263: The literature reports that artesunate is clastogenic in vitro and in vivo:
L264: **In vitro evaluation of the genotoxic and cytotoxic effects of artesunate, an antimalarial drug, in human lymphocytes.** *Environmental and Molecular Mutagenesis.* 2011;52(7):590-4.
L265: Key Study Findings:
L266: - Artesunate increased the frequency of micronuclei and induced DNA damage in a concentration-dependent manner in primary human blood lymphocytes under conditions of this study. Significant increases in DNA damage and MN formation were observed at the lowest concentration tested (0.5 mcg/mL).
L267: GLP compliance: unknown
L268: Test system: Primary human blood lymphocytes. Cells were exposed for 52 h (MN test) or 20h (Comet Assay) to 0.5, 1 and 2 mcg/mL artesunate or 20 ng/ml doxorubicin (positive control).
L269: Study is valid: Yes
L270: ## **Genotoxic evaluation of the antimalarial drugs artemisinin and artesunate in human HepG2 cells and effects on CASP3 and SOD1 gene expressions.** *Genetics and Molecular Research.*
L271: 2013;12(3):2517-2527.
L272: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L273: - Artesunate induced DNA damage in Hep2G cells under conditions of this study. Results of COMET assay showed Class 1 and 2 DNA damage at non-cytotoxic concentrations of artesunate (2.5 and 5 mcg/mL).
L274: GLP compliance: unknown
L275: Test system: Human hepatoma cells (HepG2). Cells were exposed for 3h to 2.5, 5, or 10 mcg/mL artesunate or 2 mcg/mL doxorubicin (positive control).
L276: Study is valid: Yes
L277: ## **Genotoxicity assessment of the antimalarial compound artesunate in somatic cells of mice.**
L278: *Food and Chemical Toxicology.* 2011;49(6):1335-9.
L279: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L280: - Oral artesunate induced mild DNA damage (Class 1) in liver cells at 5 and 50 mg/kg along with a dose-dependent increase in the number of micronucleated polychromatic erythrocytes (MNPCE) in bone marrow cells at \ge 50 mg/kg, approximately 1.6 times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons, without evidence of cytotoxicity.
L281: GLP compliance: Unknown
L282: Test system: Mice (male Swiss albino) were administered oral (gavage) artesunate at 5, 50, and 100 mg/kg or 80 mg/kg doxorubicin (positive control). Bone marrow (MN assay), peripheral blood and liver cells (comet assay) were collected 24h after treatment.
L283: Study is valid: Yes
L284: *Reviewer comment: Although GLP status of the literature reports are unknown, the publications contain mean \pm SD and/or individual animal data across dose groups for the endpoints evaluated and included concurrent positive controls and statistical analysis that allow for confidence in reported findings. There have been no in vivo micronucleus studies conducted with intravenously administrated artesunate.*
L285: ## 5.5.3 Carcinogenicity
L286: Not applicable. No carcinogenicity studies were conducted with IV artesunate.
L287: ## 5.5.4 Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L288: ## Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L289: Fertility studies have not been conducted with artesunate. No histopathologic findings of reproductive organs were observed following 28 days of repeated IV dosing of artesunate in rats and dogs, nor were there any artesunate-related changes in sperm motility, sperm count or morphology (See review of Study Nos. 351852 and 352638 in Section 5.5.1 above). Available literature evaluating the effects of artesunate on fertility and spermatogenesis in mice and rats
L290: following oral or intraperitoneal administration are summarized below and in the Nonclinical Appendix.
L291: ## **Long-Term Administration of Artesunate Induces Reproductive Toxicity in Male Rats (J Reprod Infertil. 2011; 12(4):249-260)**
L292: ## **Key Study Findings**
L293: - A significant reduction sperm motility and viability was observed in rats following a 5-day and 6-week treatment with artesunate. At 6 weeks, this was accompanied by an increase in the percentage of abnormal sperm and decreases in daily sperm production, body weight and relative weight of the testes and epididymis. Histopathological findings included duration-dependent degenerative changes in the testes and epididymis, with noted disorganization of the seminiferous tubules, degeneration of the plasmalemma and arrest of spermatogenesis. Artesunate-induced changes in sperm production and histopathology showed evidence of recovery at 6 weeks.
- No changes in serum LH, FSH or testosterone were observed in animals exposed to artesunate following a 5-day or 6-week treatment period.
- No significant changes were reported in the litter size of females cohabitated with male rats that were treated with artesunate for 6 weeks.
L294: (b) (4)
L295: Conducting laboratory and location:
L296: GLP compliance:
L297: Unknown
L298: ## **Methods**
L299: Dose and frequency of dosing:
L300: 2.9 mg/kg daily for 5 days or 6 weeks
L301: Route of administration:
L302: Oral (gavage)
L303: Formulation/Vehicle:
L304: Distilled water
L305: Species/Strain:
L306: Rat/Wistar
L307: Number/Sex/Group:
L308: 6 males/group
L309: Study design:
L310: Adult male rats were administered 2.9 mg/kg/day artesunate for 5 days or 6 weeks followed by a 6-week recovery period. Sperm characteristics, serum FSH, LH, testosterone, testicular and epididymal histology were assessed. Males treated for 6 weeks were cohabited with untreated females at a 1:2 ratio to evaluate effects of artesunate of fertility.
L311: Deviation from study protocol
L312: Unknown
L313: affecting interpretation of results:
L314: *Reviewer comment: The study is limited in that only one dose level of artesunate was tested using a route that is not clinically relevant and fertility assessment only included effects on litter size. How the findings of abnormal/decreased sperm production and adverse histopathological changes following oral artesunate treatment translate to a risk of adverse effects on male fertility in humans following IV artesunate is uncertain.*
L315: ## **Effects of Short- and Medium-Term use of Artesunate on Fertility in Male Rats** (Nigerian Journal of Health and Biomedical Sciences. 2008. Vol 7(2):18-21)
L316: **Key Study Findings:** A significant reduction in sperm density (~60%) with approximate 2-fold increase in the percentage of morphologically abnormal spermatozoa was observed in rats following a 4-week treatment with 2 mg/kg oral artesunate. Following a shorter duration of treatment, increases in percentage of abnormal spermatozoa were observed in animals from the 2 mg/kg group whereas no changes were not noted between the 1 mg/kg group and controls. The findings in the 2 mg/kg group were accompanied by histopathological evidence of damage to the seminiferous epithelium (i.e., destruction of cells in adluminal compartment and exfoliation of cells from seminiferous epithelium).

L340: ## **The antimalarial agent artesunate causes sperm DNA damage and hepatic antioxidant defense in mice** (Mutation Research 777 (2015) 1-6)

- Artesunate induced significant DNA damage as evidenced by increase in % of tail DNA, olive tail movement, and DNA damage index.

L366: ## **Embryo-Fetal Development**
L367: *Reviewer comment: The oral bioavailability of artesunate in the pregnant rat is &lt; 1% and the extent to which artesunate and its metabolites distribute to gestational tissues following oral administration is unknown. Given the extent of embryo lethality observed following a single IV administration in the pregnant rat, embryo-fetal development studies conducted with IV AS cannot feasibly be conducted at doses that would be clinically relevant. It is likely that the findings described below following oral exposure to artesunate during organogenesis*
L368: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L369: *underestimate the risk of IV artesunate on embryofetal toxicity. Given that there are noted species-specific differences in the rate of conversion to DHA by the different artemisinin derivatives (i.e., arteether, artemether, and artesunate) and potential differences in the bioavailability of DHA and its distribution to gestational tissues following oral versus intravenous exposure, the findings of embryofetal development studies conducted using oral artemisinin derivatives in evaluating the risk for IV artesunate use in human pregnancy is of uncertain value.*
L370: ## **SB-714702 (Artesunate): Single Dose Intravenous Embryo-Fetal Development Study in Rats/CD2006/01142/00 (Study No. I06318)**
L371: ## **Key Study Findings**
L372: - Significant post-implantation loss occurred in all animals administered a single intravenous dose of either 1.5 or 3 mg/kg artesunate on Day 11 post coitum (pc) (0.1 to 0.2-fold clinical dose based on body surface area comparison). A reduction in mean maternal body weight gain of up to 62% was also observed, likely due to total litter loss. No effects on embryo-fetal survival were noted in animals receiving a single dose of 0.75 mg/kg on Day 11 pc.

L397: ## **Artesunate: Oral (Gavage) Study of Embryo-Foetal Development in the Rabbit/Study No. CD2005/00560/00 ((b) (4) Report Number 1552/94-D6154)**
L398: ## **Key Study Findings**
L399: - No NOAEL could be established given that artesunate increased the incidence of fetal cardiovascular, rib, and brain malformations at all doses tested, doses which were to 0.7 to 1.6-fold the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons.
- At the highest dose tested (12 mg/kg), artesunate was embryofetal toxic, inducing abortion, total embryo-fetal loss, and increased incidences of intrauterine deaths and lower fetal weight.
- No maternal toxicity was observed at any of the doses tested.
- Cmax values for DHA increased with increasing administered dose with higher Cmax and AUC(0-4h) DHA values on Day 7 than on Day 19 (see Section 5.4 ADME/PK above).

L422: ## **Artesunate: Oral (Gavage) Study of Embryo-Foetal Development in the Rat/Study No. CD2005/00559/00 ( (b) (4) Report Number 1552/93-D6154)**
L423: ## **Key Study Findings**
L424: - No NOAEL could be established given that artesunate was embryotoxic with total embryo-fetal loss, increased incidence of intrauterine death and decreased fetal weights observed at all doses tested (0.4 to 1.1-times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons).
- Increased incidence in cardiovascular and skeletal malformations were observed in fetuses from the low and mid-dose groups. Long bone and/or scapular malformations and ventricular septal defects were not observed in fetuses from the high dose group, likely due to the small number of surviving fetuses (mean of 1.4 per female).
- No maternal toxicity was observed at any of the doses tested.
- C_{max} values for Artesunate and C_{max} and AUC_{(0-4h)} values for DHA increased with increasing administered dose and were higher on Day 6 than on Day 17; T_{max} for dihydroartemisinin occurred later on Day 17 of gestation than on Day 6 (see Section 5.4 ADME/PK above).

L466: ## **A Study for the Effect of SB-714702 (Artesunate) on Embryo-Foetal Development in Cynomolgus Monkeys by Oral Administration/CD2006/00794/00 (Study No. G04361)**
L467: ## **Key Study Findings**
L468: - A NOAEL of 4 mg/kg/day was determined based on lack of any treatment-related effects in dams and fetuses in this dose group. Treatment-related decreases in reticulocyte count, embryonic deaths and abortions were observed between gestation days 30 and 40 in dams administered 12 and 30 mg/kg/day oral artesunate, approximately 1.6 to 4-times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons.
- A significant decrease in the length of ossification of the ulna was observed in the 12 mg/kg group. Dosing in the 30 mg/kg group was discontinued due to high incidence of abortion/embryonic death prior to Day 40 of gestation.
- Systemic exposure to artesunate and DHA increased in an approximately dose-proportional manner between 4 and 12 mg/kg/day. Lack of data precluded any TK assessments from the 30 mg/kg/day dose.
L469: Conducting laboratory and location:
L470: GLP compliance:
L471: Yes
