L487: 80, and 90 of gestation. Cesarean sectioning was performed on Day 100 of gestation and fetuses were removed and examined.
L488: Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:
L489: No
L490: ## Observations and Results
L491: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	None
Clinical Signs	None
Body Weights	None; significant decrease in food consumption was observed in HD on Days 23 and 27.
Hematologic findings	
A decrease in reticulocyte count (0.0-0.2% of blood cells) was observed in 1/11 dams in MD group and 4/9 dams in HD group at Day 31 of gestation and/or day of embryonic death, abortion or cesarean sectioning compared with pre-treatment values (reticulocytes 0.8-2.1% of blood cells).
A decrease in erythrocyte count, leukocyte count, and hematocrit value and increase in platelet count was noted in the MD and HD on Day 31 of gestation and/or day of embryonic death, abortion or cesarean sectioning compared with before treatment.
No treatment-related effects were observed in fetal hematological parameters (nucleated and non-nucleated RBC or leukocytes) on Day 100 of gestation.

Necropsy findings
Cesarean Section Data	
MD: Embryonic death was observed in 7 of 11 dams between Day 30 and 45 of gestation.
HD: abortion/embryonic death occurred in 4 dams on Day 37 or 40 of gestation. Administration to the remaining 5 dams was discontinued on Day 39, 37, 36, 32, or 26 of gestation. Of these, embryonic death occurred in 1 dam on Day 39 of gestation and 1 dam aborted on Day 37 gestation.

Necropsy findings
Offspring	
MD: Significant decrease in length of ossification of the ulna
No visceral abnormalities or variations were observed in any fetus.
L492: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose
L493: A histopathological evaluation of surviving embryos from the 30 mg/kg treatment group indicate an overall reduction in erythroblasts, with evidence of cardiovascular defects in 2 embryos. Both artesunate and DHA were shown to be cytotoxic in vitro to maternal and fetal blood cells isolated from pregnant Cynomolgus monkeys. (see review of Study Nos. CD2006/01492/00 and CD2006/00795/00 in Nonclinical Appendix).
L494: The critical window for embryolethality and developmental malformations associated with oral artesunate exposure during pregnancy was subsequently evaluated in rats and cynomolgus monkeys in study Nos. CD2004/00133/00, CD2004/00353/00 and CD2007/00245/00. In rats,
L495: the critical window for oral artesunate exposure associated embryolethality and developmental toxicity was determined to be between days 9 and 14 of pregnancy. The critical window for artesunate-induced embryolethality in cynomolgus monkeys following oral administration is estimated to fall between days 20 and 27 of pregnancy (see Nonclinical Appendix for review).
L496: A separate study (CD2004/00606/01) conducted in rats comparing the embryo-fetal toxicity following a single oral administration of artesunate, its active metabolite DHA, or other artemisinin-derived compounds (i.e., artemether and arteether) to rats on day 10 of pregnancy found similar treatment-related effects on embryolethality and cardiovascular and skeletal malformations, suggesting the DHA metabolite plays a significant role in mediating the developmental toxicity associated with artesunate exposure. This is further supported by in vitro studies demonstrating concentration-dependent cytotoxic effects of DHA on fetal blood cells isolated from pregnant monkeys during gestation (see Nonclinical Appendix for review).
L497: *Reviewer comment: Similar to the gestation-dependent sensitivity of cynomolgus fetal blood to artesunate and DHA (reported in Study No. 2006/00795/00), a pattern of gestation-dependent sensitivity to artesunate has been reported in the published literature for rat embryos, both in vitro and in vivo. Laffan et al. (2005)3 report that rat embryonic blood cells are more sensitive that adult erythrocytes to artesunate-induced cytotoxicity, with an apparent enhanced sensitivity of blood cells isolated from GD 12 embryos compared with blood cells isolated from GD 14 embryos. White et al. (2006)4 report that following a single oral dose of 17 mg/kg artesunate to rats on GD 10, GD 10.5 or GD 11, depletion of embryonic erythroblasts preceded malformations (i.e., thin atrial and ventricular walls and thin/underdeveloped trabeculae) and embryo death. In addition, Longo et al. (2006)5 found that addition of DHA to rat whole embryo cultures cultured from GD 9.5 to GD 11.5 resulted in yolk sac anemia and disorganization of yolk sac vessels. Use of oral artesunate and other oral artemisinin class drugs in pregnant women has not identified a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. The apparent discrepancy between the nonclinical and clinical data regarding the safety of oral artemisinin use during pregnancy has been hypothesized to be due, in part, to the period of time during which the embryonic erythroblast cells are sensitive to artesunate-induced cytotoxicity. As maturation of embryonic RBC occurs, the embryo becomes less sensitive to artesunate-induced developmental toxicity. In humans, this sensitive period during which primitive erythroblasts are formed and remain circulating in the embryo is longer than that in rodents, which could theoretically allow time for artesunate-damaged cells to be replaced thereby curtailing any teratogenic effects. However, there is insufficient information regarding the risk associated with the use of intravenous artesunate during pregnancy. As noted above, a single exposure to intravenous artesunate during pregnancy in rats at 0.1-times*
L498: 3 Laffan, S.B., et al. (2005) "In vitro sensitivity of rat embryonic blood cells to the anti-malarial drug artesunate" *Birth Defects Res (Part A)* **73**:13
L499: 4 White, T., et al. (2006) "Artesunate-induced depletion of embryonic erythroblasts precedes embryolethality and teratogenicity in vivo" *Birth Defects Res (Part B)* **77**:413-429
L500: 5 Longo M., et al. (2006) "Effects of the antimalarial drug dihydroartemisinin (DHA) on rat embryos in vitro" *Reprod Toxicol* **21**(1): 83-93
L501: *the clinical dose based on body surface area comparison resulted in complete embryolethality. Given that the bioavailability of oral artesunate is expected to be significantly lower than intravenous artesunate, the clinical relevance of studies involving oral exposure to artesunate and other oral artemisinin class drugs is uncertain.*
L502: ## The Assessment of Embryonic Toxicity of Artesunate with Multiple Injective Doses in Rats/MIDRP (AQ0004_04_WR)
L503: ## Key Study Findings
L504: - Intramuscular administration of artesunate during organogenesis induced high levels of postimplantation loss, with complete resorption of litters seen at 0.75 mg/kg, 0.05-times the clinical dose based on body surface area comparisons. The fetal LD50 of AS was 0.61 mg/kg/day (95% CI: 0.59-0.63).
- The critical window for AS-induced embryolethality following IM administration occurs between Days 11-15, though significant post-implantation loss is also observed when administered late in gestation, between Days 15 and 20.
L505: Conducting laboratory and location:
L506: GLP compliance: No

L509: Route of administration: Intramuscular
L510: Formulation/Vehicle: 0.3M PBS, pH 8.1
L511: Species/Strain: Rat/Sprague-Dawley
L512: Number/Sex/Group: 3-12 mated females/group
L513: Study design: *Dose-response portion:* Groups of 3 mated female rats were administered artesunate at 0 (vehicle), 0.5, 0.55, 0.6, 0.65, 0.75, 1.0, 1.25, 1.5, 4.5, or 13.5 mg/kg/day from Day 6 to Day 18 of gestation, inclusive.
L514: *Early embryonic development study:* Groups of 5 mated female rats were administered artesunate at 0, 2, 4, or 6 mg/kg/day from Days 6 to 8 of gestation.
L515: *Critical window study:* Groups of 5 mated female rats were administered artesunate at 6 mg/kg/day from Days 6 to 10, Days 11-15, or Days 16-20 of gestation.
L516: Dams were killed on Day 20 of gestation and uterine contents examined.
L517: Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results: No

Reductions in body weight increases observed in dams administered \ge 0.75 mg/kg IM artesunate are likely reflective of litter loss.

L528: ## Prenatal and Postnatal Development
L529: No pre- and post-natal development studies have been conducted with artesunate, nor are there any publications that evaluate the pre- and post-natal developmental toxicity of artemisinins.
L530: *Reviewer comment: The Applicant cited a WHO 2003 publication "Assessment of the safety of artemisinin compounds in pregnancy" which indicates that GLP studies on pre-and postnatal dosing and subsequent development of pups were conducted according to requirements of major regulatory agencies found no effect on postnatal development and maturation of offspring of dosed dams for any artemisinin derivatives, including artesunate. However, the WHO publication does not provide any information regarding study design (species, route of administration, dose) or study findings. Given the available data on the embryofetal toxicity and fetal malformations associated with artesunate in rat, rabbit and monkey, it is unlikely that the doses used in the negative pre- and postnatal study(ies) referenced in the WHO document achieved clinically relevant exposures.*
L531: ## 5.5.5 Other Toxicology Studies
L532: **Quantitative tissue distribution of drug-related material using whole body autoradiography (WBA) following a single intravenous dose of [14C] artesunate (5 mg/kg) to 11-day pregnant Sprague Dawley rats/**(b) (4)**-132N-0501**
L533: ## Key findings:
L534: - The highest concentrations of artesunate in tissues following intravenous administration in rat were observed at 0.5 hr post dosing, with ~41% of radioactivity amassed in small intestine and its contents and ~6% in bile of the bile duct. At 8 hr post dosing, high levels of radioactivity were present in small intestine (28.2%) and bladder (25.8%).
L535: - Approximately 8% of total radioactivity was found in reproductive tissues (e.g., uterus and placenta), suggesting that ^{14}\text{C}-AS and related drug material can readily cross the placenta and distribute to the developing conceptus. A greater amount of radiolabeled AS distributed in the ovary, placenta and uterus compared with blood (\sim2 to 4-fold). The total \text{AUC}_{0-192\text{h}} of the radioactivity was 22879 mcg equivalents-h/g in all measured tissues, with 6.5% (1480 mcg equivalents-h/g) of total radioactivity present in feto-reproductive tissues. The half-life of radioactivity in blood was also shorter compared with feto-reproductive tissues, 97.7 hr in blood compared with 159.9, 200.5, and 152.6 hr in ovary, placenta, and uterus, respectively.
L536: Conducting laboratory and location: (b) (4)
L537: GLP compliance: No

L548: ## **Pharmacokinetics and bioequivalence of new artesunate intravenous formulation compared to Chinese drug product in rats and beagle dogs/Study No. ET-05003**
L549: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L550: - In rats, pharmacokinetic analysis indicated that the 90% confidence interval (CI) of the difference between the Guilin (vehicle, 5% NaHCO3) and WRAIR (b) (4) formulations following daily intravenous dosing for 3 days were contained within 80 to 125% of the geometric mean of Cmax and AUC. Bioequivalence was fulfilled with AS (82.3-117.7% for all parameters) and partially satisfied for DHA in rats following single or multiple injections.
- In dogs, pharmacokinetic analysis also indicated that the 90% CI of the difference between the Guilin and WRAIR formulations following a single intravenous dose were contained within 80 to 125% of the geometric mean of Cmax and AUC. Following multiple injections, bioequivalence was fulfilled with regards to AUC but not Cmax.

L573: ## **Evaluation of a Test Article to Induce Hemolysis in Human, Rat, Dog and Monkey Blood/Study No. 0725XW16.001**
L574: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L575: - Hemolysis of dog blood was observed following incubation with 0.3M phosphate buffer, pH 8.1 (OD_{450} of 1.481 compared with an OD_{450} of 0.028 in saline). Human, rat and monkey blood were found to be compatible with 0.3M phosphate buffer, pH 8.1.
- Artesunate caused hemolysis in human (68.74%), rat (54.38%), and monkey (58.87%) blood at 10 mg/mL, with no hemolysis observed at 30 or 60 mcM artesunate. In dog blood, 100% hemolysis was observed following exposure to 30 mcM and 60 mcM and ~44% hemolysis was observed at 10 mg/mL artesunate.
L576: GLP compliance: Yes
L577: Test system: Artesunate (30 mcM, 60 mcM and 10 mg/ml), vehicle control (0.3M phosphate buffer, pH 8.1), saline, and water were mixed with whole blood from human, rat, dog and monkey and incubated for 4 hr. Red cells were removed from samples via centrifugation, and Hb in supernatant measured spectrophotometrically (OD at ^{(b)}(4) nm).
L578: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L579: ## 6.1 Executive Summary
L580: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology reviewed the relevant information contained in NDA 213036. The clinical pharmacology information submitted in the NDA supports the approval of Artesunate for Injection (IV AS) for the initial treatment of severe (b) (4) malaria in adult and pediatric patients. The dosing regimen for IV AS is proposed as 2.4 mg/kg (b) (4) administered (b) (4) at time 0, then at 12, 24 (b) (4) hours (b) (4).
L581: The PK of AS and DHA following IV administration of artesunate was investigated in healthy subjects (Studies 1128 and 1142) and in patients with uncomplicated malaria (Study 1168). The PK of AS and DHA following administration of IV AS was evaluated in patients with severe malaria in 3 published studies. The in vivo drug-drug interactions (DDI) between AS and DHA and various concomitant medications was evaluated in 9 published studies. These studies were deemed to be acceptable for review. See Sections 6.2.2 and Appendix 18.3 below for more details of the PK and DDIs.
L582: The Applicant is relying on the efficacy and safety information from two published pivotal randomized clinical efficacy trials of intravenous artesunate compared with intravenous quinine in severe malaria: SEAQUAMAT, which evaluated 730 patients aged 2 to 87 years who received IV artesunate compared with 731 patients who received IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2005); and AQUAMAT, which studied 2715 pediatric patients administered IV artesunate compared with 2713 who received IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2010). A Phase 2 study, EDCTP/MMV07-01, which compared a 5-dose regimen of IV artesunate with a 3-dose regimen of IV artesunate with the same total dose, provided *P. falciparum* parasite clearance information at 24 hours in children with severe malaria. See sections 7, 8, and 9 for the Clinical and Statistical reviews of the safety and efficacy of IV AS for the initial treatment of severe malaria.
L583: The Applicant proposed the same dose of 2.4 mg/kg IV artesunate for both adults and pediatric patients based on the results of the SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT trials. It is to be noted that the WHO recommends a higher dose of 3 mg/kg for pediatric patients &lt;20 kg body weight. During the clinical pharmacology review, the Pharmacometrics review team evaluated the appropriateness of the WHO recommended dose of 3 mg/kg for patients &lt;20 kg body weight by reviewing a publication by Zaloumis, et al7 that described the population PK of IV artesunate in adults and pediatric patients with severe malaria. Additionally, adult and pediatric patient data were obtained from 6 different studies with diverse study designs and dosing scheme. The Pharmacometrics team reviewed the data and performed simulations to evaluate the appropriateness of the 2.4 mg/kg dosing regimen proposed by the Applicant in pediatric patients. The PK data were pooled from the different studies for
L584: 7 Zaloumis, CPT Pharmacometrics Syst. Pharmacol. (2014) 3, e145
L585: pharmacokinetic analysis. Patients received either IM, intra-rectal (IR), or IV artesunate depending on study specific designs and dosing schemes. PK data following IV AS administration were used for population PK modeling; this included IV AS data collected in cross-over trials.
L586: Based on the simulations, it was concluded that the 2.4 mg/kg dosing regimen is appropriate for all patients with severe malaria regardless of body weight or age. This dosing regimen is predicted to result in comparable or greater steady-state DHA exposure (AUC_{0-12}) in neonates and infants to that observed in older children and adults. For example, the average predicted steady-state AUC_{0-12} was up to 90% greater in pediatric patients with severe malaria weighing less than 6 kg (neonates and infants less than 3 months old) compared to older children and adults weighing 21 kg to 25 kg administered 2.4 mg/kg IV AS. Since no exposure-response relationship for safety was able to be evaluated, the safety risk of this higher DHA AUC_{0-12} cannot be determined. However, given the severity of severe malaria in pediatric patients, the clinical pharmacology review team believes that the benefits of Artesunate for Injection outweigh this unknown risk.
L587: ## 6.2 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L588: ## 6.2.1 Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L589: **Table 6-1. Summary of the Clinical Pharmacokinetics of IV Artesunate**
L590: Pharmacokinetics (PK)	Following repeat administration of 2.4 mg/kg IV Artesunate 4 times over 3 days at 0, 12, 24, and 48 hrs, the Cmax and AUC of AS in patients with severe malaria are approximately 3.3 µg/mL and 0.7 µg-h/mL, respectively. The Cmax and AUC of DHA are approximately 3.1 µg/mL and 3.5 µg-h/mL respectively.
Distribution	Human plasma protein binding of AS and DHA is approximately 73% and 93%, respectively. The volume of distribution of AS and DHA is approximately 70 L and 60 L, respectively.
Elimination	Mean terminal half-life of AS and DHA is approximately 0.2 hours and 1.2 hours, respectively. The clearance of AS and DHA is approximately 180 L/h and 32.3 L/h, respectively.
Metabolism
Artesunate is rapidly hydrolyzed to DHA by plasma esterases. DHA is not metabolized by CYP 450 oxidation or by cytosolic sulfotransferases. A glucuronide metabolite, \alpha-DHA-G, is a major metabolite of DHA in humans and its formation is catalyzed by UGT1A9 and UGT2B7
Excretion
A radiolabeled mass balance study was not conducted and so the disposition and extent of elimination and/or excretion pathways of AS and DHA are unknown.
L591: ## 6.2.2 General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L592: ## General Dosing
L593: The Applicant's proposed dosing regimen for severe malaria is supported by efficacy and safety data from the published clinical trials submitted in the application. The proposed dosing regimen for IV AS is 2.4 mg/kg administered (b) (4) at time 0, then at 12, 24 (b) (4) hours (b) (4) (b) (4)
L594: ## Therapeutic Individualization
L595: ## Intrinsic Factors
L596: No dedicated PK studies were conducted to evaluate the effect of age and gender on the PK of AS and DHA. Both children and women were included in the pivotal clinical trials.
L597: ## Extrinsic factors
L598: No clinically significant drug interactions were reported with co-administration of oral artesunate with atovaquone/proguanil, mefloquine, amodiaquine and sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine. Therefore, drug interactions between IV AS and these drugs are not expected. There are two scenarios described below, where coadministration of IV AS should be avoided with nevirapine, ritonavir and other strong UGT inducers.
L599: ## Drug Interaction Studies from the Literature
L600: ## Effect of Other Drugs on IV Artesunate:
L601: ## Nevirapine:
L602: Co-administration of **oral** Artesunate with nevirapine8 resulted in a decrease in Cmax and AUC of DHA by 59% and 68%, respectively. However, it is to be noted that the authors evaluated oral artesunate in this study, which is not approved in the US. This reduction in systemic exposure is may result in the loss of efficacy of IV Artesunate. Based on this reduction of systemic exposure observed in this study, IV Artesunate should not to be given with nevirapine. Thus, co-administration of IV Artesunate with nevirapine should be avoided.
L603: ## Ritonavir
L604: Co-administration of **oral** Artesunate with ritonavir9 resulted in a 27% and 38% decrease in Cmax and AUC, respectively, of DHA. However, it is to be noted that the authors evaluated oral artesunate in this study, which is not approved in the US. This effect of reduced DHA exposure following coadministration of oral artesunate with ritonavir maybe due to induction effect of ritonavir on DHA metabolism via CYP3A, 1A2, 2C9, 2C19 and 2B6. It is expected that co-administration of ritonavir with IV artesunate may also decrease the Cmax and AUC of DHA. Thus, co-administration of IV Artesunate with ritonavir should be avoided.
L605: ## Outstanding Issues
L606: A multiple-dose safety, tolerability, PK study in pediatric patients with severe malaria from 0 to 12 months of age receiving 2.4 mg/kg IV AS is recommended by the clinical pharmacology review team to confirm the adequacy of the PK extrapolation approach in pediatric patients. Given the scarcity of PK data from infants younger than 6 months, the Applicant will be asked to place additional focus on this age group so that the majority of pediatric patients enrolled in the study will be 6 months of age and younger. For the PK assessment, a random sparse sampling population approach to DHA PK can be conducted with post-infusion PK sample(s) collected after any dose during treatment with 2.4 mg/kg of Artesunate for Injection.

L608: 9 Morris, Am. J. Trop. Med. Hyg., 86(3), 2012, pp. 489–495
L609: ## 6.3 Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L610: ## 6.3.1 General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L611: ## Summary of Pharmacologic Activity and Clinical Pharmacology
L612: ## **Mechanism of action:**
L613: Artemisinins are endoperoxides derived from artemisinin, for which activation of the endoperoxide bridge is essential for antimalarial activity. Semisynthetic lactol derivatives such as DHA improve bioavailability and efficacy.
L614: ## **Active Moiety:**
L615: DHA is the active in vivo metabolite of artesunate.
L616: ## **QT prolongation:**
L617: The QT-IRT group provided an assessment of the QTc prolongation effect of IV AS. Based on the group's conclusion, no significant QTc prolongation effect of artesunate or DHA was detected.
L618: The effect of artesunate on the QT interval was evaluated in Studies 1128 and 1142. Study 1128 was randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study assessing the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics of artesunate following intravenous infusion (dose levels - 0.5, 1, 2, 4, and 8.0 mg/kg; 10 mg/mL) in healthy subjects (n=40). Study 1142 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple ascending dose (3- days) study assessing the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of artesunate following intravenous infusion (dose levels - 2, 4, and 8 mg/kg; 10 mg/mL) in healthy subjects (n=26/40). In both studies, the highest dose evaluated was 8 mg/kg (administered as intravenous infusion over 2 min), which is the maximum studied dose and covers the expected therapeutic exposures (C_{2\text{min}}: 17650 ng/mL; end-of-infusion). Since the first ECG was collected at 5 min and the infusion duration was 2 min, the peak concentrations of artesunate and DHA were predicted based on the linear pharmacokinetics.
L619: The data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as the primary analysis, which did not suggest that artesunate or DHA are associated with a significant QTc prolonging effect and are presented in Table 6-2.
L620: **Table 6-2: The Point Estimates and the 90% CIs (FDA Analysis)**

L636: Following administration of single IV doses of artesunate to healthy subjects, across the range of 1.0 mg/kg - 8.0 mg/kg, both AS and DHA AUC and Cmax estimates showed approximate dose proportional increases. After multiple doses of 2, 4 and 8 mg/kg IV injection of AS, the Cmax and AUC values of AS and DHA increased proportionally to the AS dose. No plasma accumulation of AS and DHA was observed upon repeat administration of IV Artesunate. This is to be expected given the short half-lives of AS and DHA.
L637: ## 6.3.2 Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L638: ## Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L639: The pivotal clinical safety and efficacy studies conducted in support of this application did not include PK and PD assessments and thus, exposure-response analyses were not conducted.
L640: ## Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?
L641: From a clinical pharmacology perspective, the proposed IV Artesunate dosing regimen of 2.4 mg/kg (b)(4) is acceptable for adult and pediatric patients with severe malaria. The assessment of the IV Artesunate dosing regimen is based on two published clinical studies mentioned previously. No dose-response data exist for IV Artesunate and the Applicant did not conduct systematic dose-finding studies. Based on a published report10 in the literature, the systemic PK exposure (Cmax and AUC) of AS and DHA following administration of IV Artesunate is similar in pregnant women and post-partum female patients.
L642: 10 Mcgready R, Phyo AP, Rijken MJ, et al. Artesunate/dihydroartemisinin pharmacokinetics in acute falciparum malaria in pregnancy: absorption, bioavailability, disposition and disease effects. Br J Clin Pharmacol. 2012;73(3):467-77.
L643: ## **Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?**
L644: From a clinical pharmacology perspective, no alternative dosing regimens for IV AS are deemed necessary based on intrinsic patient factors. No clinically significant differences in the effectiveness of IV AS were observed based age, on sex and race.
L645: ## *Pediatric Patients*
L646: The Applicant proposed the same dose of 2.4 mg/kg IV Artesunate for both adults and pediatric patients based on the results of the SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT trials. It is to be noted that the WHO recommends a higher dose of 3 mg/kg for pediatric patients &lt;20 kg body weight. During the clinical pharmacology review, the Pharmacometrics review team evaluated the appropriateness of the WHO recommended dose of 3 mg/kg for patients &lt;20 kg body weight by reviewing a publication by Zaloumis, et al11 that described the population PK of IV artesunate in adults and pediatric patients with severe malaria. Additionally, patient data (adult and pediatric) were obtained from 6 different studies with diverse study designs and dosing scheme. The Pharmacometrics team reviewed the data and performed simulations to evaluate the appropriateness of the 2.4 mg/kg dosing regimen proposed by the Applicant in pediatric patients. The PK data were pooled from the different studies for pharmacokinetic analysis. Patients received either IM, intra-rectal (IR), or IV artesunate depending on study specific designs and dosing schemes. PK data following IV AS were used for population PK modeling; this included IV AS data collected in second periods for cross-over trials.
L647: Based on the simulations (Figure 6-1), it was concluded that the 2.4 mg/kg dosing regimen is appropriate for all patients with severe malaria regardless of body weight or age. This dosing regimen is predicted to result in comparable or greater DHA exposure (AUC_{0-12}) in neonates and infants to that observed in older children and adults. For example, the average predicted AUC_{0-12} was up to 90% greater in pediatric patients with severe malaria weighing less than 6 kg (neonates and infants less than 3 months old) compared to children weighing 21 kg to 25 kg administered 2.4 mg/kg IV AS. The safety risk of this higher DHA AUC_{0-12} cannot be determined. However, given the severity of the disease, the clinical pharmacology review team believes that the benefits outweigh this unknown risk.
L648: **Figure 6-1: Simulated exposure (AUC_{0-12}) of DHA in patients with severe malaria after the first dose of IV AS (2.4 mg/kg) across a range of weights or ages (inset) using a PK model accounting for size and organ maturation.**

Clearance was estimated using a combination of allometric weight scaling with a sigmoid function to account for organ maturation.

L652: A multiple-dose pediatric DHA PK and safety post-marketing commitment study is recommended by the clinical pharmacology review team to confirm the adequacy of the PK extrapolation approach in pediatric patients with severe malaria 1 month of age and older. For the PK assessment, a random sparse sampling population PK approach can be conducted with post-infusion PK sample(s) collected after any dose during treatment with 2.4 mg/kg IV AS. in pediatric patients &lt;20 kg body weight. See section 18.4 for additional details of the Pharmacometric review.
L653: ## *Renal or Hepatic Impairment*
L654: The effect of renal or hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of IV Artesunate was not studied by the Applicant. Given that patients with severe malaria present with some degree of hepatic and renal impairment and given that the adverse event profiles in patients with renal and hepatic impairment were similar to the adverse event profile in patients with normal hepatic and renal function, no dosage adjustments are deemed necessary in patients with any degree of hepatic and renal impairment
L655: ## **Are there clinically relevant drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?**
L656: Yes, there are PK drug-drug interactions (DDIs) that potentially pose a clinically significant risk (efficacy loss) for IV AS.
L657: ## Clinical DDI Studies
L658: The Applicant did not conduct any dedicated DDI studies to evaluate whether AS and DHA are substrates/inhibitors/inducers of the CYP enzymes. However, the Applicant provided several published papers that evaluated interactions between oral artesunate and other medications such as mefloquine, malarone, amodiaquine, nevirapine, pyronaridine/ritonavir and sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine. Oral artesunate is not approved in the US and thus, these DDI studies should be interpreted with caution. Based on review of the literature papers, it was concluded that no significant interactions have been observed for most of the above-mentioned drugs. However, coadministration of IV AS with nevirapine and ritonavir should be avoided. A description of the results of these two published interaction studies between oral artesunate and nevirapine and ritonavir is given below:
L659: ## Nevirapine
L660: Co-administration of 200 mg oral artesunate with nevirapine12 administered at a dose of 200 mg resulted in a decrease in C_{max} and AUC of DHA by 59% and 68%, respectively. However, it is to be noted that the authors evaluated oral artesunate in this study, which is not approved in the US. This reduction in systemic exposure is likely to result in the loss of efficacy of IV Artesunate. Based on this reduction of systemic exposure observed in this study, IV Artesunate should not be given with NVP. Thus, co-administration of IV Artesunate with nevirapine and other CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided.
L661: ## Ritonavir
L662: Co-administration of 60 mg oral Artesunate with ritonavir13 administered at a dose of 100 mg twice daily for 17 days resulted in a 27% and 38% decrease in C_{max} and AUC of DHA. However, it is to be noted that the authors evaluated oral artesunate in this study, which is not approved in the US. This effect of reduced DHA exposure oral artesunate following coadministration with ritonavir could be due to induction effect of ritonavir on DHA metabolism via CYP3A, 1A2, 2C9, 2C19 and 2B6. It is expected that co-administration of ritonavir with IV AS will also decrease the C_{max} and AUC of DHA. Thus, co-administration of IV AS with ritonavir should be avoided.
L663: ## Evaluation of Potential DDIs from In Vitro Studies
L664: In vitro AS is not an inhibitor of the major drug metabolizing CYP enzymes CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, or 3A4/5 based on maximal inhibition no greater than 4% at AS concentrations exceeding those observed in healthy volunteers by approximately 2-fold (see Appendix 21.4).
L665: 12 Fehintola, AIDS Research and Treatment Volume 2012, Article ID 703604,
L666: 13 Morris, Am.J. Trop. Med. Hyg., 86(3), 2012, pp. 489-495
L667: In vitro DHA is deemed to be a weak inhibitor of CYP 1A2 and 2C19 based on inhibition assays14. Additionally, a static mechanistic model (AUCR) using DHA's maximum concentration after IV administration of the proposed 2.4 mg/kg dose in patients with severe malaria and worst-case unbound fraction (39%) predicts an in vivo potential interaction with the values slightly past the threshold value (i.e., R&gt;1.02). However, when the typical unbound fraction (10%) is considered, no potential in vivo interaction is predicted (R&lt;1.25 threshold value). Taken together, the data suggest a low in vivo potential interaction for DHA.
L668: 14 Ericsson, Xenobiotica, 2014; 44(7): 615–626

L679: Abbreviations: AS, artesunate; BID, twice daily; DB, double-blind; LTE, long-term extension study; MC, multi-center; N, number of subjects; OL, open-label; PC, placebo-controlled; PG, parallel group; R, randomized.

L681: ## 7.2 Review Strategy
L682: In this NDA, evidence of clinical efficacy is based primarily on a single randomized, open-label clinical efficacy trial in patients 2 to 87 years of age with severe malaria, (South East Asian Quinine Artesunate Malaria Trial [SEAQUAMAT]), which evaluated 730 patients randomized to receive IV artesunate compared with 731 patients randomized to IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2005).
L683: Supportive evidence for efficacy was obtained from a randomized open-label trial (African Quinine Artesunate Malaria Trial [AQUAMAT], which studied 2715 pediatric patients administered IV artesunate compared with 2713 who received IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2010) and a randomized dose-finding study in Africa (EDCTP-MMV07-01) which evaluated 194 pediatric patients with severe malaria receiving one of two dose regimens of IV artesunate. (Kremsner et al. 2012)
L684: For SEAQUAMAT, this application included a protocol dated June 30, 2003, an annotated and sample case report form (CRF), CSR (a re-analysis of dataset from SEAQUAMAT by U.S. Army and the (b) (4) Corporation in 2010), analysis datasets, and publication (Dondorp et al. 2005). According to the SEAQUAMAT Analysis Data Reviewer's Guide, the analysis files were derived from a single legacy dataset file provided by the Wellcome Trust. Review of AQUAMAT relied on the publication (Dondorp et al. 2010) only; no protocol, CRF, CSR, or datasets were provided by the Applicant. A SAP for a re-analysis of the data was written on 10/15/2008 by the (b) (4) Corporation after the study was conducted and results were published in 2005.
L685: A Phase 2 study, EDCTP/MMV07-01, which compared a 5-dose regimen of artesunate at 2.4 mg/kg/dose with a 3-dose regimen of artesunate at 4.0 mg/kg/dose, each with the same total dose of 12 mg/kg, provided *P. falciparum* parasite clearance information at 24 hours in pediatric patients with severe malaria. The U.S. Army (WRAIR) formulation of IV artesunate was used in the study and was claimed to be equivalent to the Guilin formulation in the Clinical Study Report (CSR) by Medicines for Malaria Venture. Protocol, sample CRF, and CSR were provided for EDCTP/MMV07-01, with no datasets submitted.
L686: None of the 3 trials were conducted by the Applicant.
L687: ## 8 Statistical and Clinical Efficacy Evaluation
L688: ## 8.1 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L689: ## 8.1.1 SEAQUAMAT: A multicenter, open, randomized comparison between quinine and artesunate for the treatment of *falciparum* malaria in SE Asia. (Dondorp et al. 2005)
L690: ## 8.1.1.1 *Study Design*
L691: This was a multicenter, open-label, randomized, active comparator trial conducted between June 2003 and May 2005. The trial was conducted at 10 sites in 4 countries in Southeast Asia: Myanmar (7 sites), Bangladesh (1 site), India (1 site), and Indonesia (1 site). It was sponsored by the Wellcome Trust, with a study coordinating center at the Wellcome Trust Unit/Mahidol University in Thailand.
L692: The study objectives were to compare the mortality of severe *falciparum* malaria treated with parenteral quinine and artesunate (primary) as well as significant sequelae, recovery times, and development of complications of severe malaria.
L693: Patients with suspected malaria were admitted and examined. Malaria diagnosis was confirmed using HRP-2 (histidine rich protein 2) immunochromatographic test (Paracheck, Orchid Biosystems, Goa India), designed to detect *P. falciparum*; confirmatory microscopy was performed after randomization.
L694: Key eligibility criteria included the following:
L695: - Inclusion criteriaClinical diagnosis of severe malaria
- Positive test result for Paracheck
- Age 2 years or older
- Written informed consent by patient or relative
- Exclusion criteriaKnown allergy to artemisinin derivatives or quinine
- Convincing history of artesunate, mefloquine or significant quinine (&gt;2 g) intake in the previous 24 hours.
L696: Eligible patients were randomized 1:1 to the parenteral artesunate or quinine group. The original randomization method was planned to use blocks of size 10 or 20, but unrestricted randomization was implemented according to the Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP).
L697: Study drug (artesunate or quinine) was started with dosage based on body weight, as follows:
L698: - **Artesunate:** Intravenous artesunate (Guilin No 2 Pharmaceutical Factory, Guangxi, China) 2.4 mg/kg on admission, 12 hours after admission, and thereafter once daily until able to take po.
L699: **Oral artesunate** at 2 mg/kg/day was started once a patient was able to take tablets, to complete a total course (including parenteral) of 7 days; with total cumulative dose between 17 and 18 mg/kg over 7 days.
L700: - **Quinine:** Intravenous quinine dihydrochloride (Government Pharmaceutical Organization, Thailand) 20 mg salt/kg loading dose infused over 4 hours (in 666 ml 5% dextrose water) over 2 hours three times a day until the start of oral therapy. Loading dose of quinine was not to be given if patient received partial pretreatment with quinine or mefloquine.
L701: **Oral quinine** at 10 mg/kg every 8 hours was started once a patient was able to take tablets, to complete a total quinine course of 7 days.
L702: During the study, supportive treatment was given to patient in line with guidelines provided (World Health Organization 2000). At the time of hospital discharge or death in the hospital, information on outcome was recorded. Complications and significant neurological sequelae were assessed in discharged patients.
L703: Under the protocol, investigators were to replace any study center that enrolled primarily patients without severe malaria, with another center in the same country. Severe malaria was defined on study entry, with a single criterion listed below, in addition to asexual *P. falciparum* parasites on the peripheral blood smear (Dondorp et al. 2005):
L704: - Glasgow coma scale &lt;11/15 in adults, or Blantyre coma scale ≤3/5 in children.
- Shock, as assessed by the admitting physician (low blood pressure and cool peripheries)
- Blood bicarbonate &lt;15 mmol/L
- Hematocrit &lt; 20% and *P. falciparum* parasitemia &gt;100,000/μL
- Visible jaundice and *P. falciparum* parasitemia &gt; 100 000/μL
- Blood (BUN) &gt; 17 mmol/L
- Asexual *P. falciparum* parasitemia &gt;10%
- Plasma glucose &lt;2.2 mmol/L
- Respiratory distress (&gt;32 breaths/min)
L705: The trial was open-label. Local investigators were only blinded until after patients were randomized. The trial center, microscopists assessing blood slides, and data analysts remained unaware of treatment allocation until the end of the study. An external data safety monitoring committee assessed the progress of the trial. The study monitors inspected randomization, drug boxes, and patient records. External monitors regularly inspected the trial sites. From the publication, it is not clear if study monitors and external monitors were the same individuals.
L706: There was no information on compliance with Good Clinical Practices (GCP).
L707: ## **8.1.1.2 Study Endpoints**
L708: The following primary and secondary endpoints were defined in the protocol.
L709: - Primary efficacy endpoint was in-hospital all-cause mortality measured to time of hospital discharge (approximately one week)
L710: The protocol specified the main outcome measure as in-hospital mortality, but mortality, including both in hospital and after discharge were included as “in-hospital death” in the publication. In this review, we refer to in-hospital mortality as only deaths occurring in hospital and overall mortality as deaths occurring both in hospital and out of hospital.
L711: - Secondary efficacy endpoints (in order of assigned priority):Combined incidence of in-hospital death or significant neurological sequelae
- Significant neurological sequelae (cortical blindness, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia, loss of balance, extrapyramidal rigidity, hemiplegia/pareisis, neuropsychiatric, e.g. psychosis, other)
- Other significant sequelae
- Complications after hospital entry that were not present on admission (admission to ICU, development of coma, development of convulsions, development of shock, need for vasopressors, need for mechanical ventilation, need for dialysis, development of severe anemia, bleeding, blackwater fever, hypoglycemia, jaundice, sepsis, abortion during admission (pregnant patients), use of antibiotics, other)
- Fetal death as pregnancy outcome (pregnant patients)
- Time to death in days (with discharge at any time as additional category)
- Time to discharge in days (with death at any time as additional category)
- Time to eat in days (unaided)
- Time to sit in days (unaided)
- Safety: Drug exposure
L712: ## **8.1.1.3 Statistical Analysis Plan**
L713: A protocol was submitted, with very few statistical details. A SAP for a re-analysis of the data was written on 10/15/2008 (Version 2 by the [REDACTED](b) (4)) after the study results were published in 2005.
L714: The intent-to-treat population (ITT) included all patients randomized in the study. The severe malaria population consisted of randomized patients having parasitemia on blood smear
L715: fulfilling the criteria for severe malaria on post-hoc analysis using modified definitions by Hien at al. (1996), as previously listed.
L716: In the protocol, Kaplan-Meier plots and Cox's regression analysis were specified for the primary and secondary outcome variables. In the SAP, Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) method was specified for the primary efficacy analysis in the ITT population, stratified by site (hospital). The relative risk and odds ratios with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) would be calculated. We will report the results from both survival and binary outcomes.
L717: According to the SAP, for sensitivity analyses, the primary efficacy endpoint in the ITT population would be analyzed using the logistic regression method. According to the study report dated June 2010, covariates used in the sensitivity analyses included age (child&lt;15 years versus ≥15), gender, and severe malaria (yes/no).
L718: Primary efficacy endpoint would be analyzed in the severe malaria population and non-severe malaria populations using the CMH method. Analyses of mortality would be conducted in the three populations using Pearson's Chi-squared test (with no stratification).
L719: Secondary efficacy endpoint analyses of the incidence of significant sequelae, the overall incidence of significant sequelae, the combined incidence of mortality and any significant sequelae after hospital admission, the incidence of specific complications would be conducted using the logistic regression if there were sufficient incidence data to permit reliable statistical testing.
L720: Fetal death or abortion as a pregnancy outcome would be summarized for the pregnant ITT patients. Besides time to death, time to discharge, to eat, and to sit were analyzed using survival analysis.
L721: A sample size of 2000 was calculated for a detection of a 33% reduction in mortality from 12% to 8% with a statistical power of 80% and a two-sided Type I error of 5%, allowing a drop-out rate of 5%. The mortality estimate was approximately half the mortality rate of 24% from patients meeting the modified WHO criteria for severe malaria.
L722: According to the CSR, the protocol specified an interim analysis by an external Data Safety Monitoring Committee (DSMC) after inclusion of 500 patients and subsequently at time points chosen by the committee. The protocol did not provide a formal pre-specified plan to control the Type 1 error and there were no formal stopping rules pre-specified. The DSMC was advised that the clinical practice should only be changed if there was compelling evidence of at least a 25% treatment effect for artesunate compared with quinine. The DSMC was chaired by Prof. T. Peto, Oxford University, Oxford, UK.
L723: The first interim analysis was conducted after 659 patients completed the study. A second interim analysis was conducted when 1294 patients (about two thirds of the planned sample size) completed the trial with 244 reported deaths, which exceeded the number of the deaths projected for a completed trial. The p-value from the interim analysis was 0.0007. The trial was stopped after the second interim analysis. After termination of the trial, unblinded analysis results of 1461 patients were published. The estimated treatment effect in the publication was slightly smaller than that observed in the second (also the final) interim analysis.
L724: The primary endpoint was planned to be analyzed with a two-tailed test with a Type I error =0.05 at the final analysis with the planned sample size. There was no consideration for Type I error control due to the interim analyses. The method used to control the Type I error should have been prespecified in the analysis plan of the study. The Applicant considered this for the interim analyses and claimed that the test statistic would have crossed the stopping boundary had either of two commonly used stopping criteria been proposed, the O'Brien-Fleming or the Haybittle-Peto procedure with 3 to 5 interim analyses planned. Using either method, the study would have stopped early with the results seen at the second interim analysis. Additional discussion of this can be found in Statistical Issues 8.3.
L725: ## 8.1.1.4 *Study Results*
L726: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L727: This information is not available.
L728: ## 8.1.1.5 *Financial Disclosure*
L729: This trial was supported by the Wellcome Trust. No information is available for financial disclosure from the Applicant.
L730: ## Patient Disposition
L731: A total of 1461 patients were enrolled and randomized in the trial, as shown in the following table. All patients received the assigned treatment and were included in the ITT population. About 72% of the patients had severe malaria. There were 10 hospitals (sites) in the study, enrolling from 2 to 288 patients per hospital. Although the study report states that two sites from Myanmar were replaced, the data sets and analyses did not exclude these two sites (155 patients).

L734: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L735: The original plan was to assign a center to a route of administration (IV or IM) for the study drugs. However, all centers administered the study drugs IV. Additionally, a few centers (Chittagong Hospital in Bangladesh, the Ispat Hospital in Orissa, India) did not administer doxycycline as part of the sequential therapy with oral study drug as planned.
L736: A total of 143 patients in the quinine group did not receive a loading dose. Of these, 105 received no pre-treatment with quinine, which appears to be a deviation from the assigned treatment protocol. Specific information on protocol deviations was not captured in the database.
L737: ## Demographic Characteristics
L738: Demographic characteristics are shown in the following table. About 13.8% of subjects were under the age of 15 years and 2.6% were 65 years old or older. The majority of the subjects were male. Age, sex, and body weight were well balanced between the two treatment groups. The proportion of subjects with severe malaria or cerebral malaria was slightly lower in the artesunate group than in the quinine group. Therefore, efficacy analysis by these two diagnoses will be conducted.

L749: ## Other Baseline Characteristics
L750: Pre-treatment was the treatment received prior to admission, which included antimalarial drugs, such as quinine, artemisinin derivative, chloroquine, and sulphadoxine-pyrimethamine. About 21% of the patients were pre-treated with an antimalarial drug and 16.6% with an effective antimalarial drug (quinine, artemisinin derivative, or mefloquine). About 13% of the patients were pre-treated with quinine, the most commonly used antimalarial drug. The artesunate group had a slightly higher proportion of pre-treatment with quinine. As chloroquine was not considered as an effective antimalarial drug in this trial, the slightly higher proportion of patients using chloroquine in the artesunate group should not be a concern.

L788: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L789: Treatment compliance was not evaluated in the study since treatment was administered by nurses in a hospital setting.
L790: A total of 588 subjects in the quinine group (80.4%) received the loading dose, 93 of whom were pre-treated with antimalarial drugs (46 with quinine and 47 with other). A total of 143 subjects (19.6%) in the quinine group did not receive the loading dose of quinine, 94 of whom were not pretreated with antimalarial drugs, and 49 of whom were pretreated with antimalarial drugs (38 with quinine and 11 with other drugs). It is not clear why some subjects did not receive the loading dose.

L801: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L802: The primary efficacy endpoint, in-hospital mortality, was significantly lower in the artesunate group (Table 8-14), based on the statistical reviewer's analysis. The odds ratio (OR) from the stratified CMH analysis was 0.59 [95% CI: 0.44, 0.79], stratified by study site (hospital) with a p-value of 0.0002. The Breslow-Day test for homogeneity of the ORs across strata for in-hospital mortality did not detect a difference across sites (p-value 0.28). The OR from unstratified analysis of in-hospital mortality was 0.59 [95% CI: 0.44, 0.78] with a p-value of 0.0002. The overall mortality demonstrated a similar result as the in-hospital mortality (OR=0.60, 95% CI [0.45, 0.79], stratified by study site (hospital) with a p-value of 0.0002). The Breslow-Day test for homogeneity of the ORs across strata for overall mortality did not detect a difference across sites (p-value 0.34). The results for overall mortality were reported in the publication and CSR.
L803: The OR from unstratified analysis of overall mortality was 0.59 [95% CI: 0.45, 0.78] with a p-value of 0.0001, based on the statistical reviewer's analysis.

L814: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
L815: Results from selected secondary endpoints are presented in Table 8-16. The combined incidence of overall death or significant neurological sequelae was statistically significantly lower in the artesunate group than in the quinine group. The OR was 0.63 with a 95% CI of [0.48, 0.82] and p-value of 0.0007 from the CMH test, stratified by study site (hospital) (Breslow-Day test for homogeneity of the ORs was 0.31).
L816: Neurological sequelae in the CRF included cortical blindness, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia, loss of balance, extrapyramidal rigidity, hemiplegia/pareisis, neuropsychiatric, e.g. psychosis, other. Ten patients were discharged with significant neurological sequelae as captured in the CRF with the overall question on significant neurological sequelae (yes/no), 7 in the artesunate group and 3 in the quinine group. The listed sequelae for those subjects were identified from each sequela question. An additional 5 and 4 subjects had specific neurological sequelae on discharge, but not captured in the CRF overall question.

L824: discharge was likely to indicate that the two groups had comparable observation time for the assessment of the in-hospital mortality, minimizing the possibility that the reduced mortality in the artesunate group was due to short in-hospital observation time. L829: ## Subgroup Analyses
L830: The following table displays the in-hospital mortality subgroup analysis results by age, sex, and selected baseline disease characteristics. The artesunate group had a consistently lower mortality than the quinine group for all subgroups except in children less than 20 kg body weight. However, due to the small sample sizes in children &lt; 20 kg, it is difficult to draw conclusions based on these results. Artesunate reduced mortality in subgroups of cerebral malaria, pre-treatment with any or effective antimalarial treatment, parasite smear or count result, pregnancy, and hypoglycemia. For cerebral malaria, the dataset and publication provide different numbers, but these differences did not impact the treatment effect across different subgroups.
L831: It is noted that in subjects in the quinine group not pretreated with quinine, the mortality rate in subjects who did not receive the quinine loading dose was lower compared to those subjects receiving the loading dose. As this was an open-label study, receiving the loading dose may have been dependent on the severity of the disease; the interpretation of this result is difficult. In the artesunate group, pretreatment with quinine did not affect the mortality significantly.
L832: In a logistic regression conducted by the statistical reviewer, the impact of pretreatment antimalarial therapy was explored. The logistic regression contained the following variables: pretreatment with antimalarials, severe malaria, cerebral malaria, treatment group, and age group (&lt;15 years or not). All were significantly associated with in-hospital mortality. In a regression to test if there was an interaction between pretreatment and treatment group, the interaction was not significant. From this analysis, pretreatment and treatment with artesunate had independent effects on the outcome after controlling for severe malaria, cerebral malaria, and age. As the following table shows, it appears that artesunate was more efficacious in subjects with severe malaria, especially those without cerebral malaria, who were pretreated with antimalarials [mortality: 2/49 (4.1%) in the artesunate group versus 8/31 (25.8%) in the quinine group], although the trial could not reach a definitive conclusion on this effect and there were no reasons identified.

L839: ## 8.1.2 AQUAMAT: Artesunate versus quinine in the treatment of severe falciparum malaria in African children: an open-label, randomized trial
L840: ## 8.1.2.1 *Study Design*
L841: This was a multicenter, randomized, open-label trial conducted between October 2005 and July 2010 at 11 centers in nine African countries (Mozambique, The Gambia, Ghana, Kenya, Tanzania, Nigeria, Uganda, Rwanda, and Democratic Republic of the Congo). This study was funded by the Wellcome Trust and coordinated by the Wellcome Trust Mahidol-Oxford Tropical Medicine Search Unit in Bangkok, Thailand. Data, protocol, and study report were not submitted for this study. All information is obtained from the trial publication (Dondorp et al. 2010).
L842: This study compared the mortality of severe *falciparum* malaria treated with artesunate versus quinine. Key eligibility criteria and exclusion criteria are as follows:
L843: - Major inclusion criteriaChildren younger than 15 years of age
- Positive rapid diagnostic test for *P. falciparum* (Optimal)
- Severe malaria in the opinion of the admitting physician
- Informed consent from patient or guardian
- Major exclusion criteriaConvincing history of full treatment with parenteral quinine or an artemisinin derivative for more than 24 h before admission
- Age criteria varied slightly at different sites according to the request of the respective ethics review boards
L844: Severe malaria was defined as in the following table.
L845: **Table 8-19: AQUAMAT Modified Criteria for Severe Malaria**
L846: At least one of:
• Plasma base excess < -3.3 mmol/L
• Glasgow coma scale < 11 of 15, or Blantyre coma scale < 3 of 5 in preverbal children
• Hemoglobin < 50 g/L and parasitemia greater than 100,000 per \muL
• BUN > 10 mmol/L
• Compensated shock (capillary refill \ge 3 sec or temp gradient on legs, but no hypotension)
L847: Decompensated shock; systolic BP < 70 mm Hg and cool peripheries
Asexual parasitemia more than 10%
Visible jaundice and > 100,000 parasites per \muL
Plasma glucose < 3 mmol/L
Respiratory distress, defined as costal indrawing, use of accessory muscles, nasal flaring, deep breathing, or severe tachypnea.
L848: Decompensated shock; systolic BP < 70 mm Hg and cool peripheries
Asexual parasitemia more than 10%
Visible jaundice and > 100,000 parasites per \muL
Plasma glucose < 3 mmol/L
Respiratory distress, defined as costal indrawing, use of accessory muscles, nasal flaring, deep breathing, or severe tachypnea.
L849: Source: Panel 1 of the publication
L850: Source: Panel 1 of the publication
L851: Eligible patients were randomized to treatment with either intravenous or intramuscular artesunate or quinine (blocks of 20, with sealed envelopes associated with a study box containing the study drug, CRF and other items needed for study). Treatment was to be started immediately.
L852: Eligible patients were randomized to treatment with either intravenous or intramuscular artesunate or quinine (blocks of 20, with sealed envelopes associated with a study box containing the study drug, CRF and other items needed for study). Treatment was to be started immediately.
L853: - **Artesunate** (Guilin Pharmaceutical Factory, Guangxi): 2.4 mg/kg IV on admission, at 12 hours, 24 hours and thereafter once daily until oral medication could be taken reliably.60 mg vial was dissolved in 1 mL 5% sodium bicarbonate (provided with study drug), then diluted with 5% dextrose before injection either as a bolus IV or via deep intramuscular (IM) injection in the anterior thigh.
- **Quinine** (Indus Pharma, Pakistan) was given as 20 mg salt per kg loading dose infused over 4 h in 5-10 mLs /kg 5% dextrose, followed by 10 mg/kg infusion over 2-8 hours three times daily until starting oral therapy.IM doses were the same as IV doses but quinine was diluted in normal saline for a concentration of 60 mg/mL. Loading dose was a split dose in each thigh.
- When a patient was able to take po and after a minimum of 24-hour parenteral treatment, **oral artemether-lumefantrine (Coartem)** was administered at a standard dose (1.5 mg artemether/9 mg lumefantrine/kg) for three days with milk or fat, to complete the treatment.
L854: - **Artesunate** (Guilin Pharmaceutical Factory, Guangxi): 2.4 mg/kg IV on admission, at 124 hours, 24 hours and thereafter once daily until oral medication could be taken reliably.
- 60 mg vial was dissolved in 1 mL 5% sodium bicarbonate (provided with study drug), then diluted with 5% dextrose before injection either as a bolus IV or via deep intramuscular (IM) injection in the anterior thigh.
- **Quinine** (Indus Pharma, Pakistan) was given as 20 mg salt per kg loading dose infused over 4 h in 5-10 mLs /kg 5% dextrose, followed by 10 mg/kg infusion over 2-8 hours three times daily until starting oral therapy.IM doses were the same as IV doses but quinine was diluted in normal saline for a concentration of 60 mg/mL. Loading dose was a split dose in each thigh.
- When a patient was able to take po and after a minimum of 24-hour parenteral treatment, **oral artemether-lumefantrine (Coartem)** was administered at a standard dose (1.5 mg artemether/9 mg lumefantrine/kg) for three days with milk or fat, to complete the treatment.
Patients were discharged at the discretion of the physician; a discharge assessment was completed. Active follow-up after discharge was instituted. Children who had not made a full neurological recovery by discharge were followed at regular intervals for 12 months or until full recovery. Full neurological assessments were completed at every visit.

Two committees provided assessment and classifications of outcomes before study unblinding:

- Neurological outcome committee (two pediatricians and one malariologist) assessed four domains: motor function, visual function, hearing, and speech. This committee also evaluated whether the patient developed epilepsy. Sequelae persisting at follow-up were graded as mild, moderate, or severe in each domain according to the extent of functional impairment.
L855: Patients were discharged at the discretion of the physician; a discharge assessment was completed. Active follow-up after discharge was instituted. Children who had not made a full neurological recovery by discharge were followed at regular intervals for 12 months or until full recovery. Full neurological assessments were completed at every visit.
L856: Two committees provided assessment and classifications of outcomes before study unblinding:
L857: - Neurological outcome committee (two pediatricians and one malariologist) assessed four domains: motor function, visual function, hearing, and speech. This committee also evaluated whether the patient developed epilepsy. Sequelae persisting at follow-up were graded as mild, moderate, or severe in each domain according to the extent of functional impairment.
L858: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L859: - Endpoint review committee (one pediatrician and one clinical malariologist) identified cases in which a pathological change other than malaria or its acute complications was likely to be the main cause of death.
L860: Trial was open-label at each site but none of the investigators or trialists, apart from the statistician, had access to the summaries of treatment allocations. When notes or CRFs were reviewed, study drug details were removed to preserve masking. All case reviews and laboratory assessments were blinded.
L861: Sites were monitored regularly. Investigators provided reports to coordinating center in Bangkok every two weeks and met every year to review study progress. DSMB made three interim analyses during the trial. Though, there did not appear to be any procedures for controlling the Type I error due to the interim analyses, it does not appear that the study stopped early based on an interim analysis.
L862: ## 8.1.2.2 *Study Endpoints*
L863: The primary endpoint was in-hospital mortality. Secondary endpoints included incidence of severe neurological complications assessed at 28 days (range 3-8 weeks) and a combined outcome measure of death and severe persistent neurological sequelae. The publication notes that initially neurological outcomes were assessed only at discharge from the hospital, but the authors state that this led to a substantial overestimation of the neurological deficit, especially in young children. The protocol was changed in April 2007 after 11% of patients were enrolled such that children who had not yet fully recovered at discharge were assessed 28 days after enrollment, with active follow-up thereafter.
L864: ## 8.1.2.3 *Statistical Analysis Plan*
L865: The sample size of 5306 was planned to demonstrate a 25% reduction in mortality from 8% to 6% with an 80% statistical power and a Type I error of 0.05. Mortality estimate was based on an estimated severe malaria mortality of 16% with about half the enrolled patients anticipated to fulfill the criteria for severe malaria.
L866: Primary analysis was intention-to-treat (ITT), although it was not defined in the publication. Based on the publication, it appears to have included all randomized subjects.
L867: Per-protocol analysis was also conducted, excluding patients:
L868: - Not fulfilling entry criteria;
- With negative or missing malaria smear for *P. falciparum*;
- Who died before receiving study drug; or
- Who were missing a study drug dose on the first day of treatment.
L869: Subgroup analyses included those fulfilling the criteria of severe malaria (all centers) and HIV status (some centers).
L870: The sample size of 5306 was planned to demonstrate a 25% reduction in mortality from 8% to 6% with an 80% statistical power and a type I error of 0.05. Mortality estimate was based on an estimated severe malaria mortality of 16% with about half the enrolled patients anticipated to fulfill the criteria for severe malaria.
L871: There was no statistical analysis plan submitted. For binary outcomes, the ORs between the treatment groups were estimated using the CMH method, stratified by study site. Heterogeneity between sites was examined with the Breslow-Day test. Time to events was compared with the Log-rank test, and hazard ratios (HRs) were estimated by a Cox proportional hazards model, stratified by study site.
L872: ## **8.1.2.4 Protocol Amendments**
L873: This information was not provided. As discussed above, initially neurological outcomes were assessed only at discharge from hospital. Due to overestimation of the outcomes, especially in young children, patients who had not fully discovered at discharge were instead assessed 28 days after enrolment when 11% patients had been enrolled.
L874: ## **8.1.2.5 Study Results**
L875: ## **Compliance with Good Clinical Practices**
L876: This information was not available.
L877: ## **Financial Disclosure**
L878: This trial was supported by the Wellcome Trust. No information is available for financial disclosure from the Applicant.
L879: ## **Data Quality and Integrity**
L880: It is not applicable as there were no data submitted.
L881: ## **Patient Disposition**
L882: A total 11148 patients were assessed for eligibility and 5425 patients were enrolled. Enrolled subjects were randomized (1:1) to the artesunate group or quinine group. About 65% of randomized subjects received treatment by IV (1779/2712 on artesunate and 1724/2713 on quinine) and 35% by IM. All enrolled patients were included in the ITT population. A total of 148 and 161 ITT patients were excluded from the per-protocol population. The numbers of patients for each exclusion reason were comparable, except for the imbalance of deaths before receiving the assigned treatment (6 on artesunate versus 22 on quinine). According to the
L883: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L884: publication, this imbalance of deaths was indicative of the difficulties in administering parenteral quinine promptly and safely, as IV quinine needs a careful rate-controlled infusion and continuous or three-time daily administration to avoid dangerous hypotension. Note that a delay in treatment likely increased mortality in the quinine arm. This appears to have impacted mortality prior to receipt of randomized treatment, but it might have also increased mortality in the quinine group if quinine administration was delayed in the treated patients as well. This should be taken into account when interpreting the study and will be discussed in the result section.

L908: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L909: The following table shows mortality results from the study. In the ITT analysis, there was a
L910: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L911: significantly lower mortality rate in the artesunate group than in the quinine group (OR=0.75, 95% CI [0.63–0.90]; p=0.0022). There was no significant heterogeneity in treatment effect between study sites (p=0.99). An unstratified Chi-squared test conducted by the reviewer provided the same results as the reported stratified analysis.
L912: In the per protocol analysis, the treatment effect of artesunate remained highly statistically significant. As mentioned above, there was an imbalance of deaths after randomization but prior to treatment and these subjects were excluded from this per protocol analysis. Nevertheless, this is concerning in that the control group was potentially weakened by delays of treatment, impacting not only these patients but potentially a larger proportion of patients in the quinine group. Therefore, the reported results might have been less of a comparison between IV artesunate and IV quinine and more between IV artesunate and delayed IV quinine.
L913: Eight subjects (5 on artesunate, 3 on quinine) were taken from hospital against medical advice and could not be followed up further. If they were considered as deaths in the primary analyses, the treatment effect still remained highly significant.
L914: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
L915: Some secondary endpoints are presented in the following table. Death or sequelae at 28 days occurred less frequently in the artesunate group (OR=0.78, 95% CI [0.65–0.93], p=0.0056).
L916: A lower proportion of children in the artesunate group had convulsions that either developed after admission, or were present on admission and persisted for more than 5 hours (OR=0.80, 95% CI [0.66–0.97]; p=0.0199) or had a deterioration of coma score after starting antimalarial treatment (OR=0.78, 95% CI [0.64–0.97]; p=0.0245).
L917: In the HIV positive children, mortality was numerically lower in the artesunate group than in the quinine group.
L918: Development of coma, deterioration of coma score, convulsions developing or persisting &gt;6 hours after admission, and hypoglycemia occurred statistically significantly less frequently in the artesunate group than in the quinine group.
L919: The incidence of blackwater fever was numerically higher and the incidence of severe anemia was numerically lower in the artesunate group, but not statistically significantly different between the two treatment groups.
