L945: ## 8.1.3 EDCTP/MMV07-01 Phase II Randomized, Double-Blind Study of the Efficacy, Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Intravenous Artesunate in Children with Severe Malaria
L946: ## 8.1.3.1 *Study Design*
L947: This was a double-blind, multicenter, randomized, parallel-group study, conducted by Medicines for Malaria Venture in Switzerland from October 2007 to July 2008. Only the protocol, sample CRF, and study report was submitted, with no data sets. The review is based on these submitted materials.
L948: There were three centers in this study:
L949: - Center 1001: Albert Schweitzer Hospital, Lambarene, Gabon
- Center 2001: Universite de Medecin et Science de la Sante, Libreville, Gabon
- Center 3001: Queen Elizabeth Central Hospital, Blantyre, Malawi
L950: The primary objective was to evaluate the effectiveness of two IV artesunate dosing regimens in clearing *P. falciparum* parasites in pediatric patients with severe malaria. The secondary objectives were 1) to compare the tolerability and safety of the 2 IV artesunate dosing regimens and 2) to evaluate differences in the pharmacokinetic profile of IV artesunate by patient age and clinical presentation.
L951: We consider that the censoring was non-informative, a strong assumption for survival analysis.
L952: Patients (children with severe malaria) would be block randomized to 1 of 2 regimens, with 6 patients per block and a randomization ratio of 1:1. Doses for the two regimens were as follows:
L953: - 5-dose regimen: 2.4 mg/kg initially and at 12, 24, 48, and 72 hours
- 3-dose regimen: 4.0 mg/kg initially and at 24 and 48 hours (normal saline at 12 and 72 hours to maintain the study blind)
L954: As soon as the patient was able to receive oral medication and no signs and symptoms of severe malaria were present, but not before the last pharmacokinetic sample was taken (approximately 50 hours after the start of therapy), a single dose of sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine based on body weight was to be administered to ensure parasitological cure. Sites 1001 and 2001 used oral sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine. Site 3001 used oral artemether/lumefantrine.
L955: We noted that the 2.4 mg/kg regimen matched the doses of the previous two trials up to 72 hours and the 4.0 mg/kg regimen was at a higher dose and did not include the dose at 12 hours,
L956: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L957: although the total dose was the same for the two regimens. Based on the proposed dose for this product, the 5-dose regimen is the focus of this review. Due to a possible use of other single-dose antimalarial drug around 50 hours, only the doses at 0, 12, and 24 hours in the 5-dose regimen could be used for the evaluation of efficacy. After 48 hours, the effect could be the combined effect of artesunate and the other antimalarial drug.
L958: In this double-blind study, the patient and research team (except for the study pharmacist/designee) were kept blinded as to the assigned treatment.
L959: Patients were hospitalized for at least 4 days (Days 0, 1, 2, and 3) and returned to the study site for study visits on Days 7, 14, and 28. Patients with unresolved adverse events by Day 28 would be followed for an additional 30 days or until resolution of the event or no further medical management was deemed necessary. If any untoward event occurred within 30 days of completing the study drug, the patient was instructed to return to the study site.
L960: Inclusion criteria were as follows:
L961: - Male or female children aged 6 months and \ge 5\text{kg} to 10 years, inclusive.
- Clinical diagnosis of severe *P. falciparum* malaria requiring hospitalization.
- Parasitemia (\ge 5,000 parasites/\mu\text{L} on initial blood smear).
- Availability of child's parent/guardian and their willingness to provide written informed consent in accordance to local practice.
- Willingness and ability to comply with the study protocol for the duration of the study.
- Willingness to remain in the hospital for 4 days.
L962: Exclusion criteria included:
L963: - Known serious adverse reaction or hypersensitivity to artemisinins, including artesunate, artemether, dihydroartemisinins or Coartem (artemether/lumefantrine).
- Any underlying disease that may compromise the diagnosis and the evaluation of the response to the study medication (including concomitant infection, concomitant neurological disease and malnutrition)
- Participation in any investigational drug study during the 30 days prior to screening
- Adequate anti-malarial treatment within 24 hours prior to admission
L964: The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of patients with parasite clearance (\ge 99\% reduction from the baseline asexual parasite count) at 24 hours after initiation of study drug.
L965: Secondary efficacy endpoints included:
L966: - Time to total clearance of asexual parasites (\text{PC}_{100})
- Time to 99% reduction of asexual parasites (\text{PC}_{99})
L967: - Time to 90% reduction of asexual parasites (PC_{90})
- Time to 50% reduction of asexual parasites (PC_{50})
- Adequate clinical and parasitological response on day 28
- PCR corrected adequate clinical and parasitological response on day 28
- Parasitological cure rate on day 28
- Percent reduction in asexual parasites from baseline at 24 hours after initiation of randomized study drug
- Percent reduction in asexual parasites from baseline at 48 hours after initiation of randomized study drug
- Percent reduction in gametocytes from baseline at 24 hours after initiation of randomized study drug
- Percent reduction in gametocytes from baseline at 48 hours after initiation of randomized study drug
L968: PC_{100} was defined as no asexual parasites for 2 consecutive negative readings \ge 6 hours apart, with a confirmed negative reading 24 hours after the first negative slide. Time to PC_{100} was defined as the time from the first dose of study drug to the first negative reading. Patients for whom PC_{100} could not be calculated because they did not have parasite clearance by 72 hours after randomization or who were withdrawn for treatment failure before 72 hours were censored at 72 hours. Patients who terminated early without achieving parasite clearance for reasons other than treatment failure (e.g., adverse event, patient request, intercurrent illness) were censored at the last available parasite count.
L969: ## **8.1.3.2 Statistical Analysis Plan**
L970: The planned sample size was 200 in total. Assuming a 0.68 proportion of patients achieving PC_{99} in both treatment groups and 100 patients per treatment group, the 95% CI (based on the normal approximation) for the observed treatment group difference of zero would be -0.13 to 0.13. The 95% CI for the estimated PC_{99} for each treatment group would be 0.59 to 0.77.
L971: Intent-to-treat (ITT) population included all patients from the safety population who had parasitologically confirmed infection with *P. falciparum* prior to treatment had an evaluable primary endpoint and no serious protocol violation or withdrawal of consent.
L972: We do not agree with the condition of having an evaluable primary endpoint and no serious protocol violation or withdrawal of consent. The ITT population should include all randomized subjects, regardless of having evaluable primary endpoint, serious protocol violation or withdrawal of consent, or other post-randomization information. These post-randomization exclusions could be impacted by the study treatment, making the two treatment arms being analyzed differently, greatly impacting the benefits of randomization. We will conduct additional analyses including all randomized subjects.
L973: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L974: Per Protocol (PP) population included all patients from the ITT population who received at least two doses of randomized study drug and reached the primary efficacy endpoint (or reached an efficacy endpoint such as non-response, relapse, etc.). No antimalarial agent other than the protocol-specified treatment regimens was administered during the period from 72 hours prior to start of randomized study drug until after Day 28 unless the subject is considered a study treatment failure. Inclusion in the PP population was determined before breaking the study blind. This was a supportive analysis population for efficacy.
L975: ## Analysis Methods
L976: Chi-squared test was used to assess treatment group differences in parasite clearance for the PP and ITT populations. Two-sided 95% CIs were provided for the estimated parasite clearance rates in each treatment group and for the difference between treatment groups.
L977: In addition to the planned analysis, in the study report, a noninferiority test was performed. Two-sided 90% CI was provided in order to have one-sided test at a Type I error of 5% to test the 3-dose group was not inferior to the 5-dose group, using a relative risk of 0.8 as the margin. Given that both groups are considered test groups and the noninferiority margin was not justified, we will not cover the results of the noninferiority test.
L978: PC100 was summarized using Kaplan-Meier estimates. Patients for whom PC100 could not be calculated because they did not have parasite clearance by 72 hours after randomization or who were withdrawn for treatment failure before 72 hours were noted as having a censored PC100 of 72 hours.
L979: Patients who terminated early for reasons other than treatment failure (e.g., adverse event, patient request, intercurrent illness) were noted as having a censored PC100 as of the last available information on parasite counts. PC99, PC90, and PC50 were similarly defined and analyzed. Treatment group comparisons for percent reduction in asexual parasites from baseline at 24 hours and 48 hours after initiation of randomized study drug were made with the Wilcoxon rank sum test.
L980: Percent reduction in parasites was imputed with last observation carried forward for those patients who prematurely discontinued before the scheduled evaluation.

The analyses of secondary endpoints Adequate Clinical and Parasitological Response on Day 28 (genotype-corrected, genotype-uncorrected) were not performed, for unknown reasons.

L992: There were no data submitted for FDA review. Per CSR, the study monitors were verifying that the data were recorded correctly and completely. The CSR by Medicines for Malaria Venture stated that all data collected in the CRF were double entered into the data management system for the analyses.
L993: ## Patient Disposition
L994: Of all 197 patients randomized, 194 were included in the safety population and 3 were excluded for no dosing done, withdrawal of consent, or serious protocol violation. Due to serious protocol violation (3), withdrawal of consent (1) or aparasitemia on day 0 (1), and missing time for parasite clearance at 24 hours (7), 12 subjects in the safety population were excluded from the ITT population, leaving 182 subjects in the ITT population. We do not know the assigned treatment for these excluded subjects as no data were available. It is even more difficult to rely on the results of the defined ITT because we cannot assess how subjects were excluded between the arms. Eleven ITT subjects were excluded from the PP population due to the protocol deviations described below. Two patients died; one for unknown reason on Day 2 and the death was classified as possibly related to the study drug. The second patient died on day 1 following grand mal convulsion which was classified as an event not related to the study drug. Both patients were from the 3-dose group.

L1015: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L1016: Study drug was administered by authorized staff only and amount was recorded. Compliance with administration according to the protocol was achieved. Adjunctive therapy, including fluids, glucose, and blood (according to SMAC standards and WHO guidelines for the treatment of severe malaria) was given. In case of initial treatment failure with IV artesunate or a severe drug reaction to artesunate, parenteral quinine was administered. However, this information was not included in the study report.
L1017: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L1018: The primary efficacy endpoint, the proportion of patients with parasite clearance (\ge 99\% reduction from the baseline asexual parasite count) at 24 hours after initiation of study drug, is shown in the following table. At the time of assessment of the primary endpoint (24 hours), since the dose administered at 24 hours would have had no time to act, the comparison of the 2 arms compares the effect of the first 2 doses of 2.4 mg/kg at 0 and 12 hours in the 5-dose regimen to the first dose (4.0 mg/kg) at 0 hours of the 3-dose regimen. If we assume that the 4.0 mg/kg would have had the same or stronger effect than the 2.4 mg/kg dose at time 0, the difference seen can be considered a conservative estimate of the effect of the 2.4 mg/kg dose given at 12 hours. The difference of 8.5% (95% CI: [-2.7%, 19.7%]) in PC_{99} was indicative of an effect of the 12-hour dose although it was not statistically significant.
L1019: As a sensitivity analysis, we assumed the randomized subjects excluded from the ITT subjects as failures, or as failures on 5-dose regimen and successes on 3-dose regimen. The difference was similar to the result from the ITT population and was close to 0 for two assumptions, respectively.

L1032: ## 8.2 Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L1033: ## 8.2.1 Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L1034: The SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT trials were supported by the same sponsor, the Wellcome Trust, with similar study designs (open-label, randomized) to compare the efficacy and safety of parenteral artesunate versus quinine. However, there were some differences. The study population in SEAQUAMAT was Asian with adults (86.2%) and pediatric patients aged 2-15 years (13.8%) while in AQUAMAT, African pediatric patients younger than 15 years old (exact range not reported) were enrolled. The administration route in SEAQUAMAT was IV and in AQUAMAT was IV (64.5%) and IM (35.5%). The third study, which had a different study design and primary endpoint and for which no data were submitted, will not be covered in this section.
L1035: ## Primary Endpoint
L1036: In the first two trials, the primary endpoint was in-hospital mortality. Artesunate demonstrated a significant reduction in mortality compared with quinine, as the following table shows. AQUAMAT had a higher overall mortality rate; however, given the differences in these trials, including location and patient age, differences in mortality rates between the trials are not unexpected.

L1052: ## 8.2.2 Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L1053: Although patient-level data were only available from SEAQUAMAT, the data were considered adequate to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness based on the persuasive finding of mortality benefit in this trial with a similarly robust mortality benefit reported in the AQUAMAT trial publication.
L1054: SEAQUAMAT demonstrated the efficacy of IV artesunate in the treatment of severe malaria in adults and pediatric patients. Significantly reduced mortality was observed in the artesunate group compared to the quinine group. In this trial, the observed in-hospital mortality was 96/730 (13.2%) in the artesunate group and 150/731 (20.5%) in the quinine group, with an OR of 0.59 (95% CI: 0.44, 0.79, p=0.0002). The in-hospital mortality was lower in the subgroup of subjects who received any prior antimalaria treatment than in the subgroup of subjects who did not. AQUAMAT provided supportive information for IV artesunate's efficacy in severe malaria in a younger patient population in a different region of the world; however, patient level data were not available for review.
L1055: A third trial, EDCTP/MMV07-01, demonstrated high proportions of early parasite reduction in subjects treated with artesunate IV. Slight differences in the treatment regimens in that trial, demonstrated the importance of the 12-hour dose; however, patient level data were also not available.
L1056: ## 8.3 Statistical Issues
L1057: No major statistical issues were identified. However, there were a few minor statistical issues worthy of discussion. In SEAQUAMAT, according to the protocol, an interim analysis was to be performed after enrollment of 500 patients and after that at time points chosen by the DSMC. It is noted that the DSMC was advised that clinical practice would only change if there was compelling evidence of at least a 25% treatment effect for artesunate compared to quinine, although it is not clear a 25% treatment effect was on absolute difference in mortality rates, relative risk, or odds ratios. There were two interim analyses conducted in the trial. The first interim analysis was conducted in June 2004 with 659 patients included (129 deaths). The second interim analysis was conducted in February 2005 when there were 244 deaths for the 1294 patients included. The p-value at that point was 0.0007. The trial was recommended to stop at this interim analysis based on the strength and robustness of the efficacy findings and supporting safety results. The trial was stopped on May 11, 2005 with 1461 patients included. Despite not having plans in place to control the Type I error, it is noted that the study results would have been significant based on the commonly used and acceptable stopping rules (the O'Brien-Fleming or the Haybittle-Peto procedure) even if 3-5 interim analyses were considered.
L1058: Intravenous quinine requires a carefully rate-controlled infusion with a continuous or three times daily administration to avoid dangerous hypotension. In the AQUAMAT ITT analysis population, more subjects died before receiving the assigned treatment in the quinine group. More early deaths in the quinine group might be pointing to an impact of the delay in the quinine group and a potential weakness/vulnerability of that group. It should be clear that a direct comparison of artesunate to quinine might not be appropriate based on this study. It was a practical comparison of efficacy, given the constraint in drug administration, not a direct (theoretical) comparison of administering the drug in the same time period. The trial still demonstrated superior efficacy, but it might not have demonstrated such a large effect over a prompt treatment with quinine. In addition, lack of patient-level data was a limitation of this study.
L1059: In these two trials, as there was no specific follow-up procedure after discharge, mortality data after discharge may not have been consistently collected. Therefore, the interpretation of comparative overall mortality results between the two treatment groups should be made with caution.
L1060: Both adjusted and unadjusted analyses of in-hospital mortality by study site (hospital) produced a similar or the same results, indicating that the treatment effects were homogeneous and not greatly impacted by hospital.
L1061: ## 8.4 Conclusions and Recommendations
L1062: The SEAQUAMAT study was considered by the statistical reviewer as an adequate and well-controlled trial that demonstrated a statistically significant improvement in the in-hospital
L1063: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1064: mortality in patients receiving IV artesunate compared to IV quinine for the treatment of severe malaria. The results were strong and consistent across multiple subgroups. AQUAMAT provided supportive evidence from a different region and in younger patients. The application meets the evidentiary standard of effectiveness and demonstrated efficacy of artesunate. Therefore, artesunate is recommended for approval for the proposed indication of treatment of patients with severe malaria.
L1065: ## 9 Clinical Microbiology Review
L1066: ## 9.1 Nonclinical Microbiology
L1067: ## 9.1.1 Mechanism of Action
L1068: The precise mechanism by which artesunate and DHA exhibit activity against *Plasmodium* species is not clearly understood. Studies suggest that the mechanism of action of artesunate and DHA, like other artemisinins, is likely multifactorial. Endoperoxide bridge is essential for the antiparasitic activity (Figure 9-1). Upon activation by heme (liberated as a result of plasmodial protease-mediated degradation of host hemoglobin), artesunate and DHA, like other artemisinins, generate free radicals (e.g., superoxide anions, H_2O_2 and other intermediates), increase oxidative stress within the infected RBCs, cause alkylation of proteins and lipids as well as ultrastructural changes leading to decrease in parasite function, growth and survival. The mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum, as well as digestive vacuole appear to be the sites of damage.
L1069: **Figure 9-1: Diagrammatic representation of the mechanism of action and resistance to qinghaosu/artemisinin.**
L1070: The activation by Fe^{2+} releases free radicals, hydroperoxides, and other intermediates, and the addition of these products to target biomolecules, (e.g., proteins, forming damaged proteins), leads to rapid parasite death (shown in solid line symbols). The resistant parasites have up-regulated unfolded protein response pathways including reactive oxidative stress complex, decreasing protein damage caused by artemisinin action and slowing parasite growth rate (shown in broken line symbols).
L1071: Source: (Krungkrai and Krungkrai 2016)
L1072: The uptake of artemisinins varies with different developmental forms of the *P. falciparum* parasite; the ring forms accumulate lower amounts of DHA compared to trophozoites. At the early ring stage, activation appears to rely primarily on the parasite's heme biosynthesis pathway and at latter stages on hemoglobin digestion; it appears that most of the proteolysis of hemoglobin occurs during the trophozoite stage that the parasite endocytosed from the RBC into an acidic digestive vacuole.
L1073: The artesunate treatment regimen, proposed by the Applicant, spans the entire asexual life cycle of the *Plasmodium* parasite in the blood.
L1074: For details see Section 18.5.1.
L1075: ## 9.1.2 *In vitro* Activity
L1076: Methods for measurement of *in vitro* sensitivity of *Plasmodium* species are not standardized and limited to testing in research laboratories. The most common *Plasmodium* species used for *in vitro* testing is *P. falciparum* and the testing is based on blood stage parasites.
L1077: ## 9.1.2.1 *Exoerythrocytic forms of Plasmodium species*
L1078: Meister *et al.* (Meister et al. 2011) reported that artemisinin is not active against the liver stage parasites of *Plasmodium yoelii*. Artemisinin class of drugs are not active against the exoerythrocytic (liver) stage parasites of *Plasmodium* species (Tilley et al. 2016).
L1079: ## 9.1.2.2 *Erythrocytic forms of Plasmodium species*
L1080: The studies supporting the activity of artesunate and DHA against the different erythrocytic stages of *P. falciparum* and other *Plasmodium* species are summarized in Section 18.5.2.
L1081: Overall, the studies suggest that artesunate and DHA are active *in vitro* against the blood stage parasites of *P. falciparum*, *P. vivax*, *P. malariae*, *P. ovale* and *P. knowlesi*. The artesunate 50% inhibitory concentrations (IC_{50}) against the laboratory strains and clinical isolates were \le 2.14 ng/mL and \le 2.69 ng/mL, respectively; the DHA IC_{50} against the laboratory strains and clinical isolates were \le 6.54 ng/mL and \le 3.98 ng/mL, respectively.
L1082: The activity may vary against the different developmental forms in the blood. The mature gametocytes appear to be the least sensitive.
L1083: ## 9.1.3 *In vivo* Activity (Animal Studies)
L1084: The suppressive activity of artesunate and/or DHA was measured in rodent and nonhuman primate malaria models including severe malaria animal models. Overall, the studies support the activity of artesunate and DHA in severe malaria animal models that include rodents infected with the erythrocytic stage parasites of *P. berghei*, immunocompromised chimeric mice infected with *P. falciparum*, nonhuman primates infected with *P. coatneyi*, *P. cynomolgi*, *P. falciparum* or *P. knowlesi*. However, recrudescence occurs after discontinuation of treatment.
L1085: DHA was also effective in curing immunocompromised mice infected with a chloroquine and quinine resistant strain of *P. falciparum*; it is noted that DHA was effective in curing mice that had failed chloroquine treatment.
L1086: For details see Section 18.5.2.
L1087: ## 9.1.4 Drug Resistance
L1088: The nonclinical and clinical studies supporting a potential for development of resistance to artesunate or DHA in the blood stage parasites of *Plasmodium* species are summarized in Section 1.1.1. Briefly, studies in the *P. falciparum* humanized murine immunocompromised model suggest that delay in parasite clearance time was not associated with increased IC50 values; however, in stable resistant strains, there was an association with an increase in the IC50 values as well as mutation in the *RAD5* but not in the *Kelch13* gene.
L1089: Published clinical studies suggest that resistance to artemisinins, including artesunate and DHA, in the *P. falciparum* parasites is manifested as recrudescence and/or delay in parasite clearance time. This is thought to be due to short half-life of the artemisinin drugs. Therefore, artemisinin drugs are administered in combination with long-acting drugs such as lumefantrine.
L1090: The mechanism of resistance is likely to be multifactorial and complex with more than one genotype/phenotype associated with artemisinin resistance. A delay in parasite clearance time and/or recrudescence has been shown to be associated with alterations in some genetic regions, such as *Kelch13* and *Pfmdr1* of *P. falciparum* isolates from some of the regions. A possibility of different mechanism of resistance associated with delayed parasite clearance and recurrence (recrudescence) of parasites after initial cure exists.
L1091: There is no standardized assay to evaluate the in vitro sensitivity of the isolates to artemisinins, including artesunate and DHA. Recent studies (Witkowski et al. 2013; Klonis et al. 2013) suggest that a ring-stage survival assay may be useful for testing sensitivity to artemisinins; there was a good correlation between in vitro sensitivity and a delay in parasite clearance time. Thanh et al. (Thanh et al. 2017) reported that recrudescence was associated with a decrease in the ex vivo response to DHA as measured by the ring survival assay.
L1092: ## 9.1.5 Cross-resistance
L1093: The nonclinical studies in vitro and in animal models of malaria suggest a potential for development of cross-resistance to quinine, halofantrine, and amodiaquine (for details see Section 1.1.2). However, the clinical relevance of these findings is not known. Cross-resistance with quinine, amodiaquine, and halofantrine may be due to changes in the *Pfmdr1* gene, which encodes the P-glycoprotein pump essential for parasite detoxification.
L1094: ## 9.2 Clinical Microbiology
L1095: Parasitological assessments, for the two phase 3 trials, SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT, to support the efficacy of artesunate in patients with severe malaria as well as other supportive studies are summarized.
L1096: ## 9.2.1 Parasitological Assessments
L1097: Parasitological assessments, in the clinical trials (SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT, EDCTP/MMV07-01, Study 1263ab and Study CDC-060) include rapid diagnostic tests and/or microscopic examination of blood smears.
L1098: ## 9.2.1.1 *Study SEAQUAMAT (Dondorp et al., 2005)*
L1099: The study was conducted in Southeast Asia (Myanmar, Bangladesh, India, and Indonesia) in adults and children \ge 2 years of age. The initial malaria diagnosis was performed using a rapid Paracheck test that was confirmed by microscopic examination of blood smears. The details of the parasitological methods used were not included. Following is summarized based on published information.
L1100: **Rapid test:** The initial malaria diagnosis was performed using a histidine rich protein 2 (HRP-2) immunochromatographic Paracheck test (Orchid Biosystems, India). The Paracheck test is not cleared by the FDA. Based on the information in the test package insert, the sensitivity and specificity of the Paracheck test, compared to microscopic examination of blood smears, were 98.2% and 99.3%, respectively. Some of the endogenous (e.g., bilirubin, creatinine, triglycerides, and uric acid at high physiological levels) and exogenous (e.g., antimalarial drugs, antibiotics, and anti-inflammatory drugs at high concentrations) substances may interfere with the test performance. The test should be used for the diagnosis of *P. falciparum* malaria with signs and symptoms consistent with malaria infection. Also, the test results should be confirmed by microscopy.
L1101: The following information for the Paracheck test is from the WHO compendium (World Health Organization 2014) that summarized the performance characteristics of several malaria rapid diagnostic tests:
L1102: - For the *P. vivax* samples with 200 parasites/\muL (a low parasite burden) and 2,000-5,000 parasites/\muL (a higher parasite burden), there were no false-positive *P. falciparum* rates.
- For clean negative samples, the total *Plasmodium* species false positive rate was between 0.9 and 1.3%.
L1103: No information on *Plasmodium* species other than *P. falciparum* or *P. vivax* was included in the WHO compendium. The Paracheck test appears to be specific for *P. falciparum* species.
L1104: **Microscopic examination of blood smears:** Slide reading was performed in reference laboratories in the Shoklo Malaria Research Unit in Mae Sot, Thailand, or in the Faculty of Tropical Medicine, Mahidol University, Bangkok, Thailand. The details for the preparation of blood smears, slide reading and quality control parameters implemented were not included in the study report or the publication.
L1105: Patients with severe malaria, enrolled in the clinical trial had at least one of the 9 criteria for severe malaria that include asexual parasitemia &gt;10%, hematocrit &lt;20% + &gt;100,000
L1106: parasites/\muL (2%) or visible jaundice and &gt;100,000 parasites/\muL (2%) besides being positive by the Paracheck test.
L1107: Of the 1461 patients enrolled based on the Paracheck test, 5.4 % (n=79; 41 in the artesunate arm and 38 in the quinine arm) were smear negative. The *Plasmodium* species identified in the smear positive patients was *P. falciparum*. Hyperparasitemia (\ge10%), severe malaria, and cerebral malaria were reported in 15.7%, 71.9%, and 40.2% of the patients, respectively. Overall, artesunate was more effective than quinine in reducing mortality (for details see Section 8).
L1108: No information on post-treatment follow-up of patients for parasitemia was included.
L1109: ## 9.2.1.2 *Study AQUAMAT (Dondorp et al., 2010)*
L1110: The study was conducted in African children, \le15 years of age, with malaria. The initial malaria diagnosis was performed using a rapid Optimal test that was confirmed by microscopic examination of blood smears. The following is summarized based on published information.
L1111: **Rapid test:** The initial malaria diagnosis was based on a positive pLDH test (Optimal, Switzerland); the test package insert was not available for review. The test is not cleared by the FDA. The following information for the Optimal test is from the WHO compendium (World Health Organization 2014):
L1112: - The false-positive rates when *P. falciparum* was present at 200 parasites/\muL (a low parasite burden) or 2,000-5,000 parasites/\muL (a higher parasite burden) were 1.5% and 0.5%, respectively.
- For *P. vivax* samples with low or high parasitemia, the false positive *P. falciparum* rate was 0.0% and 20.3%, respectively.
- For clean negative samples, the total *Plasmodium species* false positive rate was 2.0%.
L1113: Overall, the results suggest that the test is not specific for *P. falciparum* and that other *Plasmodium* species may show positive findings. No information on *Plasmodium* species other than *P. falciparum* or *P. vivax* was included in the WHO compendium.
L1114: **Microscopic examination of blood smears:** Thick and thin blood smears were prepared for determining parasitemia and *Plasmodium* species at the site laboratories as well as the Bangkok reference laboratory. The details for the preparation of blood smears, slide reading and quality control parameters implemented were not included in the study report or the publication.
L1115: Of the 5425 patients enrolled, central laboratory assessments for parasite counts were not available for 109 patients; therefore, parasitemia results based on slide reading at the site laboratory were used. Slides were missing for 211 patients (112 in the artesunate arm and 99 in the quinine arm) and 42 patients (17 in the artesunate arm and 25 in the quinine arm) were blood smear negative. Of the 5214 patients with a positive rapid diagnostic test result, 99.2% were blood smear positive. Hyperparasitemia (&gt;10%) was reported in 1157 patients (21.3%;
L1116: 584 in the artesunate arm and 573 in the quinine arm) and severe malaria in 4618 (88.6%; 2280 in the artesunate arm and 2338 in the quinine arm) patients; although the number of patients with cerebral malaria was not specified, coma and convulsions were reported in 1825 (33.6%) and 1690 (31.2%) patients enrolled. Overall, artesunate was more effective than quinine in reducing mortality (for details see Section 8).
L1117: No information on post-treatment follow-up of patients for parasitemia was included.
L1118: ## 9.2.1.3 *Study EDCTP/MMV07-01 (Kremsner et al., 2012)*
L1119: The study was conducted in African children with *P. falciparum* severe malaria; patients were randomized to either 3-dose (0, 24, and 48 hours) or 5-dose (0, 12, 24, 48, and 72 hours) IV artesunate groups. Parasitological assessments performed include qualitative and quantitative examination of blood smears for enrolling patients with \ge 5000 *P. falciparum* parasites/\mu\text{L} and severe malaria as well as evaluating efficacy (\ge 99\% parasite clearance).
L1120: Thick and thin blood smears for parasitological assessments were prepared at 6-hourly interval after treatment as well as prior to the next dose of treatment for at least 72 hours following the first dose of study drug or until 2 consecutive negative smears were recorded within the last 24-hour period. The Giemsa stained blood smears were examined, at the site laboratories, by 2 qualified microscopists who were blinded to each other's results and to the treatment status of the patient. Non-concordance among microscopy interpretations was referred to a 3rd expert microscopist for final slide determination; additionally, 5% of the concordant results between Microscopist 1 and 2 were re-examined by the third expert microscopist, however, these results were not recorded on the CRFs. Non-concordance was defined as disagreement about any of the following:
L1121: - Presence of asexual forms of *Plasmodia* on blood smear.
- Species of *Plasmodium* present on blood smear.
- Density of parasitemia within a factor of 2, if either microscopist determined parasitemia to be &gt;100 asexual parasites/\mu\text{L}. If both microscopists agreed to the *Plasmodium* species as well as the presence of parasitemia, with both counts &lt;100 parasites/\mu\text{L}, the results were considered concordant even if the reported parasitemia were not within 2-fold of each other.
- Parasitemia within an absolute range of 50,000 parasites/\mu\text{L}. For example, a count by one microscopist of 200,000/\mu\text{L} and a count by the 2nd Microscopist of 300,000 parasites/\mu\text{L} was not considered concordant.
L1122: It is noted that the criteria for non-concordance/concordance between &gt;100 and &lt;50,000 parasites/\mu\text{L} were not specified.
L1123: The parasitological and clinical follow-up examinations were performed on Days 7, 14, 21, and 28. As soon as the patient was able to receive oral medication and no signs and symptoms of severe malaria were present (\sim 50 hours after the start of artesunate treatment), a single dose
L1124: of sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine (in Gabon) or artemether-lumefantrine (in Malawi) was to be administered to ensure parasitological cure. Recurrent infection within 28 days was treated with artemether-lumefantrine.
L1125: Parasitemia at baseline ranged between 869 and 1870624 parasites/\muL (0.02-37.41%, respectively). Within 24 hours of treatment initiation, 99% reduction in parasitemia (PC_{99}) was achieved in 85% of the patients in the 5 dose group compared to 78% in the 3 dose groups; the median parasite clearance time for PC_{99} ranged between 24 and 18 hours in both 5-dose and 3-dose group.
L1126: Parasite clearance (PC_{100}; based on two consecutive negative blood smears), was achieved in 84% of the patients in the 5 dose group and 80% in the 3-dose group; the median time to parasite clearance was 36 hours in both groups.
L1127: The median time to fever clearance was 12 hours in both groups.
L1128: Of the 16 patients with recurrence of parasitemia at Day 28, 7 were in the 5-dose group compared to 9 in the low dose group. It is noted that the recurrence rate is based on initial parental artesunate treatment followed by oral treatment with either a combination of sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine or artemether-lumefantrine.
L1129: For details see Section 8.
L1130: ## 9.2.1.4 *Study 1263ab*
L1131: Study 1263ab was conducted in Thailand and Kenya in patients with acute, uncomplicated *P. falciparum* malaria with parasitemia of \ge 5,000/\muL. Artesunate was administered, IV, at doses of 1.2, 2.4, and 4.8 mg/kg for 3 days followed by mefloquine or Malarone. For determining parasitemia, two thick and thin malaria smears were prepared at different timepoint and Giemsa stained at the site laboratories by trained microscopists. Slides were assessed for the quality of slide preparation and staining prior to slide interpretation; poorly prepared slides were not used for assessment. Slides were examined by two qualified microscopists who were blinded to each other's results and to the treatment status of the patient. A total of 200 oil immersion fields were examined before a blood smear was considered negative. A non-concordance of the two microscopists in the results of the initial smear (positive/negative; identification of *Plasmodium* species) resulted in an exclusion of the subject.
L1132: Parasite densities were calculated based on a count of parasites per 200 WBCs in a thick film (if \ge 10 parasites seen), 500 WBC in thick film (if 9 or less parasites seen at 200 high power fields) or per 5000 RBCs in thin film (if parasites &gt; 500/200 WBCs). The final parasite density was calculated as the arithmetic mean of the density determined by the two microscopists. Additionally, the individual microscopist's evaluation of the parasite density had to be less than 2-fold of each other or the resultant parasite density was considered non-concordant and
L1133: referred to a third certified expert microscopist as a referee. The 3rd expert microscopist reviewed both smears and determined a final interpretation. Additionally, if either of the two primary microscopists calculated a parasite density of \le 100 parasites/\mu\text{L}, the slides were automatically referred to the certified expert microscopist for the final density determination. Overall, the malaria slide interpretation was managed by a slide coordinator, who was not a microscopist, to assess concordance between the first two readers and to ensure that blinding was maintained.
L1134: The Applicant states that for quality assurance, 5% of the concordant results were re-examined by the referee expert microscopist; however, these readings and parasite density evaluation were not considered as the final result.
L1135: A majority of the patients were infected with *P. falciparum* and treated with either 1.2, 2.4, 4.8 mg/kg of IV artesunate for 3 days followed by mefloquine or Malarone. The primary objective was to compare parasite clearance time. Overall, 90% parasite clearance was achieved by 48 hours in all subjects and 100% clearance by 72 hours range 12-164 hours (median 32-40 hours) in patients treated with 2.4 mg/kg artesunate for 3 days followed by mefloquine or Malarone. Recrudescence was reported on Day 4, in one patient (Subject ID (b) (6) treated with 2.4 mg/kg artesunate for 3 days (bid on Day 0, followed by once daily for 2 days).
L1136: One of the exclusion criteria was exclusion of subjects with “mixed malaria infection on admission by malaria smear (i.e., any parasite morphologies not consistent with a *P. falciparum* mono-infection)”; however, two patients with mixed infection due to *P. falciparum* and *P. ovale* from Kenya were enrolled. One patient was treated with 1.2 mg/kg and another with 4.8 mg/kg dose of IV artesunate. In both patients, the *P. falciparum* parasitemia was reduced by \ge 42-fold on Day 2 and became aparasitemic by Day 3 of treatment; for *P. ovale*, no parasites were detected on Day 2 (Table 9-1).
L1137: **Table 9-1: Study 1263ab - Follow-up of *P. falciparum* and *P. ovale* parasitemia in 2 patients with mixed infection**

L1140: ## 9.2.1.5 Study CDC-060
L1141: The Study R-CDC-060 was a treatment protocol performed in the United States. Microscopic examination of blood smears was performed at the local hospital clinical laboratory for a majority of the patients; specimens from some of the patients were sent to reference
L1142: laboratories. For one patient (Subject (b) (6) *P. falciparum* species was confirmed by a PCR method at the CDC. The details of the methods used were not provided.
L1143: Of the 102 patients, 87 patients were evaluable based on microscopic confirmation of *P. falciparum* and met the criteria of need for IV treatment; parasitemia was \ge 5\% in 65 patients. *Plasmodium* species other than *P. falciparum* were identified in 5 patients (3 with *P. vivax* and 2 with *P. ovale*).
L1144: Deaths were reported in 5 (5.7%) patients with *P. falciparum* (baseline parasitemia ranged between 12% and 90%) and one patient with *P. vivax* (baseline parasitemia 0.7%). Two patients with *P. ovale* infection survived (Table 9-2). The Applicant states that patient with *P. vivax* who died was “essentially moribund at presentation”.

L1156: ## 9.2.1.6 Published studies
L1157: Some of the published studies reported efficacy of artesunate in patients with severe malaria due to *Plasmodium* species other than *P. falciparum*; these were mostly case series and are summarized in Table 9-4. Overall, the studies suggest that artesunate should be effective in improving survival of patients with severe malaria due to the asexual blood stage parasites of *P. vivax* (n=58), *P. knowlesi* (n=39) and *P. ovale* (n=1) as well as mixed infections.

L1167: ## **Reviewer comments:**
L1168: *Overall, the studies suggest that artesunate maybe effective in improving in treating patients with severe malaria to different Plasmodium species.*
L1169: *Post-treatment follow-up of patients for parasitemia was not assessed in the two Phase 3 studies (SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT). However, post-treatment follow-up for parasitemia was conducted in patients with severe malaria in Study EDCTP/MMV07-01 that showed that 99% parasite clearance was achieved by 24 hours in 85% of the patients in the 5 dose group compared to 78% in the 3 dose groups; the median parasite clearance time for 99% reduction ranged between 24 and 18 hours in both 5-dose and 3-dose group. A study in patients with uncomplicated malaria (Study 1263ab) showed that 100% parasite clearance was achieved between 12-164 hours (median 32-40 hours) in patients treated with 2.4 mg/kg artesunate for 3 days followed by mefloquine or Malarone.*
L1170: *Additional information on parasite clearance time, based on published studies is summarized in Table 9-5. Overall, the studies support that the parasite clearance time after treatment with artesunate is shorter (48-72 hours) compared to other antimalarials such as quinine (5 to 6 days).*
L1171: **Table 9-5: Time to parasite clearance in patients with severe malaria**

Uganda

PCT was based on serial blood smears performed 6-hourly after start of treatment until 6 h post first negative blood smear when parasite clearance was achieved.	Median 2 days; none of the patients had parasitemia on Day 3.
United Kingdom – African ethnicity; traveled to West Africa
(Eder et al. 2012)	22 severe malaria patients were administered IV artesunate (2.4 mg/kg at 0 and 12 h, followed by every 24 h treatment until smears were negative for trophozoites; patients were then treated with Malarone. Time of examination of blood smears and criteria for parasite clearance to determine PCT were not specified.

L1176: *Recrudescence can occur after discontinuation of artesunate treatment. Patients with P. vivax or P. ovale infection should be treated with an anti-malarial that is active against the liver stage parasites of Plasmodium species.*
L1177: *A postmarketing surveillance study should be conducted to evaluate the potential development of resistance to artesunate and DHA as obtained from ongoing resistance monitoring programs on antimalarials collected by the CDC as well as international consortia and organizations (e.g., World Health Organization).*
L1178: ## 9.2.2 Interpretive Criteria
L1179: The Applicant has not requested any interpretive criteria in the labeling. This is appropriate as the tests to measure in vitro sensitivity of parasites of the *Plasmodium* species are not standardized and their use is limited to research laboratories.
L1180: ## 10 Review of Safety
L1181: ## 10.1 Safety Review Approach
L1182: In this NDA, evidence for clinical safety of IV artesunate was based primarily on one randomized open label trial (SEAQUAMAT) and a retrospective analysis of data collected under an expanded access IND (CDC-060). Additional supportive evidence for safety was provided by two phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers (Studies 1128 and 1142), and two phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria (Studies 1148 and 1263ab). Studies 1128, 1142, 1168 and 1263ab were sponsored and conducted by the U.S. Army and &gt; 50% of CDC-060 was funded by the U.S. Army. The U.S. Army transferred rights for these studies to Amivas prior to the NDA submission. Study protocols, patient level datasets, annotated CRFs, and CSR were submitted for each of these studies. SEAQUAMAT was not conducted by the Applicant or the U.S. Army.
L1183: SEAQUAMAT was conducted in Southeast Asia and evaluated 730 patients aged 2 to 87 years who received IV artesunate, compared with 730 patients who received at least one dose of IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2005). SEAQUAMAT included limited safety follow up as data were collected only during hospitalization, until death or discharge. Laboratory data were reported only for the admission timepoint. The primary endpoint was in-hospital mortality and secondary endpoints were neurological sequelae and recovery times (times to eat, speak sit and discharge), and development of severe complications. For adverse event analyses in SEAQUAMAT, the safety population in this section included all patients who received a least one dose of study medication.15
L1184: Study CDC-060 presented data retrospectively collected from the first 102 patients who received IV artesunate under IND 76725 for the treatment of severe malaria in the United States. Datasets collected information on mortality, adverse events, and laboratory data until death or discharge, with limited additional information reported in patient narratives.
L1185: The phase 1 studies 1128 and 1142 in healthy volunteers followed patients until Day 15 and routine laboratory evaluations including hematology panels (Hb, Hct, platelets, reticulocytes, and neutrophils), bilirubin, AST, blood urea nitrogen, and creatinine, as well as ECG datasets (machine read). The phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria included laboratory evaluations at Day 7 if previously abnormal (WRAIR Study 1168) and at Days 7, 14 and 28 (WRAIR 1263ab).
L1186: Supportive information for safety was provided by AQUAMAT, a randomized open label trial of severe malaria in Africa, which studied 2715 pediatric patients less than 15 years old
L1187: 15 In SEAQUAMAT, one patient (Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) randomized to quinine did not receive study medication. Applicant's safety analyses used the ITT population.
L1188: administered IV artesunate compared with 2713 who received IV quinine (Dondorp et al. 2010). In this review, AQUAMAT data and analyses were derived solely from the publication as the patient level dataset was not provided in the NDA.16 The primary endpoint was in-hospital mortality, and secondary outcomes were similar to SEAQUAMAT and included the incidence of neurological sequelae and recovery times (i.e., times to eat, speak, sit, and discharge). One key difference with SEAQUAMAT was that after 11% of patients were enrolled in AQUAMAT, the study protocol was amended to assess patients who had not fully recovered at discharge at 28 days and at regular intervals to 12 months or until full recovery. Limited information on these assessments, however, were included in the publication.
L1189: Supportive safety information was also provided by Study EDCTP-MMV07-01, a study in pediatric patients 6 months to 10 years with severe malaria, comparing five-dose regimen of IV artesunate 2.4 mg/kg/dose at 0, 12, 24, 48, and 72 hours with a three-dose regimen of 4.0 mg/kg/dose once a day for three days, for the same total dose of 12 mg/kg. The application included the protocol, CRF, and CSR; datasets including PK data were not available.
L1190: Consultations from the QT-IRT team and Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health were summarized in Section 10.4.9 and Section 10.9.2, respectively.
L1191: The safety review focused primarily on the safety of IV artesunate at the proposed dose of 2.4 mg/kg/dose at 0, 12, 24 hours when administered to patients with severe malaria. While most patients in studies submitted to the NDA had malaria due to *P. falciparum*, review of safety included subjects with other malaria species (CDC-060). Adverse events of special interest such as hematologic, renal, and hepatic were analyzed across the clinical development program if the data were collected, see Table 10-1 below. In addition, see Section 6 for the clinical pharmacology reviewer's assessment of Phase 1 studies.

L1198: See Table 7-1: Clinical Trials Relevant to this NDA for summary of clinical studies reviewed for safety as well as efficacy.
L1199: In lieu of an Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) and an Integrated Summary of Efficacy (ISE) the Applicant submitted a meta-analysis entitled “Meta-analysis of Intravenous and Intramuscular Artesunate in Complicated or Severe Malaria” conducted in 2013 by Sigma-Tau. The Amivas ISS/ISE meta-analysis focused primarily on the Cochrane meta-analysis which conducted its original search in November 2005 and an updated search in November 2010 identifying a total of eight randomized controlled trials17 (Sinclair et al. 2012). The ISS/ISE meta-analysis included an additional 25 publications through 2012, adding other controlled trials of IV artesunate versus quinine, retrospective cases of travelers, additional data on safety and efficacy in subgroups, uncontrolled or add-on trials, and studies with a control group other than quinine. SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT, and EDCTP-MMV07-01 were analyzed as part of the ISS/ISE meta-analysis but CDC-060, published in Twomey et al. 2015, was not included.

L1226: ## 10.2.2 Relevant characteristics of the safety population
L1227: The safety population for this application included all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of IV artesunate. Studies for which patient-level data were submitted included subjects 18 months to 85 years of age who received IV artesunate. In SEAQUAMAT, among subjects who were treated with IV artesunate 161 (22.1%) were 2 - &lt;18 years old and 17 (2.3%) were 65 years of age or older. CDC-060 included patients 18 months to 72 years of age: 12 (11.8%) were &lt;18 years old and 3 (2.9%) were 65 years of age and older.
L1228: Regarding the supportive studies, EDCTP-MMV07-01 included subjects as young as 6 months and up to 10 years of age. The lower age range for AQUAMAT is uncertain as the datasets were not submitted in the application and the publication (Dondorp 2010) included only interquartile range for the enrolled pediatric patients. (Dondorp et al. 2010)
L1229: Demographic characteristics are summarized in Section 8 (Statistical and Clinical Evaluation) for SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT and EDCTP-MMV07-01, and in Section 18.6 for CDC-060 and Studies 1168, 1263ab, 1128 and 1142. See Section 12 for a discussion of the safety database in pediatric subjects.
L1230: In studies of severe malaria, SEAQUAMAT enrolled patients in Myanmar, Bangladesh, India, and Indonesia. The highest enrolling sites for SEAQUAMAT were in Myanmar (40%), Bangladesh (30.4%) and Indonesia (19.6%). CDC-060 enrolled patients in U.S. hospitals who acquired malaria while traveling outside the U.S. According to the study publication, AQUAMAT enrolled patients at 11 centers in 9 African countries including Mozambique, The Gambia, Ghana, Kenya, Tanzania, Nigeria, Uganda, Rwanda, and Democratic Republic of the Congo. Study sites in EDCTP-MMV07-01 were in Gabon and Malawi.
L1231: In the clinical trials SEAQUAMAT, CDC-060, EDCTP-MMV07-01 and phase 1 and 2 studies, patients received IV artesunate; in AQUAMAT, 933 of 2712 (34.4%) subjects received artesunate intramuscularly.
L1232: For SEAQUAMAT, datasets included selected clinical events and laboratory evaluations on admission as well as selected clinical events and adverse event (AE) data after admission and at discharge. CDC-060 datasets included AEs, labs and vital signs through death or discharge. For those subjects with longer hospital stays, data were generally collected through day 7, with data for a few patients with prolonged courses reported through days 8-9. Phase 2 study 1168 of uncomplicated malaria included AEs, safety labs and vital signs through day 3, or day 7 if abnormal, and Study 1263 included AEs, labs and vital signs through day 28. Phase 1 studies 1128 and 1142 of healthy volunteers (with placebo controls) followed patients for AEs, labs, ECG, and vital signs through day 15.
L1233: ## 10.2.3 Adequacy of the safety database
L1234: The number of patients exposed to IV artesunate in the studies submitted to the NDA appeared adequate to perform a safety evaluation. The numbers of patients in the greater than 65 years or in other demographic categories were insufficient to conduct subgroup analyses. (Dondorp et al. 2010)
L1235: In SEAQUAMAT, AEs, per se were not collected by the Wellcome Trust, the sponsor of the study. Clinical events and complications associated with severe malaria disease were captured along with a free text field on admission, after admission, and upon discharge. The Applicant coded legacy data obtained from the Wellcome Trust according to MedDRA preferred terms.
L1236: Overall, the safety assessment for this application was constrained by the limited safety data collected in key trial SEAQUAMAT and supportive trial AQUAMAT. Both studies focused primarily on efficacy, limiting safety observations to the clinical events associated with malaria, e.g., coma, anemia, etc., and the duration of in-patient hospitalization, until death or discharge. AQUAMAT included neurological follow up until day 28 but information on outcomes on day 28 was limited. CDC-060 included safety observations until death or discharge for most subjects but contained some prolonged follow up information for select patients, for example, pregnancy outcome. No patient narratives were available for SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT. Narratives for patient deaths and SAEs were available for CDC-060 and EDCTP-MMV07-01.
L1237: The lack of patient-level data for AQUAMAT prevented robust cross-study comparisons with SEAQUAMAT and prevented ascertainment of the lower age range of enrolled pediatric patients, as the AQUAMAT publication (Dondorp 2010) reported only the median and interquartile range. The AQUAMAT publication did not contain an analysis of adverse events by route of administration (IV versus IM).
L1238: While a clinical study report was provided for EDCTP-MMV07-01, the report lacked Appendix 12 which included the statistical analysis plan; patient data listings for discontinued patients, compliance data, adverse event listings, and individual laboratory measurements; and CRFs for deaths, SAEs, and withdrawals; the Applicant was unable to obtain this information from the study authors.
L1239: ## 10.3 Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L1240: ## Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L1241: The Applicant submitted patient-level data, annotated CRFs and clinical study reports for SEAQUAMAT and CDC-060 (severe malaria), Phase 2 Studies in uncomplicated malaria 1168 and 1263ab, and Phase 1 studies 1128 and 1142. No issues were identified regarding data integrity and submission quality. A clinical study report was submitted for EDCTP-MMV07-01 but no patient-level datasets were included, limiting the analyses that could be performed. The
L1242: Applicant included statements of Good Clinical Practice for each trial.
L1243: ## Categorization of Adverse Events
L1244: AEs were coded using MedDRA Version 22.0 (SEAQUAMAT), Version 14.0 (CDC-060), Version 10.0 (Study 1263ab), Version 8.1 (Study 1168), version 12.0 (Study 1142), and Version 8.0 (Study 1128). SAEs were not recorded for SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT. In CDC-060, SAEs were not prospectively recorded but in the retrospective data abstraction, Grade 4 AEs were coded as SAEs. SAEs were recorded in EDCTP-MMV07-01. One SAE was recorded in Phase 2 Study 1168 with no SAEs reported in the remaining phase 1 and 2 studies.
L1245: Symptoms and sequelae of malarial disease were included as AEs in the phase 3 studies of severe malaria and phase 2 trials of uncomplicated malaria.
L1246: ## Routine Clinical Tests
L1247: Phase 1 studies (1128 and 1142), phase 2 trials (1168 and 1263 ab), EDCTP-MMV07-01 and CDC-060 included routine laboratory testing of blood chemistries, liver profiles, and hematology panels. Datasets for SEAQUAMAT did not include routine blood tests following admission; AQUAMAT publication included limited information on anemia, hypoglycemia and renal function (BUN).
L1248: Vital signs were reported for phase 1 and 2 studies as well as EDCTP-MMV07-0 and CDC-060. SEAQUAMAT datasets included only diastolic blood pressure and patient weight. Height was not recorded in Phase 2 or 3 studies.
L1249: ECGs were not performed in phase 2 or 3 trials but were performed in phase 1 studies 1128 and 1142. ECGs were evaluated by a cardiologist at the clinical site and were also digitized and analyzed by Cardiocore. In addition to the reports from Cardiocore, the Applicant submitted machine-read ECG data for these two clinical studies. A summary of the findings by the FDA QT-IRT can be found in Section 10.4.9.

L1263: ## 10.4.1 Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
L1264: Differences among the clinical trials in dose regimens (dose administered, timing, and length of therapy), indications (severe versus uncomplicated malaria), study populations, available supportive care, and adverse event data collection, made cross-study analysis of TEAEs difficult. Moreover, the Applicant generally did not distinguish between the clinical symptoms due to malaria and treatment emergent adverse events following artesunate therapy. Treatment emergent adverse events reported in each study are summarized below.

L1270: **Reviewer comments:** In SEAQUAMAT, AEs that occurred more frequently in recipients of artesunate compared to those receiving quinine included dialysis (8.9% vs 7.3%, respectively), hemoglobinuria (6.7% vs 4.4%, respectively), and jaundice (2.3% vs 1.9%, respectively). The FDA analysis of renal failure requiring dialysis included those patients with CRFs recording the pre-specified term of dialysis as well as free-text fields that included the terms hemodialysis, peritoneal dialysis, intraperitoneal dialysis (IPD) and peritoneal dialysis (PD).

L1292: In studies of healthy volunteers (Studies 1128 and 1142), the most common AEs, occurring in ≥ 5% of artesunate subjects, included dysgeusia, dizziness, headache, nausea, vomiting, fatigue, myalgia, and anemia. L1293: One subject in Study 1128 who received artesunate at 8.0 mg/kg experienced urticaria which was considered moderate in severity and resolved after receiving Benadryl.

L1310: **Reviewer comments:** Dysgeusia appears to be dose related in both studies of normal volunteers. Neutropenia (ANC &lt; 1.0 \times 10^6/L) was observed in one patient in Study 1142 at the highest dose regimen of 8 mg/kg/dose for three days.
L1311: ## 10.4.2 Deaths
L1312: Note: A detailed analysis of mortality in SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT, and EDCTP-MMV07-01, can be found in Section 8.1, Review of Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy.
L1313: Cross-study comparisons of mortality between studies are complicated by the differences in populations, study design, dose regimens, indications, available supportive care, and other factors. All deaths that occurred in the clinical development program were consistent with outcomes associated with the underlying clinical illness (severe malaria) and were generally associated with the severity of symptoms at presentation and known risk factors. In SEAQUAMAT, for example, higher mortality was observed in those who met the definition of severe malaria, had hyperparasitemia, cerebral malaria, and age \ge 65 years. (See Table 10-14.)
L1314: There were no deaths in the Phase 1 Studies 1128 and 1142 or in the Phase 2 studies 1168 and 1263ab.
L1315: The table below summarizes the overall mortality in studies of severe malaria.
L1316: **Table 10-14: Overall Mortality in Studies of Severe Malaria (Safety Population)**
L1317: Study	Population	Deaths in Artesunate Recipients
n/N (%)

SEAQUAMAT (FDA analysis)2	2-87 years (SE Asia)	96/730 (13.2%)
AQUAMAT (Dondorp 2010)	< 15 years (Africa)	230/2712 (8.5%)
EDCTP-MMV07-013 (Kremsner 2012 and CSR)	6 months – 10 years (Africa)	2/194 (1.0 %)

L1328: In EDCTP-MMV07-01, two patients (2/194 or 1%) died. Available details from submission are provided below:
L1329: - Subject (b) (6) (Group B, 3-dose regimen of IV artesunate) was a 4-year old female enrolled in Gabon who was admitted with vomiting, fever and fatigue for 2 days. On admission the hemoglobin was 4.2 g/dL and parasite count was 92,700 *P. falciparum*. On day 1 following admission the patient developed coma (Blantyre score 3/5) with respiratory distress, tachypnea, thrombocytopenia and hemoglobinuria and she was started on ampicillin, ceftriaxone, and gentamicin for UTI and sepsis. Following the third
L1330: dose of study medication, the parasite count was 8000 but the patient deteriorated further with tonic-clonic seizures and progression to deep coma. Despite clearance of *P. falciparum* in her blood, the patient expired on the second day of admission.
L1331: - Subject (b) (6) (Group B, 3-dose regimen of IV artesunate) was a 34-month old male in Gabon with a four-day history of fever and vomiting, and one day history of seizures and coma (Blantyre score 2/5 on admission). His hemoglobin on admission was 4.9 g/dL and he had a parasite count of 224,000 *P. falciparum*. Ten hours after the first dose of artesunate he experienced prolonged seizures associated with respiratory distress and a Blantyre coma score of 1/5. He received a second dose of IV artesunate and was transferred to the ICU. He died later that day.
L1332: Both subjects who died in EDCTP-MMV07-01 presented with symptoms consistent with severe malaria and hyperparasitemia. Deaths were likely associated with complications of malaria.
L1333: AQUAMAT reported an imbalance of deaths prior to initiating therapy, with 22 deaths in patients randomized to receive quinine and 6 deaths in patients randomized to receive artesunate. As the two groups appeared to be balanced with respect to baseline characteristics, the authors speculated the higher number of deaths in patients assigned to quinine was caused by a delay in administration due of parenterally-administered quinine by controlled infusion compared with bolus administration of IV artesunate. In SEAQUAMAT, only one patient (assigned to quinine) died before receiving study drug.
L1334: **Reviewer comments:** Observed mortality was higher in in SEAQUAMAT than in AQUAMAT. The authors of AQUAMAT propose several reasons for this finding. African children generally have some background immunity to malaria which facilitates recovery. In addition, severe malaria may be over diagnosed in African children, a population with a relatively high incidence of sepsis and with a high background incidence of *P. falciparum* parasitemia. The older population enrolled in SEAQUAMAT and differences in available medical care in the two studies may have played a role in the higher incidence of deaths in SEAQUAMAT. The lower mortality observed in EDCTP-MMV07-01 may be due to a more expansive case definition for severe malaria which required only that symptoms were severe enough to require hospitalization.
L1335: ## 10.4.3 Serious Adverse Events
L1336: Due to the significant clinical illness associated with severe malaria, morbidity associated with malarial disease was difficult to distinguish from adverse effects that may have been associated with treatment. For example, in SEAQUAMAT, subjects had a high incidence of malaria complications on admission, including convulsions (49% in artesunate recipients), coma (39%), shock (11%), and severe anemia (6%), among other manifestations. Before receiving study treatment, 23.5% of patients in CDC-060 had impaired consciousness, 23.5% had circulatory collapse or shock, and 34.3% had acidosis. Patients enrolled in AQUAMAT had a high baseline incidence of coma (32% in artesunate recipients), convulsions (30%), severe anemia (30%), and

L1387: ## 10.4.8 Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1388: Phase 1 Studies 1128 and 1142 included ECG monitoring and cardiac safety analyses.
L1389: In addition to the Cardiocore Cardiac Safety Reports submitted in the CSRs for studies 1128 and 1142 (1128 CSR 16.1.12.10 and 1142 CSR 16.1.12.2, respectively), the Applicant submitted machine-read ECGs which were reviewed by the QT-IRT.
L1390: In phase 2 Study 1168 of uncomplicated malaria, 20% of patients receiving IV artesunate at 2.4 mg/kg/daily for 3 days experienced bradycardia and tachycardia each, but these findings were not observed in Study 1263ab at any dose or reported in SEAQUAMAT. In Study CDC-060, 6 patients reported a total of 7 cardiac-related adverse events; in only one subject was the AE considered possibly related to IV artesunate (subject # [REDACTED] (b) (6) who experience Grade 2 cardiomyopathy beginning on study day 3). CDC-060 reported two patients with cardiac arrhythmias, one patient with a mild bradycardia and one with supraventricular tachycardia (SVT). The patient with SVT (# [REDACTED] (b) (6) had a past medical history significant for significant cardiovascular disease, including congestive heart failure, mitral valve regurgitation, hypertension and coronary artery disease.
L1391: ## 10.4.9 QT Interval
L1392: In SEAQUAMAT, one subject (# [REDACTED] (b) (6) - [REDACTED] (b) (6) a 55-year old male assigned on artesunate arm experienced an AE of QT prolongation and bundle branch block at the time of admission.
L1393: The FDA QT-IRT reviewed data from Studies 1128 and 1142, the proposed product labeling and
L1394: the Applicant's review of clinical pharmacology and cardiac safety, see QT-IRT review dated 2/18/2020.
L1395: The data were evaluated using exposure-response analysis. The QT-IRT concluded that there was no significant QTc prolongation effect of artesunate.
L1396: The findings were supported by a time analysis using non-parametric method with the primary analysis based on exposure-response analysis and a categorical analysis. The Applicant used digitized data for their analyses while the QT-IRT used the dataset derived from single paper ECGs with automated ECG measurements. No significant outliers were found in the Applicant's and FDA's analyses for QTcF &gt; 500 msec. The FDA analysis identified one subject in the artesunate 8.0 mg/kg group who experienced \DeltaQTcF greater than 60 msec, while the Applicant's analysis has not identified any subjects in either study who experienced \DeltaQTcF greater than 60 msec.
L1397: The QT-IRT noted that the clinical trials submitted with this application included a placebo control but did not utilize a positive control such as moxifloxacin for assay sensitivity. Per the QT-IRT, the lack of a positive control was mitigated by using ECG data performed using automated ECG measurements that would eliminate the potential for measurement bias. The QT-IRT recommended the following wording for the product labeling:
L1398: ## **Cardiac Electrophysiology**
L1399: At the maximum approved recommended dose, artesunate does not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.

L1402: ## 10.5 Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1403: ## 10.5.1 Neurological Events
L1404: Neurological complications of severe malaria due to *P. falciparum* include coma, seizures and altered mental status and may result in long-term sequelae, particularly after recovery from cerebral malaria. While first described in adults, residual neurological impairment has also been described in children with sequelae that included hemiplegia, cortical blindness, aphasia, and ataxia. (Brewster, Kwiatkowski, and White 1990).
L1405: Artemisinins have been associated with neurotoxicity in animals, with the manifestations dependent upon the particular drug, route of administration, half-life and duration of exposure. (Efferth and Kaina 2010) Artesunate may pose less risk for neurotoxicity given its rapid clearance with shorter central nervous system exposure. (Medhi et al. 2009) Human studies of
L1406: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1407: artemisinins evaluating neurological outcomes have shown equivocal results, with some clinical studies of artemether, for example, observing prolonged recovery times from coma in patients treated with artemether compared with quinine (Hien et al. 1996; Tran et al. 1996). These findings were not confirmed by a meta-analysis that included over 1900 patients. (Stepniewska 2001) A Cochrane meta-analysis of eight randomized controlled trials (including SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT) compared artesunate (intravenous, intramuscular, or rectal) with quinine (intravenous or intramuscular) for the treatment of adults (n=1664) and children (n=5765) with severe malaria. This meta-analysis showed a reduced risk of death in both adults and children but found an increased incidence of neurological sequelae in children at the time of hospital discharge. The review characterized the majority of sequelae as transient with no significant difference observed on follow up at 28 days following discharge. (Sinclair et al. 2012)
L1408: Under the SEAQUAMAT protocol, patients were evaluated on discharge with a check box for specified neurological adverse events including the following: cortical blindness, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia, loss of balance, extrapyramidal rigidity, hemiplegia/pareisis, neuropsychiatric conditions (like psychosis), and included a separate check box for other significant neurological sequelae with a free text field to fill in details.20 Patients were not followed further after discharge. The following table describes the neurological deficits observed at the time of discharge. Specific findings from the free text field for “other significant neurological sequelae” results are also provided.
