L1418: **Reviewer Comments:** When evaluating the prespecified neurological terms, 7 patients (1%) receiving artesunate compared with 3 patients (0.4%) receiving quinine experienced neurological sequelae. When data from the free text field were included, 12 (1.6%) artesunate patients and 7 (1%) quinine patients had a specific neurological sequela noted on discharge. Two subjects had multiple events.
L1419: Review of neurological adverse events in AQUAMAT was limited to data reported in the publication. According to the publication, neurological status was initially assessed only at hospital discharge. The protocol was amended after 11% of patients had been enrolled to include neurological exam 28 days following discharge. Dondorp 2010 reported that 99 artesunate and 71 quinine recipients had neurological sequelae at discharge; denominators were not provided. According to the publication, 34 artesunate and 27 quinine recipients had neurological sequelae at day 28. Of these sequelae, 28 artesunate recipients compared with 20 quinine recipients had sequelae described as severe; these included severe motor impairment, cortical blindness, and severe speech or hearing impairment.
L1420: In patients who had cerebral malaria, the overall incidence of persistent neurological sequelae at 28 days was 3.4% (24 of 706) in artesunate recipients and 3.1% (23 of 737) in quinine recipients. The incidence of severe neurological sequelae was 2.3% in regardless of whether the patients received artesunate (17 of 706) or quinine (17 of 737). See table below for presentation of data from the publication.
L1421: **Figure 10-3: AQUAMAT – Neuro Sequelae at discharge and 28 days in children with severe falciparum malaria (Per Protocol)**
L1422: **Figure 4: Neurological sequelae at discharge and after 28 days (range 3-8 weeks) in children with severe falciparum malaria**
L1423: *Some patients had severe impairment in more than one domain.
L1424: Source: Dondorp 2010
L1425: **Reviewer Comments:** At the time of hospital discharge, a higher incidence of neurological sequelae was observed in both SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT trials. In both trials artesunate recipients demonstrated lower in-hospital mortality and the higher incidence of residual neurological deficits in children has been postulated to be due to the greater survival of patients with more severe disease rather than direct toxicity from artesunate. The absence of patient level data from AQUAMAT precluded FDA's analysis of neurological outcomes from this trial.
L1426: The Applicant's ISS/ISE meta-analysis relied heavily on studies included in the Cochrane meta-analysis. Few of these trials included safety endpoints. Other than SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT, only one study reported on neurological sequelae but the sample size of 37 subject in each treatment was too small to demonstrate a significant difference. (Cao et al. 1997)
L1427: ## 10.5.2 Delayed Hemolysis and Anemia
L1428: Following the recommendation by WHO in 2010 for use of IV artesunate as the first line treatment of severe malaria, investigators began reporting the occurrence of delayed anemia 1 to 3 weeks following artesunate therapy, first in travelers and subsequently in patients from endemic regions. (Zoller et al. 2011) (Kreeftmeijer-Vegter et al. 2012) (Briggs and Arguin 2013) (Rolling et al. 2014) While transient anemia following artesunate therapy has been ascribed to reversible bone marrow suppression (Finaurini et al. 2012) and intravascular hemolysis (Dondorp et al. 2010), the delayed hemolytic anemia observed following artesunate therapy (post-artemisinin delayed hemolysis or PADH) appears to be a separate condition with a different pathogenesis, as follows.
L1429: The rapid action of artesunate kills malaria parasites which are then removed by the spleen, resulting in “pitted” once-infected erythrocytes that remain in circulation. (Arguin 2014) By examining hematologic parameters in travelers who received IV artesunate for severe malaria, Jaureguiberry found a correlation between the pitted RBCs and the occurrence of PADH, with the once-infected RBCs being removed by the spleen 7-21 days after IV artesunate therapy. Jaureguiberry proposed a case definition of 10% drop in hemoglobin associated with haptoglobin &lt;0.1 g/L and either an increase in LDH to &gt;390 IU/L or 10% rise &gt;7 days after treatment initiation with IV artesunate. (Jaureguiberry et al. 2014)
L1430: While anemia was a frequently observed adverse event in studies submitted in this application, the occurrence of PADH could not be confirmed in this review because of the limited duration of follow up. Anemia following IV artesunate was observed in phase 1 studies of healthy volunteers (Studies 1128 and 1142), with 10% of subjects receiving single dose regimen experiencing anemia compared with 30% of subjects receiving multiple dose regimens. Anemia was also observed in phase 2 studies in uncomplicated malaria, with 13 of 30 (43%) of subjects in Study 1168 (2.4 mg/kg/dose) experiencing anemia and 23 of 100 (23%) of patients in Study 1263ab pooled IV artesunate regimens (1.2 mg/kg dose to 4.8 mg/kg/dose), without a dose response relationship. SEAQUAMAT recorded severe anemia after admission, defined as hematocrit &lt;20, as occurring in 2.7% of patients receiving artesunate versus 2.9% of patients receiving quinine; however, no information on transfusions was provided in the datasets. In study CDC-060, 65.7% of subjects had anemia and transfusions were administered in 49 patients (48%), but because follow up was limited to the period of hospitalization, it is not clear whether any of these transfusions were due to PADH. The AQUAMAT publication reports that transfusions were given to 1487 of 2712 (55%) of patients randomized to artesunate and 1495 of 2713 (55%) of patients randomized to quinine; however, details on timing were not provided. EDCTP-MMV07-01 provided descriptions of transfusions in some serious adverse event narratives but complete data and timing of transfusions for all patients were not provided. Three subjects had SAE of anemia diagnosed 7 to 14 days following admission and received transfusions.
L1431: Two case reports have described an immune-mediated hemolysis following IV artesunate associated with a direct antiglobulin (Coomb's) test. (Raffray et al. 2014) (Chamnanchanunt 2017) These patients experienced a drop in hemoglobin 12-14 days following IV artesunate therapy, had a positive Coomb's test, and improved with oral steroid therapy. The patients with immune-mediated hemolysis appear to be subset of patients with delayed hemolysis after IV artesunate.
L1432: ## 10.5.3 Neutropenia
L1433: Dose-dependent neutropenia has been described in clinical studies of patients treated with oral artesunate for *P. falciparum* malaria, with 5 of 26 patients (19%) treated with 6 mg/kg/day for 7 days experiencing neutropenia (defined in this study as absolute neutrophil count or ANC less than 1000 per mm3) and geometric mean ANCs in this high dose group remaining significantly lower than in patients receiving 2 mg/kg/day or 4 mg/kg/day on both day 6 and day 14. (Bethell et al. 2010) In a study of uncomplicated malaria, the risk of neutropenia 14 days after starting treatment with oral artesunate was higher in in HIV-infected than in HIV-uninfected children and 16% of HIV-infected children experienced severe neutropenia (ANC&lt;750 cells/mm3). (Gasasira et al. 2008)
L1434: During the clinical development of Artesunate for Injection, different studies used different definitions of neutropenia as a TEAE, hampering an overall assessment of this adverse event. In phase 1 Study 1128 for example, no patients were identified as having neutropenia as a TEAE, but 2 subjects (one receiving artesunate at 4.0 mg/kg/dose and one receiving 8.0 mg/kg dose) demonstrated a drop in neutrophil count to &lt; 1.5 x 106/L (one placebo recipient experienced a similar drop.) In phase 1 Study 1142, one subject receiving Artesunate for Injection at 8.0 mg/kg/dose (20% of total artesunate recipients) was coded as having neutropenia as a TEAE with an ANC &lt;1.0 x 106/L but an additional 2 subjects receiving 8.0 mg/kg/dose experienced neutrophil counts &lt;1.5 x 106/L (as did one placebo recipient.)
L1435: During phase 2 studies of uncomplicated malaria, severe neutropenia was defined as neutrophil count &lt;0.75 x 106/L. Severe neutropenia occurred in 4 of 30 (13.3%) of patients in Study 1168 and in 9 of 100 (9%) patients in Study 1263ab, primarily in patients who received doses greater than 2.4 mg/kg/day, but there was no strict dose-response relationship. In Study 1263ab for example, 4 of 25 patients in the 1.2 mg/kg/dose group, 0 of 50 in the 2.4 mg/kg/dose group and 5 of 25 patients in the 4.8 mg/kg/dose experienced severe neutropenia. While 5% of patients experienced neutropenia in CDC-060, only one of 102 subjects was identified as having severe neutropenia. An analysis of SEAQUAMAT data did not find any TEAEs of neutropenia, and neutropenia was not reported as an AE in the AQUAMAT publication. A transient decrease in neutrophils was observed in Study EDCTP-MMV07-01 in both the 3-dose and 5-dose treatment groups.
L1436: In summary, despite the variable coding of neutropenia as a TEAE and inconsistent assignment of severity, certain trends were apparent upon analysis of neutrophil counts for different dose
L1437: regimens. A transient, reversible drop in neutrophil count was observed in the phase 1 - 2 studies with a tendency for a greater incidence and severity at higher dose regimens.
L1438: ## 10.5.4 Hypersensitivity
L1439: Hypersensitivity reactions including anaphylaxis have been reported with artemisinin drugs, including Coartem (artemether and lumefantrine) ("Coartem (artemether/lumefantrine) Tablets [package insert]") and oral artesunate. (Leonardi et al. 2001) Anaphylaxis following IV artesunate has been described in the literature. (Mohapatra et al. 2009), (Dube et al. 2012) (Neki 2015)
L1440: Within the phase 1 clinical development of Artesunate for Injection, one case of urticaria was reported in a healthy volunteer (Study 1142) who received single dose of artesunate at 8.0 mg/kg. The rash, which was considered moderate in severity, started on the day of dosing and resolved with 24 hours following receipt of diphenhydramine. In phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria, there was one case of bullous erythema multiforme (preferred term Stevens-Johnson Syndrome) in a patient with uncomplicated malaria who also received Malarone (see Section 18.6.3 for more details).
L1441: ## 10.5.5 Reduced Reticulocyte Count
L1442: Artesunate and other artemisinins have been associated with reversible bone marrow depression. (Finaurini et al. 2012) A Thai study in healthy volunteers demonstrated a decrease in hemoglobin 7 days after administration of DHA dosed orally at 300 mg daily for two days compared to oral artesunate therapy. (Kongpatanakul et al. 2009) This finding was accompanied by a reduction in reticulocytes with nadir on day 5 (DHA 47% reduction in reticulocyte count, artesunate 75% reduction), with the authors proposing transient bone marrow suppression as a direct effect of artemisinin therapy. A reversible reduction in reticulocyte counts in healthy volunteers was previously described in healthy volunteers at total doses of IV artesunate up to 16.88 mg/kg. (Barradell and Fitton 1995) A drop in reticulocyte counts by day 5 treatment has also been described in a clinical study of patients with severe malaria receiving IV artesunate relative to patients receiving quinine. (Cao et al. 1997)
L1443: Phase 1 and 2 studies of IV artesunate submitted with this application showed a transient but consistent drop in reticulocyte count. Study 1128, a decrease in reticulocyte count with a return to baseline by Day 8 was observed which appeared to be dose dependent. In the pooled artesunate groups, a decrease in reticulocyte count below the lower limit of normal was observed in 11 of 30 (36.7%) subjects. A similar pattern with respect to reticulocytes was observed in Study 1142, see Section 18.6.5, Laboratory Findings. Phase 2 Study 1168 in patients with uncomplicated malaria also found a dose-related drop in reticulocyte count, reaching its nadir by day 4 and returning to baseline by day 7 for most patients. (Li et al. 2014) A similar dip in reticulocytes was seen in phase 2 Study 1263ab, reaching lowest levels on reticulocytes on
L1444: Day 3 in patients receiving the two higher dose regimens. This pattern was seen in both the absolute and corrected reticulocyte counts.
L1445: ## 10.6 Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
L1446: Not applicable

L1467: ## 10.9 Additional Safety Explorations
L1468: ## 10.9.1 Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1469: Not applicable
L1470: ## 10.9.2 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1471: In malaria endemic regions, pregnant women have a higher prevalence of malaria and an increased risk of severe malaria; these risks extend through the early postpartum period. (Diagne et al. 2000) Malaria during pregnancy is associated with increased maternal morbidity and mortality and adverse fetal outcomes including spontaneous abortions, still births, preterm delivery, low birth weight, and congenital infection. (World Health Organization 2019) (Fried and Duffy 2017)
L1472: CDC Guidelines for Treatment of Malaria in the US (December 2019) recommend IV artesunate for all patients with severe malaria, including pregnant women in all trimesters. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2019) WHO Guidelines for the Treatment of Malaria (World Health Organization 2015) also recommend parenteral artesunate as the treatment of choice for severe malaria in all trimesters, noting that treatment must not be delayed. In the event that artesunate is unavailable, IM artemether is recommended; if this is unavailable, parenteral quinine should be started immediately until artesunate becomes available. Limited data have been published regarding the safety or artemisinins during breastfeeding.
L1473: Embryotoxicity and fetal malformations have been reported in published nonclinical studies of artemisinin drugs administered during pregnancy at or below the equivalent therapeutic doses in humans, with timing of effects seen early in gestation; see discussion in Section 5.5.4, Reproductive and Development Toxicology. The toxicity of artemisinins, which has been observed in several animal species, has varied depending on the artemisinin drug and the route of administration. Clinical studies evaluating the use of oral artesunate and other artemisinin drugs during pregnancy, including randomized controlled clinical trials, observational studies and cohort studies have not identified adverse maternal or fetal outcomes but limited clinical data are available evaluating the safety of IV artesunate during the first trimester. Oral artesunate and other drugs in the artemisinin class have had widespread clinical use over the past several decades, however, and have been administered to scores of pregnant women without reports of significant congenital malformations or other adverse fetal or maternal outcomes.
L1474: Two studies in this application, SEAQUAMAT and CDC-060, included pregnant patients with severe malaria and were analyzed for available data. In both studies, patients were generally followed only until hospital discharge so limited data were available on outcomes.

L1498: ## ***CDC-060 Pregnancy Outcomes***
L1499: Three pregnant patients, all in the third trimester, were enrolled in the CDC IND protocol during the time period covered by CDC-060. Two patients delivered prior to receiving IV artesunate. The two remaining patients (35 and 36-37 weeks respectively) recovered from severe malaria without sequelae and delivered healthy infants by vaginal delivery within one month of initiating treatment.
L1500: ## ***Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health Review***
L1501: The Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health conducted a review of available data to assist the review team in providing guidance in product labeling. DPMH concluded that although artesunate and artemisinin derivatives are teratogens in animals, available data in humans have not identified adverse maternal or fetal outcomes, including congenital malformations, miscarriage, and still birth. DPMH and the review team collaborated in drafting language in the prescribing information for Artesunate for Injection that conveyed the risks associated to both mother and fetus of delaying or withholding treatment for severe malaria; noted that extensive experience with oral artesunate and other artemisinin class drugs in pregnant women has not identified a risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes; and described the observation in animals of embryo-lethality and fetal malformations when artesunate was administered early in gestation but noted that the clinical relevance was uncertain.
L1502: ## **10.9.3 Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound**
L1503: No abuse or withdrawal potential has been described in the literature with IV artesunate.
L1504: The application reports one case of a fatal overdose from the literature in a 5 year old child following the administration of rectal artesunate at a dose of 200 mg BID or 88 mg/kg/day for 4 days. (Campos 2008) The patient became obtunded and developed seizures, jaundice and gastrointestinal hemorrhage on the fifth day after starting treatment. Prior to death on the 12th day after the start of artesunate therapy, the patient also experienced hypotension, hypoglycemia, pancytopenia and multiorgan system failure.
L1505: ## **10.9.4 Dosing in Patients \ge 100 kg**
L1506: The proposed artesunate dosing is 2.4 mg/kg/dose regardless of the age or weight of the patient, with no maximum dose recommended. No data were found in the literature regarding safety of artesunate in patients &gt; 100 kg. In SEAQUAMAT only one recipient of quinine had a weight greater than 100 kg. CDC-060 included 14 of 102 patients (13.7%) with weight greater than 100 kg. See table below for analysis of TEAE by weight \le100 kg and &gt;100 kg.

L1511: ## 10.10 Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1512: ## *Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience*
L1513: Parenteral artesunate has been studied extensively in multiple clinical trials in various malaria-endemic regions and has been recommended by WHO as first line therapy for severe malaria since 2010.
L1514: The Applicant submitted an integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) and an Integrated Summary of Efficacy (ISE) that consisted of a meta-analysis entitled “Meta-analysis of Intravenous and Intramuscular Artesunate in Complicated or Severe Malaria” conducted in 2013 by Sigma-Tau. The Amivas ISS/ISE meta-analysis focused primarily on the Cochrane meta-analysis which conducted its original search in November 2005 and an updated search in November 2010 identifying a total of eight randomized controlled trials21 (Sinclair et al. 2012). The ISS/ISE meta-analysis included an additional 25 publications through 2012, adding added other controlled trials of IV artesunate versus quinine, retrospective cases of travelers, additional data on safety and efficacy in subgroups, uncontrolled or add-on trials, and studies with a control group other than quinine. SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT, and EDCTP-MMV07-01 were analyzed as part of the
L1515: 21 The Cochrane meta-analysis of IV artesunate for severe malaria included the following studies: (Anh 1989), (Anh, Binh, and Kim 1995), (Hien et al. 1992), (Cao et al. 1997), (Newton et al. 2003), (Dondorp et al. 2005), (Dondorp et al. 2010), and (Eltahir et al. 2010).
L1516: ISS/ISE meta-analysis but CDC-060, published in Twomey et al. 2015, was not included.
L1517: Adverse reactions identified in the ISS/ISE that were identified with higher frequency in recipients of artesunate compared with recipients of quinine were blackwater fever and neurological disorders, similar to FDA's findings in this application. Additional adverse reactions included in the WHO/Guilin artesunate SPC include elevated serum amylase and pancreatitis, tinnitus with associated hearing loss, and insomnia.
L1518: ## 10.11 Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1519: The safety database included 832 adults and children with severe malaria treated with IV artesunate in a randomized active-controlled trial in South East Asia (SEAQUAMAT). Supportive evidence for safety was provided by a retrospective analysis of data from an uncontrolled open label study in the US (CDC-060), published studies of severe malaria in Africa consisting of 2712 pediatric patients (less than 15 years of age) receiving parenteral artesunate enrolled in a randomized active-controlled trial (AQUAMAT) and 194 children 6 months to 10 years of age in randomized controlled trial comparing two dose regimens of IV artesunate (EDCTP-MMV-07-01), and Phase 2 studies in patients with uncomplicated malaria. Both SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT compared IV artesunate to IV quinine (the previous standard of care) and demonstrated superior in-hospital mortality and a lower incidence of treatment-emergent adverse events overall in patients receiving IV artesunate.
L1520: IV artesunate was generally well tolerated. Most common adverse reactions in SEAQUAMAT in patients receiving IV artesunate included renal failure requiring dialysis, hemoglobinuria, and jaundice. In CDC-060 the most frequent adverse events were anemia, elevation of transaminases, thrombocytopenia, hyperbilirubinemia, and acute renal failure. In SEAQUAMAT, at the time of hospital discharge, the incidence of neurological sequelae in artesunate recipients was higher compared to patients receiving quinine. Similarly, in AQUAMAT, incidence of neurological findings at discharge and on subsequent follow up was higher in the artesunate arm as compared to quinine arm; residual neurological sequelae at day 28 included severe motor impairment, cortical blindness, and speech or hearing impairment.
L1521: Serious adverse events were not reported in SEAQUAMAT. In CDC-060, Grade 4 TEAEs were reported in lieu of SAEs; the most common were anemia, thrombocytopenia, acute respiratory distress syndrome, and hyperbilirubinemia. One subject in a phase 2 study of uncomplicated malaria experienced an SAE of Stevens-Johnson Syndrome which was associated with concomitant Malarone therapy.
L1522: Post-artesunate delayed hemolysis, characterized by a drop in hemoglobin of 10% occurring at least 7 days or more after start of artesunate therapy and associated with laboratory markers of hemolysis, has been described in the literature both in patients from malaria-endemic countries and in travelers from endemic regions. Anemia and hemoglobinuria were among the most common TEAEs reported in pivotal and supportive trials, and transfusions were reported
L1523: in some studies during the typical timeframe for PADH; these observations point to the possible occurrence of PADH but limited follow up of patients in studies submitted in this application precluded confirmation of this adverse reaction.
L1524: Severe hypersensitivity reactions to IV artesunate such as anaphylaxis have been reported in the literature but were not observed in studies submitted in this application. Within the phase 1 clinical development of IV artesunate, one case of urticaria moderate in severity was reported in a healthy volunteer (Study 1128) who received single 8.0 mg/kg dose artesunate. Transient neutropenia and decrease in reticulocyte count have been reported in published studies of IV artesunate. These hematologic findings were also observed in phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers and phase 2 studies of uncomplicated malaria submitted in this application.
L1525: Limitations of the safety assessments in this application included the restricted period of follow up (until hospital discharge) in SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT as well as the uncontrolled US trial CDC-060. In addition, SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT focused largely on the occurrence of clinical events associated with malaria, did not record adverse events' severity, and had limited data on longer-term outcome. Moreover, it was not possible to discern whether higher incidence for some adverse events (e.g. neurological impairment at discharge) reported with IV artesunate compared to quinine may have resulted from improved survival of patients who presented with more severe disease. While study CDC-060 provides information on clinical outcomes and adverse reactions in patients treated in the US, the lack of a comparator arm makes it difficult to ascribe causality to the adverse reactions observed. Most of the reported adverse events in this study may be attributable to severe malaria, further complicating interpretation of safety as it was not possible to determine which AEs would have occurred more commonly with Artesunate for Injection than with the comparator, if one was used.
L1526: Based on limited findings from phase 1 studies, no clinically significant changes in QT were observed in patients receiving IV artesunate.
L1527: Overall, IV artesunate administered at a dose of 2.4 mg/kg/dose at 0, 12, 24 hours and once daily until able to tolerate oral antimalarial therapy was shown to have an acceptable safety profile with the risks outweighed by improved survival in patients with severe malaria.
L1528: ## 10.12 Conclusions and Recommendations
L1529: Evidence for the safety of IV artesunate in the treatment of severe malaria was derived from results of the randomized controlled trial SEAQUAMAT conducted in South East Asia and from a retrospective analysis of the data from the open-label uncontrolled study of IV artesunate use under an expanded access IND in the US (CDC-060). Published studies AQUAMAT, a randomized controlled trial, and EDCTP-MMV07-01, a controlled study comparing two dose regimens, that evaluated IV artesunate for severe malaria in African children, provided supportive safety information. Additional supportive evidence for safety was drawn from phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers and phase 2 studies of uncomplicated malaria. As summarized
L1530: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1531: in Section 8, IV artesunate was superior to quinine, the previous standard of care, for the primary endpoint of mortality and displayed an acceptable safety profile for the treatment of adults and children with severe malaria.
L1532: There were no safety signals in the application that would preclude approval.

L1534: No Advisory Committee meeting was held for this application as there were no issues that needed input from external experts.
L1535: ## 12 Pediatrics
L1536: Due to the Orphan Drug Designation, this application is exempt from the requirement to conduct pediatric studies under the Pediatric Research Equity Act; this was communicated to the U.S. Army on March 28, 2019; ownership of Orphan Drug Designation for Artesunate for Injection was subsequently transferred to Amivas.
L1537: Young children are more likely than adults to suffer severe symptoms of malaria, e.g., seizures and concomitant invasive bacterial infections, and experience complications of malaria, e.g., neurological sequelae. (World Health Organization 2015) Children also bear a heavy burden of disease with 86% of malarial deaths globally occurring in children less than 5 years of age, underscoring the need for effective antimalarial therapies in this population. (World Health Organization 2012)
L1538: Following the publication of SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT, the WHO updated its guidelines to recommend intravenous artesunate in preference to quinine for the treatment of severe malaria in children, recommending a dose of 2.4 mg/kg/dose on admission, at 12 hours and 24 hours, then once a day until able to tolerate oral therapy. (World Health Organization 2010) In 2015 WHO revised its pediatric dosage recommendations for children less than 20 kg to 3.0 mg/kg/dose based on its review of pharmacokinetic data which projected under exposure in younger children at the 2.4 mg/kg/dose regimen (World Health Organization 2015) (Hendriksen et al. 2013)
L1539: The Applicant has proposed a dose regimen of Artesunate for Injection at 2.4 mg/kg/dose at 0, 12, 24, (b) (4) hours for adults and children; no lower age limit for the pediatric indication was proposed. Additional analyses were performed by the review team on the effectiveness and safety of intravenous artesunate, and Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacometrics review team evaluated the dose regimen and lower age range for the pediatric indication, see discussion below.
L1540: This application included pediatric data from five studies: SEAQUAMAT, AQUAMAT, EDCTP-MMV-0701 and CDC-060 for the treatment of severe malaria and Study 1263ab for uncomplicated malaria. Characteristics of the pediatric database are presented in the table below.

L1593: **Reviewer comments:** In a review of the literature, case reports published between 2010 and 2019 identified 10 infants less than 6 months of age who received IV artesunate for severe malaria. Age range was newborn (preterm infant 31 weeks gestational age, 700 g) to 0.4 years of age; 7 infants had malaria due to *P. falciparum*, 3 infants were infected with *P. vivax*. All 10 patients were considered cured; in patients where parasite clearance was described, clearance occurred within 1 to 3 days. While safety was not the primary focus of these reports, no safety issues with IV artesunate were identified.

L1599: **Reviewer comments:** According to MMWR Surveillance Summaries, up to 4.8% of children with severe malaria in the US were less than 5 years of age; however, the specific age groups and number of children receiving IV artesunate were not reported. Of the Surveillance Summaries with information on infants &lt; 6 months, none of the reports cited IV artesunate as the drug administered although some were missing this information and patients may have received IV artesunate.
L1600: To evaluate the proposed dose regimen in pediatric subjects &lt; and \ge 20 kg, the Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacometrics review team employed PK modeling and simulation with data from published studies (for details see Pharmacometrics review in Section 18.4). PK modeling and simulation found that pediatric patients younger than 6 months of age had comparable or higher predicted PK steady-state AUC of DHA when compared with older children or adults at the recommended 2.4 mg/kg dose regimen of Artesunate for Injection. The Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacometrics review team recommended no dose adjustment for pediatric patients regardless of age or bodyweight. In addition, review of the literature for use of intravenous artesunate for severe malaria in pediatric patients less than 6 months of age identified no significant safety concerns.
L1601: ## Conclusion
L1602: The safety and efficacy of Artesunate for Injection for treatment of severe malaria in children is supported by evidence from adequate and well-controlled studies in pediatric patients 6 months of age or older; support of efficacy and safety in patients less than 6 months was provided by PK modeling and simulation and published studies. The Applicant has agreed to a postmarketing commitment to evaluate safety, tolerability, and PK of IV artesunate in pediatric patients with severe malaria from 0 to 12 months of age receiving 2.4 mg/kg Artesunate for Injection, with the majority of pediatric patients to be 6 months of age and younger.

L1605: ## Prescribing information
L1606: Table 13-1: Summary of Significant Labeling Changes
L1607: Summary of Significant Labeling Changes (High level changes and not
Section	Proposed Labeling	Approved Labeling
Indications and Usage	
Artesunate for Injection is an antimalarial indicated for the initial treatment of severe malaria in adult and pediatric patients. Treatment of severe malaria with artesunate should always be followed by a complete treatment course of an appropriate oral antimalarial regimen.
Deleted
Limitations of Use
Artesunate for Injection does not treat the hypnozoite liver stage forms of Plasmodium and will therefore not prevent relapses of malaria due to Plasmodium vivax or Plasmodium ovale. Concomitant therapy with an antimalarial agent such as an 8-aminoquinoline drug is necessary for the treatment of severe malaria due to P. vivax or P. ovale.

Dosage and Administration	
The recommended dosage of Artesunate for Injection is 2.4 mg/kg administered intravenously at 0 hours, 12 hours, and 24 hours, and thereafter, administered once daily until the patient is able to tolerate oral antimalarial therapy.
Administer diluted Artesunate for Injection intravenously as a slow

Section	Proposed Labeling	Approved Labeling
Use in Specific Populations	8.1 Pregnancy	Standard PLLR language was added on background risk of major birth defects, fetal loss, or other adverse outcomes.
Human data	Additional data added regarding first trimester use of intravenous artesunate in humans.
Animal data	Added findings from animal reproductive studies conducted using clinically relevant route.
8.2 Lactation Risk Summary	Edits made to reflect the finding of DHA in human milk and the lack of data on the effects of artesunate on the breastfed infant or milk production.
(b) (4)
8.4 Pediatric Use	Regarding pediatric patients less than 6 months of age, text added to reflect that a PK extrapolation approach using modeling and simulation indicated comparable or higher than predicted PK steady-state AUC of DHA between this age group and older children or adults at the recommended 2.4 mg/kg dose regimen.
8.5 Geriatric Use	Revised to reflect that clinical studies did not include sufficient numbers of patients 65 years and older to determine whether they responded differently than younger subjects.
8.6 Renal Impairment	Edited to note that in patients with renal or hepatic impairment, no specific PK studies have been carried out and no specific dose adjustments are needed.
8.7 Hepatic Impairment	Edited to note that in patients with renal or hepatic impairment, no specific PK studies have been carried out and no specific dose adjustments are needed.

Form FDA 3454 was signed by the Applicant certifying that based on information from the sponsor or participating clinical investigators, the listed clinical investigators did not participate in any financial arrangement, had no proprietary interest in the product or significant equity interest, and had not received significant payments of other sorts. The Applicant noted that these studies were sponsored by either the Wellcome Trust (SEAQUAMAT and AQUAMAT) or Medicines for Malaria Venture (EDCTP-MMV07-01) and provided a list of trial investigators for each study. Form FDA 3454 was submitted certifying that as the Applicant who submitted studies sponsored by a firm other than the Applicant, the listed clinical investigators did not participate in any financial arrangement with the sponsor whereby the value of compensation for conducting the study could be affected by the outcome of the study, have no proprietary or significant equity interest, and had not received significant payments of other sorts.

L1662: ## 18.2 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1663: ## *Safety Pharmacology*
L1664: **Behavioral and neural toxicity of the artemisinin antimalarial, arteether, but not artesunate and artelinate, in rats.** *Pharmacology Biochemistry and Behavior.* 2000;67(1):37-44. Adult male rats (Sprague-Dawley) were administered equivalent molar concentrations of arteether (25 mg/kg in sesame oil; n=6), artesunate (31 mg/kg in sodium carbonate; n=6), and artelinate (36 mg/kg in saline; n=6) via IM injection daily for 7 days, with performance on an auditory discrimination task and neurohistology conducted. Control animals received IM injection of sodium carbonate (n=6).
L1665: No observable effects on any performance measures of the auditory discrimination task were observed following artesunate or artelinate treatment compared with vehicle control. In contrast, significant performance deficits (percent correct and stimulus response) were observed in rats receiving arteether. These behavioral effects were greater during the latter portion of the treatment regimen and during post-administration testing sessions. No apparent histopathological changes were observed in brainstem nuclei of rats treated with artesunate or artelinate compared with control. Brainstem nuclei of rats treated with arteether showed evidence of chromatolysis, pyknosis, and gliosis.
L1666: ## *General Toxicity*
L1667: ## Dose Range Finding Study of Injectable Artelinate and Artesunate in Rats/SR01-1
L1668: ## Key findings:
L1669: - The maximum tolerated dose (MTD) for single IV administration of artesunate in rats was between 200 and 400 mg/kg; the MTD for multiple IV doses (up to 7 days) was observed to be \ge 75 mg/kg.
- Artesunate-related hematologic changes following 7 days of repeated IV dosing included increased MCV, reticulocyte % and count, neutrophil and monocyte values and decreased RBC, HGB, and HCT values. There were no macroscopic or microscopic lesions observed in hindbrain from animals at any dose tested.
L1670: (b) (4)

L1672: GLP compliance: No

Phase 2: 0, 9.4, 18.8, 37.5, or 75 mg/kg daily, for 7 days
Phase 3: 0 or 150 mg/kg daily, for 7 days
L1676: Route of administration:
L1677: Intravenous

L1684: ## **Observations and Results:**
L1685: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	Phase 1 (HD): All animals were found dead on the day after dosing
Phase 3 (HD): 1 animal (female) was found dead on Day 6
Clinical Signs	Phase 1 (HD): 3 of 4 animals displayed transient ataxia within 30 minutes after dosing.
Phase 2 (HD): females displayed diarrhea, hunched posture and wet skin/fur on 2 or more days between Days 3 and 8.
Body Weights	Up to 10% decreased body weight gain and/or weight loss \ge 75 mg/kg (F) and at 150 mg/kg (M)
Hematology	HD (150 mg/kg; F only): increased MCV (108%), reticulocyte % and count (2 to 4-fold), neutrophil and monocyte values and decreased RBC (79%), HGB, and HCT values.
Clinical Chemistry	HD (150 mg/kg; F only): decreased albumin (76%) and A/G values (49%).
Gross Pathology	None
Histopathology
Hindbrain only	None for AS.
AE: 1 out of 3 females displayed degenerative lesions in trapezoid nucleus.
L1686: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose.
L1687: *Reviewer comment: The study also included an arm in each phase in which rats were dosed with artelinate in lysine. Only findings from artesunate-treated animals and the positive control (arteether) are discussed. The study was not conducted in compliance with GLP regulations, but notes that all study procedures, data recording, and reporting were performed in a manner consistent with the standard of GLPs.*

L1690: ## **Histological studies of the toxicity of artesunate on the testes in Wistar rats** (Biology and Medicine. 2010. Vol 2(2):49-56)
L1691: Key Study Findings: Oral administration of artesunate for 7 days in rats caused mild sloughing of germ cells from the seminiferous tubule basement membrane, with a dose-related reduction in the population of germ cells.

L1700: ## **Modulatory Effects of Ascorbic Acid on Spermatotoxicity of Artesunate in Rats** (Nigerian Medical Practitioner 53 (2008) 62-65)
L1701: ## Key Study Findings
L1702: - Artesunate significantly reduced sperm count, percent sperm motility and serum testosterone levels in rats following a 7-day repeated oral administration. The artesunate-induced effects were ameliorated with concomitant treatment with ascorbic acid. There were no noted changes in testicular histology other than an apparent reduction in gonial cell layers of the seminiferous epithelium.

L1711: ## Embryo-Fetal Development
L1712: ## **Histopathological Investigation of the Embryonic Effect of SB-714702 (Artesunate) in Cynomolgus Monkeys/Study No. CD2006/01492/00 (No. SBL097-004)**
L1713: This study was conducted to investigate the effects of artesunate on surviving embryos from dams in the 30 mg/kg treatment group in Study CD2006/00794/00. Histopathological examinations were done on the three live embryos collected from dams in the 30 mg/kg group on Days 26, 32, and 36 of gestation and compared against an untreated Day 30 gestation embryo obtained from a historical collection.
L1714: GLP compliance: The EFD study in cynomolgus monkeys from which the embryos were removed (Study No. CD2006/00744/00) was conducted under GLP conditions. The histopathological examination of the embryos was not performed in compliance with GLP regulations.
L1715: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1716: - An overall relative reduction in erythroblasts was observed in embryos from artesunate-treated dams compared with the control embryo.
- Embryos from artesunate-treated dams had atrial chamber enlargement with thin atrial and ventricular walls compared to the Day 30 gestation embryo from the historical collection; the embryo collected on Day 36 gestation displayed an enlarged ventricular space and evidence of pyknotic nuclei in hepatocytes.
L1717: *Reviewer comment: The presence of cardiovascular malformations in surviving embryos of the high dose group are consistent with those reported in other species (i.e., rat and rabbit) following oral artesunate exposure. The reduction in erythroblasts in embryos from artesunate-treated dams should be interpreted with caution given the lack of gestation day-matched control embryos allowing for a direct comparison.*
L1718: ## **Artesunate: In Vitro Effects on Embryonic and Fetal Blood Cells of Cynomolgus Monkeys/Study No. CD2006/00795/00 (Study No. 105188)**
L1719: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1720: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1721: - An approximate 6 to 14% nucleated RBCs was observed in untreated fetal blood collected on Day 50 of gestation, whereas no nucleated RBCs were noted in untreated fetal blood collected on Day 100 of gestation.
- Fetal blood cells were more sensitive than maternal blood cells to the cytotoxic effects of artesunate and DHA on Day 50 of gestation compared with Day 100 of gestation, with up to 25% greater fetal blood cell death observed following exposure to 50 mcM artesunate and 20 mcM DHA.

L1751: Reviewer comment: Based on the lack of artesunate-induced embryofetal toxicity when administered between days 27-33 of gestation with the same dose causing embryonic death between days 30 and 55 of gestation when administration began on day 20 of gestation (Study No. CD2006/00794/00), it can be inferred that the critical window of embryotoxicity following oral administration in cynomolgus monkey falls between gestation day 20 and 27.
L1752: **SB-714702 (Artesunate): Oral Critical Window Embryo-Fetal Development Study in Rats/Study No. CD2004/00133/00 (Study No. G03312)**(b) (4)
L1753: Key Study Findings
L1754: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1755: - The critical window for artesunate-induced embryolethality in rats following oral administration of 10 mg/kg was determined to be between Days 9 and 12 post-coitum (pc). A higher dose of 30 mg/kg administered orally later during gestation (Days 14-17 pc) induced nearly total implantation loss.
- Cardiac and skeletal malformations in developing fetuses occurred when artesunate was orally administered between Days 9 and 10 pc. The malformations included ventricular septal and subclavian artery defects, curved/misshapen scapulae and long bones, and brachyury.
L1756: (b) (4)
L1757: Conducting laboratory and location:
L1758: GLP compliance:
L1759: ## Methods
L1760: Dose and frequency of dosing:	10 and 30 mg/kg/day
Route of administration:	Oral (gavage)
Formulation/Vehicle:	1% methyl cellulose
Species/Strain:	Rat/CD™ (Sprague-Dawley)IGSBR
Number/Sex/Group:	10-20 pregnant females/group
Satellite groups:	None
Study design:	Animals were administered 10 mg/kg/day artesunate on Days 9-10, 11, 12, 13, or 14-17 pc or 30 mg/kg/day on Days 14-17 pc. Control animals were treated from Days 9-17 pc. C-sections were performed on Day 21 pc and uterine contents examined.
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:	No. Mating dates were determined to be incorrect for some females, causing some animals in the 10 mg/kg group to be dosed on Days 8-9, 9, 10-11, and 14 pc and some animals in the 30 mg/kg group to be dosed on Days 13-16 pc. Impact was considered to be minimal given the objective of the study (i.e., determine critical windows for developmental toxicity of artesunate) was met.
L1761: ## **Observations and Results**
L1762: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	None
Clinical Signs	None
Body Weights	None
L1763: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1764: Necropsy findings
Cesarean Section Data	LD: Total litter resorption was observed in 2 of 21 litters after dosing on Day 11 pc, 1 of 19 litters after dosing on Day 12 pc, and 1 partially resorbed litter in each group after dosing on Days 9-10 (of 13 litters) or Day 10 pc (of 18 litters). No increased resorptions were observed in animals dosed on 8-9, 9, 13, or 14-17 pc.
HD: Total litter resorption in 90% of litters with remaining litters displaying significant increase in postimplantation loss (up to 86% of implants resorbed).
Necropsy findings
Offspring	LD: cardiovascular defects (ventricular septal defects and retroesophageal right subclavian arteries in 6 fetuses from 2 litters) and skeletal malformations (curved, shortened and/or misshapen scapula, radius, femur, humerus, or tibia, unossified or incomplete ossification of pelvic bones in 6-7 fetuses from 3 litters) were observed following treatment from Days 9-10 pc. Short tails were also observed in fetuses with other treatment-related effects. Variations in liver shape (absence of deep fissure and/or ligamentum teres hepatic) occurred in 8 fetuses from 1 litter with other artesunate-related findings dosed on Day 10 pc. No treatment-related abnormalities were observed after dosing on Days 8-9, 9, 13 or 14-17 pc.
HD: Observed skeletal changes in the small number of surviving fetuses following administration between days 13 and 16 pc were similar to those observed in LD treatment group
L1765: ## **SB-714702 (Artesunate): Oral Embryo-Fetal Development Study During Critical Development Windows in Rats/Study No. CD2004/00353/00 (b) (4) Study No. G04015**
L1766: ## Key Study Findings
L1767: - A critical window for artesunate-induced embryolethality and developmental toxicity was determined to be days 10 to 14 pc. A single treatment with 17 mg/kg artesunate on Days 10 or 14 pc induced significant postimplantation loss (up to 60%). In surviving fetuses, treatment-related cardiovascular, liver, and skeletal malformations were noted, with greater incidence occurring following treatment on Day 10 pc.
- No increases in artesunate-related embryolethality or fetal abnormalities were noted following a single dose of 17 mg/kg on Day 9 pc or 30 mg/kg on Day 16 pc.

L1788: ## Observations and Results
L1789: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	None
Clinical Signs	None
Body Weights	None
Necropsy findings
Cesarean Section Data	LD: Significant increases in embryolethality occurred following a single dose administered on Day 10 or 14 pc (61% and 40%, respectively). Eight litters were 100% resorbed following administration on Day 10 pc, with 2 additional litters displaying 5 or more resorptions. Following administration on Day 14 pc, 2 litters were 100% resorbed with 4 additional litters having 4 or more resorptions. A 13-14% decrease in fetal weight was noted following treatment on Day 10 or 14 pc (13-14% and 5-6%, respectively). No increased embryolethality was observed after single dose on Day 10 pc.
HD: No increased embryolethality was observed following administration on Day 16 pc.
Necropsy findings
Offspring	LD: Treatment-related fetal abnormalities occurred following administration on Days 10 or 14 pc, but not Day 9 pc. Following treatment on Day 10 pc, fetal abnormalities included brachyury (10 fetuses in 3 litters), ventricular septal defects (9 of 71 fetuses from 3 litters), retroesophageal right subclavian vessels (4 fetuses from 2 litters), and skeletal defects and abnormalities such as curved scapula and femurs, short femur, knobby ribs and variation and shape and incomplete ossification of sternebrae (4 fetuses from 2 litters). Several fetuses also displayed liver variations (absence of deep fissure of median lobe and associated absent ligamentum teres hepatis). Following treatment on Day 14 pc, fewer fetal malformations were observed compared with Day 10 pc (i.e., 3 fetuses from 3 litters displaying VSD with no great vessel or liver abnormalities noted). Fetuses from this group did display more skeletal variations (i.e., increased incidence of knobby ribs) and unique skeletal variations including incomplete ossification of cervical vertebral arches (15 of 121 fetuses from 6 litter) and incompletely ossified skull bones.
HD: No treatment-related fetal abnormalities were observed following administration on Day 16 pc.
L1790: ## **Artemisinins: Oral Embryo-Fetal Development Comparison Study in Rats/Study No. CD2004/00606/01 (b) (4) Study No. G04117)**
L1791: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1792: - Artesunate, DHA, artemether and arteether induced significant embryolethality (55 to 75% postimplantation loss) in rats following oral administration of a single dose on Day 10 pc. An approximate 20% reduction in mean fetal weight was observed in all artemisinin-treated groups compared with control. No maternal toxicity was noted.
- Similar treatment-related fetal malformations were noted following exposure to all artemisinins. These included cardiovascular malformations (ventricular septal defects and interrupted aortic arch), skeletal defects (curved scapula, curved, misshapen or short humerus/femur, duplicate sternebrae), and a distinct liver variation (absence of the deep fissure of the median lobe).

L1819: ## 18.3 OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP recommendations)
L1820: ## Study 1128:
L1821: This study was a Phase 1a, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, in-patient, ascending single IV dose study to determine the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics of Intravenous Artesunate (IV AS) in healthy male and non-pregnant, non-lactating female subjects aged 18 to 55 years old. Five cohorts of 8 volunteers per cohort were studied. Within each cohort, 6 subjects received a single dose of IV AS infused over a period of 2 minutes at 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 4.0, and 8.0 mg/kg, and 2 subjects in each cohort received a similar infusion of placebo (mannitol in phosphate buffer).
L1822: **PK results and conclusions:** The PK parameter values of artesunate and dihydroartemisinin (DHA) following single IV Artesunate doses of 0.5 mg/kg to 8 mg/kg are summarized in Table 18-1 and Table 18-2. The mean plasma concentration profiles are shown in Figure 18-1 and the dose-proportionality relationship for Cmax and AUC is shown in Figure 18-2.

L1827: Figure 18-1. Mean plasma concentration-time profiles of AS (top) and DHA (bottom), an active metabolite of AS measured by LC-MS/MS after a single intravenous dosage with a 2-minute short-term infusion of AS at 0.5, 1, 2, 4, and 8 mg/kg in healthy volunteers (N = 6 for each dose cohort).
L1828: **Figure 18-2. Dose proportional increases of AS and DHA Cmax and AUC Single Doses of IV Artesunate after Single IV Administrations of 0.5, 1, 2, 4 and 8 mg/kg by Short-term Infusion (2 minutes) in healthy volunteers**
L1829: Artesunate displayed approximately dose proportional increases in respective AUC and Cmax estimates when administered IV to healthy volunteers over the dose range of 2.0 mg/kg – 8.0 mg/kg. At doses of 0.5 mg/kg and 1.0 mg/kg insufficient plasma concentration-time data was available to draw firm conclusions for doses below 2.0 mg/kg. Artesunate half-lives ranged from 5 to 37 min.
L1830: DHA appeared rapidly (quantifiable within 5 minutes of the end of the infusion), achieved a maximum concentration within about 20 minutes and disappeared with a half-life of about 1-2 hours. Across the range of 1.0 mg/kg - 8.0 mg/kg, the DHA AUC and Cmax estimates showed approximate dose proportional increases. The clearance of DHA was approximately 50 L/hr, and the volume of distribution was approximately 100 L. Thus, in comparison with the parent compound, DHA has a smaller clearance and a larger volume of distribution, which together yield a half-life that is longer than that of artesunate.
L1831: ## **Study 1142: Multiple Ascending Dose Study**
L1832: Study 1142 was Phase 1a, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial using multiple ascending doses of intravenous artesunate to determine its safety, tolerability and PK in healthy male and female subjects. Six subjects in each dose group received AS over about 2 min as a short-term IV infusion. Blood samples for the measurements of AS and DHA levels in plasma was approximately 10 min before dosing (0 h), and at 5, 20, 40 min, 1, 2, 4, 6, and 8 h on Days 1, 2, and 3 post-dose. During the multiple-dose period, samples were collected immediately before morning dose on days 2 and 3 for additional trough values.
L1833: PK parameters for artesunate and DHA are summarized in Table 18-3 and Table 18-4, respectively.

L1839: **Reviewer Conclusion:** After IV injection of AS, parent drug rapidly declined and was converted to DHA with overall mean elimination half-lives ranging 0.1 – 0.23 h for AS and 1.23 – 1.63 h for DHA. The Cmax and AUC values of AS and DHA increased proportionally to the AS dose. AS and DHA CL, Vss, and T½ all remained relatively constant across all doses and with respect to days of dosing. Thus, the PK of AS and DHA are linear. Plasma accumulation of AS was not apparent, while accumulation of DHA was minimal; this is to be expected with the observed short half-lives of each moiety. The peak concentration (Cmax) of AS was much higher than that of DHA with a range of 3.08 – 3.78.
L1840: ## **Study 1168: A Phase II, Open Label, Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Efficacy and Pharmacokinetics of Intravenous Artesunate in Adults with Uncomplicated Malaria.**
L1841: Study 1168 was an unblinded, non-randomized, phase II clinical study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics, safety, tolerability, and efficacy (parasite clearance) of intravenous artesunate for the treatment of uncomplicated *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria in adults in Kenya. This study involved the treatment of 30 adult subjects, aged 18 to 65 years with uncomplicated falciparum malaria, with intravenous artesunate (2.4 mg/kg) once a day for 3 days as an IV injection given over 2 minutes. Following completion of the intravenous artesunate treatment, all subjects received Malarone follow-on therapy (4 tablets once daily for 3 days) to ensure parasitologic cure. Pharmacokinetic samples were obtained at 9 approximate time points (0, 5 min, 10 min, 20 min, 40 min, 1 h, 2 h, 4 h, and 6 h) on Day 0. Pharmacokinetic results for artesunate and DHA are summarized in below.

L1846: The results show that DHA was detected in all volunteers' plasma until 6 hours, while the parent drug was undetectable at time-points as early as 1-2 hours after the single short-term infusion. Although the plasma concentration of DHA was higher than AS with the ratio of AUC_{DHA/AS} of 1.89, the peak concentration of AS was much higher than that of DHA with a ratio of Cmax_{AS/DHA} of 10.35. It is known that DHA has a longer half-life of 1.26 hours, which is 7.9 folds longer than AS (T_{1/2} \sim 0.16 hours).
L1847: ## **In Vitro Studies**
L1848: Melendez, 2010: In Vitro Biotransformation and Drug Interaction Studies of Artesunate and its Active Metabolite Dihydroartemisinin Using Plasma and Liver Preparations Followed by LCMS and Fluorometric Analyses
Study #	Melendez, 2010
Objective	In vitro study designed to report a simple, fast, and reliable LCMS method for the simultaneous quantification of low nanomolar concentrations of AS and DHA from biological fluids and its application to profiling the compounds in plasma, liver microsomes from several species, and cryopreserved human hepatocytes.
Cell System Used	Cryopreserved human hepatocytes
Investigational drug concentration	Metabolic stability of AS at 100 µM. Incubations containing 10 and 25 µM final AS concentrations yielded 4.2 and 10.5 µM DHA, respectively.
Are the methods appropriate	Yes
Results and Conclusions: A simple LC-MS assay for the simultaneous determination of AS and DHA in plasma and from in vitro preparations was developed. The assay has acceptable specificity, sensitivity, accuracy and precision. Incubation of AS at 37°C in the presence of plasma or physiological buffer (phosphate, K-H) resulted in at least 25% spontaneous conversion to DHA after one hour. Hepatic microsomal metabolism of AS yielded an estimated half-life of 20 minutes and resulted in nearly complete (greater than 95%) turnover of AS into DHA after one hour. Hepatocytes incubation resulted in approximately 50% biotransformation after two hours; presumably due to membrane barriers and less CYP 450 activity compared to microsomal preparations. Small amounts of putative DHA-glucuronide and acetyl CoA were also detected but not confirmed using authentic standards. This data supports the notion that administered AS not transformed in plasma, will undergo first pass liver metabolism into a more stable compound, DHA, which is subsequently conjugated for elimination. Drug interactions studies identified CYP 450 1A2 and 2C19 as isoenzymes moderately affected by DHA (Table 1) and suggested the need for in vivo studies and/or monitoring of patients when combination of AS with other therapies is needed. The Cmax of DHA following administration of 2.4 mg/kg IV Artesunate in malaria patients is approximately 3 µg/mL. The IC50 values derived for CYP1A2 (32.3 µM) and 2C19 (75.4 µM) in this in vitro study correspond to approximately 9 µg/mL and 21 µg/mL. Thus, DHA is unlikely to affect the substrates of CYP1A2 and 2C19.
Table 1. IC50 (µM) values calculated for Artesunic acid and Dihydroartemisinin. Data represent the mean of 3 different assays done by duplicate ± standard deviation.
Isoenzyme	AS	DHA
1A2	> 100 µM	32.3 ± 2.94
2C9	> 100 µM	> 100 µM
2C19	> 100 µM	75.4 ± 5.57
2D6	> 100 µM	> 100 µM
3A4	> 100 µM	> 100 µM
L1849: Role of P Glycoprotein in Absorption of Novel Antimalarial Drugs
Study #	Crowe et al, 2006
Objective	Role of P Glycoprotein (P-gP) in Absorption of Novel Antimalarial Drugs
Cell System Used	Monolayer of a CLEFF9 subclone of human Caco-2 cells with high P-gp-mediated efflux
Investigational drug concentration	DHA - 1 \muM
Naphthoquine - 5 \muM,
Pyronidine - 5 \muM, and 30 \muM, and 10 \muM in presence of reference inhibitor for each transporter
Reference Inhibitor (concentration)	P-gP: PSC-833 and GF120918 (4 \muM)
Are controls and control concentrations appropriate?	Yes
Methods: Drug transport was studied with an in vitro gastrointestinal model by using a monolayer of a CLEFF9 subclone of human Caco-2 cells with high P-gp-mediated efflux and previously reported experimental protocols. Briefly, cells were seeded onto 0.6-cm2 polycarbonate filters in 24-well plates and grown in high-glucose Dulbecco's modified Eagle's medium for 3 weeks. Following incubation in buffered Hanks balanced salt solution (HBSS) with or without specific efflux inhibitors (PSC-833 and GF120918) for 30 min at 37°C, transepithelial electrical resistance was measured and assay medium/inhibitors were placed in the receiver chambers. Antimalarial drugs were added to the donor chamber of each well. The apical and basolateral chambers received 0.3 and 0.6 ml medium, respectively. Transepithelial electrical resistance measurements were repeated at the conclusion of the studies to ensure continued monolayer integrity
Results and Conclusions: As shown in the table below, the ratio of apical/basolateral transport is <1 for DHA, suggesting that there is no net efflux. Thus, it is unlikely that P-gp has a significant role in the gastrointestinal absorption of DHA. Given the lack of an effect of P-gP on the transport of DHA, no dosage adjustment of IV Artesunate is needed upon coadministration of P-gP inhibitors or inducers.

L1853: ## Pharmacokinetics of mefloquine alone or in combination with artesunate
L1854: *Bulletin of the World Health Organization, 1994, 72 (1): 83-87*
L1855: **J. Karbwang, K. Na Bangchang, A. Thanavibul, D.J. Back, D. Bunnag, &amp; T. Harinasuta**
L1856: A randomized comparative trial of the pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of oral doses of mefloquine and of mefloquine in combination with oral artesunate was carried out on 20 Thai male patients with acute, uncomplicated falciparum malaria. The patients were randomized to receive either mefloquine alone (8 patients; 1250 mg of mefloquine-initial dose, 750 mg; followed 6 hours later by 500 mg), or in combination with oral artesunate (12 patients-initial dose, 200 mg of artesunate; followed by 750 mg and 500 mg of mefloquine 6 hours and 12 hours later, respectively).
L1857: **Treatments.** The patients were randomized into two groups (A and B) to receive two drug regimens as follows:
L1858: - Group A (8 patients): an initial oral dose of 750 mg of mefloquine (Lariam®, Hoffmann-La Roche; 250 mg per tablet) followed 6 hours later by 500 mg.
- Group B: an initial oral dose of 200 mg of artesunate followed by mefloquine (750 mg and 500 mg, 6 hours and 12 hours after the artesunate, respectively).
L1859: **Blood for mefloquine concentrations.** Blood samples were collected for determination of mefloquine concentrations at 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, 20, 24, 30, 36, and 42 hours after the initial dose of mefloquine was administered, and then on days 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 14, 21, 28, 35, and 42.
L1860: **Pharmacokinetic analysis:** The whole blood mefloquine concentrations were determined using HPLC and the concentration-time profiles were analyzed using an iterative, nonlinear curve-fitting program (PCNONLIN, Metzler &amp; Weiner, 1984). A multiple dosing approach was used to fit a two-compartment model to the unweighted data.
L1861: ## Results:
L1862: Concomitant administration of oral artesunate with mefloquine resulted in a lowered Cmax and AUC of mefloquine (Table 18-8). The apparent oral clearance of mefloquine increased by 25% following concomitant administration of oral artesunate.
L1863: **Table 18-8: Mefloquine Pharmacokinetic parameters (Mean \pm SD) in healthy volunteers receiving mefloquine alone or mefloquine +artesunate**
L1864: Pharmacokinetic parameter	Mefloquine alone	Mefloquine + artesunate
Cmax (\mug/ml)	2.2 \pm 0.5	1.6 \pm 0.4
Tmax (hours)	20.3 \pm 5.2	15.0 \pm 3.0
AUC (\mug-day/ml)	17.2 \pm 6.4	12.8
T1/2 (days)	11.9 \pm 2.7	11.0 \pm 7.0

L1865: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L1866: Pharmacokinetic parameter	Mefloquine alone	Mefloquine + artesunate
Vd/F (l/kg)	25.0 \pm 6.0	31.8 \pm 5.1
L1867: **Reviewer's Comments:** The results of this publication indicate that the mefloquine AUC and C_{max} are reduced by about 37% when mefloquine and oral artesunate are administered together compared to when mefloquine is administered alone. The authors have hypothesized that since mefloquine is highly protein-bound, the lower concentrations that resulted when mefloquine was taken 6 hours after artesunate could arise because of competition for protein binding sites between mefloquine and artesunate or its metabolite(s), resulting in higher free mefloquine concentrations and therefore, a wider mefloquine concentration distribution. Free mefloquine can be cleared faster. This explanation is supported by the larger volume of distribution and faster clearance in the patients who received the combined regimen relative to those patients who received mefloquine alone. Alternatively, the decrease in mefloquine concentration could have resulted from the reduction in its oral bioavailability, induced by artesunate.
L1868: Overall, despite the exposure difference (\sim37% lower C_{max} and AUC) of mefloquine when administered with oral artesunate versus when given alone, given that the favorable results of the fever clearance time and parasite clearance time, no dosage adjustment is deemed necessary. It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of oral artesunate on the PK of mefloquine. However, oral artesunate is not approved in the US.
L1869: ## Assessment of the Effect of Mefloquine on Artesunate Pharmacokinetics in Healthy Male Volunteers
L1870: *ANTIMICROBIAL AGENTS AND CHEMOTHERAPY, Mar. 2007, p. 1099–1101*
L1871: Timothy M. E. Davis, Michelle England, Anne-Marie Dunlop, Madhu Page-Sharp, Nathalie Cambon, Thomas G. Keller, Ja'nos L. Heidecker, and Kenneth F. Ilett
L1872: The effect of mefloquine on artesunate pharmacokinetics was assessed using crossover design in 20 volunteers given artesunate for 3 days, and after a washout period of 21 days, by combination therapy of artesunate and mefloquine for 3 days. Each subject received 200 mg oral artesunate (Mepha Ltd., Switzerland) after an overnight fast on three consecutive mornings (period 1). After a washout phase of 21 days, this schedule was repeated, but mefloquine (Mepha) at 250 mg daily was given at the same time as artesunate (period 2). On days 1 and 3 of each period, blood samples were drawn for drug assay under a predetermined schedule from immediately before (0h) to 8 h post-dose. Additional samples were taken for mefloquine assay on the mornings of days 2, 4, 5, and 6 during and after period 2. Drug assays were by a validated HPLC-MS method.
L1873: ## Results:
L1874: Artesunate was measurable transiently and in low concentrations relative to DHA (Figure 18-3). For DHA, the Day 3 Cmax and AUC were similar when artesunate was administered alone (period 1) versus when given with mefloquine (period 2) (Table 18-9). The Day 1 Cmax of DHA following coadministration with mefloquine appeared to result in a 32% decrease compared to when artesunate was given alone.

L1881: **Reviewer's Comments:** The results of this publication indicated that mefloquine does not alter the pharmacokinetics of artesunate when the two drugs are coadministered. In contrast to the case for artemisinin from the literature on autoinduction by artemisinin drugs, the authors reported that no significant time-dependent changes in artesunate-DHA pharmacokinetics were observed.
L1882: It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of oral artesunate on the PK of mefloquine. However, oral artesunate is not approved in the US.
L1883: ## **Title: Absence of an interaction between artesunate and atovaquone \pm proguanil**
L1884: *Eur J Clin Pharmacol (1999) 55: 469\pm474*
L1885: **M. van Vugt, M. D. Edstein, S. Proux, K. Lay M. Ooh, S. Looareesuwan, N. J. White, F. Nosten**
L1886: The authors investigated whether artesunate would alter the pharmacokinetics and tolerability of atovaquone and proguanil in an open, randomized, two-period crossover study in healthy volunteers. The study was carried out in 12 healthy adult volunteers at Shoklo, a camp for displaced people on the western border of Thailand. The study was an open, randomized, two-period crossover with a washout period of at least 90 days between drug administrations. After giving written informed consent, the volunteers were allocated randomly to receive either 1000 mg atovaquone plus 400 mg proguanil, or 1000 mg atovaquone plus 400 mg proguanil plus 250 mg oral artesunate. The fixed combination atovaquone plus proguanil (Malarone) was dispensed as pink, film-coated tablets, each containing 250 mg atovaquone and 100 mg proguanil(b) (4). Each tablet of artesunate contained 50 mg artesunate and was manufactured by Guilin Pharma in China. All three doses were administered orally with 200 ml of milk-ovaltine (8% fat) after 30 min of having a standard meal (boiled rice plus chicken curry), with a high fat content. The volunteers were also randomized to receive either the combination tablet or the same doses of atovaquone and proguanil separately. Each volunteer, therefore, received the combination on one occasion and the individual drugs on another.
L1887: **Blood sampling:** Blood samples (5 ml) were obtained before the first dose (day 1) and on days 3-10 following the third dose. At the beginning of day 3, sampling was done through an indwelling Teflon cannula inserted in a forearm vein and kept patent with heparinized saline for 24 h and thereafter by venipuncture. Blood was drawn before the third dose and at the following times: 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, 32, 48, 72, 96, 120, 144 and 168 h. Over 10 days, the maximum volume of blood drawn for drug analysis was 90 ml. Blood samples were centrifuged (2000 g, 10 min), and plasma was stored at -20 °C
L1888: ## **Drug analysis**
L1889: Plasma concentrations of atovaquone, proguanil and cycloguanil (the active metabolite of proguanil) were measured by means of a validated HPLC method.
L1890: ## **Results:**
L1891: Although marked inter-individual variability was observed in the kinetics of atovaquone, there were no significant differences in the pharmacokinetic parameters of atovaquone whether it was administered as a separate drug or in the combined Malarone formulation. There was also no significant difference between these parameters with or without artesunate coadministration. The mean concentration-time profiles comparing atovaquone levels with and without artesunate coadministration is shown in Figure 18-4. The profiles were essentially superimposable, confirming the absence of any effect of co-administered artesunate on the
L1892: disposition of atovaquone. The pharmacokinetic parameters of atovaquone derived from the plasma concentration-time data are shown in Table 18-10.

L1905: **Reviewer's Conclusions/Comments:** Although this study shows that **orally** administered artesunate does not alter the pharmacokinetics of atovaquone plus proguanil (Mepreon) in healthy volunteers, it is not known if the same results would be observed for IV artesunate since the absolute bioavailability of PO artesunate is not known.
L1906: ## **Pharmacokinetics and tolerability of artesunate and amodiaquine alone and in combination in healthy volunteers**
L1907: *Eur J Clin Pharmacol (2008) 64:683–690*
L1908: **Catherine Orrell, Francesca Little, Peter Smith, Peter Folb, Walter Taylor, Piero Olliaro, Karen I. Barnes**
L1909: In a randomized, three-phase, cross-over study, amodiaquine (10 mg/kg) and artesunate (4 mg/kg) were given as single oral doses to 15 healthy volunteers. Artesunate was given to all volunteers on day 0. On day 7 they received either amodiaquine or amodiaquine plus artesunate and the alternative regimen on day 28. The pharmacokinetics of artesunate and amodiaquine and their respective main active metabolites, dihydroartemisinin and desethylamodiaquine, were compared following monotherapy and combination therapy.
L1910: **Blood sampling:** Volunteers in phase one had blood samples collected before taking artesunate and at 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8 and 12 h after artesunate administration. In phases two and three (i.e. volunteers receiving artesunate plus amodiaquine or amodiaquine alone) venous blood samples were collected pre-dose and at 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, 48 and 72 h, and days 4, 5, 7, 10, 14 and 20 post-dose. Each sample was centrifuged within 5 min of collection and the plasma transferred to separate plastic cryotubes and frozen at -70^{\circ}\text{C} until analyzed for artesunate.
L1911: ## **Drug analysis**
L1912: Artesunate and DHA were analyzed using a validated HPLC method. Amodiaquine and desethylamodiaquine were analyzed by a validated LC mass spectrometry.
L1913: ## **Results:**

L1921: The AUC, Cmax and time to Cmax (Tmax) of artesunate (AS) were similar following monotherapy and combination therapy with amodiaquine (ACT; Table 1). Following the combination therapy, dihydroartemisinin (DHA) AUC was lower [ratio 67% (95% CI 51–88%); P=0.008], Cmax was lower [ratio 51% (95% CI 33–78%); P=0.005], T1/2 was longer [ratio 157% (95% CI 115–213%); P=0.008] and Vd was larger [ratio 192% (95% CI 133–275%); P=0.003], when compared with AS monotherapy.

L1926: Treatment effects generated from the ANOVA have been exponentiated to express within-subject comparisons between monotherapy and combination therapy as a ratio, adjusted for period and sequence effects
L1927: AUC Area under the plasma concentration time curve, Cmax maximum concentration, Tmax time to Cmax, [Day 7] day 7 concentration, T1/2 elimination half-life, Vd apparent volume of distribution, f oral bioavailability or fraction of the drug absorbed, Cl clearance, AQ amodiaquine, SD standard deviation, CI confidence interval, NA not available (as too few patients had quantifiable amodiaquine concentrations)
L1928: The pharmacokinetic parameters of amodiaquine were similar when administered as monotherapy or in combination with artesunate (ACT; Table 18-13). The desethylamodiaquine AUC is lower [ratio 65% (95% CI 46–90%); P=0.015], Tmax shorter [ratio 60% (95% CI 45–80%); P=0.003] and clearance more rapid [ratio 164% (95% CI 112–243%); P= 0.016] when amodiaquine was administered in combination with artesunate rather than as monotherapy. There was a trend towards a lower day 7 concentration of desethylamodiaquine [ratio 56% (95% CI 30–104%); P= 0.064] when amodiaquine was administered in combination.
L1929: **Author's Conclusions:** To conclude, the total exposure to the main active metabolites of both artesunate and amodiaquine was significantly reduced when administered in combination to healthy African volunteers; this might be important clinically. However, because cure rates with this combination are generally higher than with amodiaquine monotherapy, artesunate and amodiaquine could remain in the armamentarium of drugs used to combat falciparum malaria, provided efficacy continues to be monitored and adequate safety precautions can be taken.
L1930: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L1931: - This study shows that oral artesunate does not alter the pharmacokinetics of amodiaquine in healthy volunteers
- The coadministration of amodiaquine reduced the Cmax of DHA by 51% and AUC by 67% when given as oral artesunate.
- Coadministration of oral artesunate reduced Cmax and AUC of amodiaquine by approximately 75% and reduced the AUC of desethylamodiaquine (DEAQ) by 65% but did not alter the Cmax of DEAQ.
- It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of oral artesunate on the PK of amodiaquine. Oral artesunate is not approved in the US. Overall, no dosage adjustments for the administration of IV Artesunate with amodiaquine are necessary, given that this study evaluated oral artesunate and also given that IV Artesunate, if approved, will not be given with amodiaquine.
L1932: ## **Pharmacokinetics of Artesunate Alone and in Combination with Sulfadoxine/Pyrimethamine in Healthy Sudanese Volunteers**
L1933: *Am. J. Trop. Med. Hyg., 90(6), 2014, pp. 1087–1093*
L1934: Kamal M. Matar, Abdelmoneim I. Awad, and Sakina B. Elamin
L1935: The authors conducted a study to assess the potential impact of sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine (SP) on the pharmacokinetics of orally administered artesunate (AS) and its active metabolite, dihydroartemisinin (DHA), in healthy adults. A single-dose, randomized, open-label, crossover study design with a washout period of three weeks was performed with 16 volunteers. After a 12-hour overnight fasting, the first group received a single AS oral dose of 200 mg and the second group received a single oral dose of AS (200 mg) in combination with Fansidar (Roche, Basel, Switzerland) (3 tablets: 500 mg of sulfadoxine/25 mg of pyrimethamine per tablet). The first and second groups received the opposite treatment after a washout period of three weeks.
L1936: ## **Blood sampling:**
L1937: Blood (5 mL) samples were withdrawn into heparinized tubes before dosing and at 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0, 3.5, 4, 4.5, 5, 6, 9, and 12 hours after drug administration. Plasma samples were immediately separated and kept frozen at -80^{\circ}\text{C}.
L1938: ## **Drug analysis**
L1939: A validated liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry analytical method for determination of artesunate, and DHA was developed.
