L1947: *IQR = interquartile range; t_{1/2} = elimination half-life; T_{max} = time needed to attain maximum concentration; C_{max} = maximum plasma concentration; A concentration-time curves; Vd/F = volume of distribution; CL/F = oral clearance.
L1948: As seen above, the PK parameters of AS and DHA were similar during AS monotherapy or when given in combination with SP. It does not appear that SP affects the PK of AS.
L1949: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L1950: It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of SP on the PK of **oral artesunate**. Oral artesunate is not approved in the US. Also, in this study the authors evaluated the effect of single doses of SP and oral artesunate. However, based on the results from this study, no dosage adjustment for the administration of IV Artesunate with SP would be needed if the two drug products are co-administered.
L1951: ## **Artemisinin antimalarials moderately affect cytochrome P450 enzyme activity in healthy subjects**
L1952: *Fundamental &amp; Clinical Pharmacology 21 (2007) 307–316*
L1953: **Sara Asimus, Doaa Elsherbin, Trinh N. Hai, Britt Jansson, Nguyen V. Huong, Max G. Petzold, Ulrika S.H. Simonsson, Michael Ashton**
L1954: The aim of this study was to investigate which principal human cytochrome P450 (CYP450) enzymes are affected by artemisinin antimalarial drugs and to what degree the artemisinin derivatives differ with respect to their respective induction and inhibition capacity. Seventy-five healthy adults were randomized to receive therapeutic oral doses of artemisinin, dihydroartemisinin, arteether, artemether or artesunate for 5 days (days 1–5). A six-drug cocktail consisting of caffeine, coumarin, mephenytoin, metoprolol, chlorzoxazone and midazolam was administered orally on days -6, 1, 5 and 10 to assess the activities of CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1 and CYP3A, respectively.
L1955: ## **Study design**
L1956: The volunteers were randomized to treatment with one of five different artemisinin study drugs; artemisinin (2×250 mg capsules; Mediplantex, Hanoi, Vietnam), dihydroartemisinin (60 mg tablets; Mediplantex), artemether (Artenam®, 2×50 mg tablets; Arenco Pharmaceutica nv, Geel, Belgium), arteether (Artecef®, 2×1 mL ampoules of the intramuscular preparation containing 50 mg arteether in sesame oil; Artecef BV, Maarsen, The Netherlands) or artesunate (Plasmotrim®, 2×50 mg tablets; Mepha Ltd, Aesch, Switzerland). Repeated oral doses were administered in the morning for 5 days (day 1–5). The cocktail of probe drugs, caffeine (Koffein Recip®, 100 mg tablets, Stock-holm, Sweden), coumarin (5 mg capsules, Laboratory of the National Corporation of Swedish Pharmacies, Goteborg, Sweden), midazolam (Dormicum®, 7.5 mg tablets; Roche a/s, Hvidovre, Denmark), mephenytoin (Epilan Gerot®, 100 mg tablets; Gerot Pharmazeutica, Vienna, Austria), metoprolol (Seloken®, 100 mg tablets; AstraZeneca Sverige, Molndal, Sweden) and chlorzoxazone (Paraflex®, 250 mg tablets, AstraZeneca Sverige) were given orally 1 week before (day -6) administration of the artemisinin drugs. On day 1 and day 5, the administration of the probe drug cocktail was repeated, 1 h after intake of the artemisinin drugs. After a washout period of 5 days (day 10), the probe drug cocktail was given again. Study drug and the cocktail were each taken with 200 mL water.
L1957: ## **Blood sampling:**
L1958: Probe compounds were measured in samples obtained pre-dose and at 4 h after intake of the cocktail drugs on days -6, 1, 5 and 10. Ten milliliters of blood was collected, and all plasma samples were immediately frozen and kept at -70°C.
L1959: **Drug analysis:** Plasma concentrations of caffeine, paraxanthine, chlorzoxazone, 6-OH-chlorzoxazone, 7-OH-coumarin, metoprolol, a-OH-metoprolol, midazolam and 1-OH-midazolam were measured in b-glucuronidase-treated samples by use of a validated LC/MS/MS method.
L1960: **Data analysis:** The plasma concentration ratio of paraxanthine to caffeine at 4 h was used to assess CYP1A2 activity. The total amount of 7-OH-coumarin excreted in 0- to 8-h urine was used as an index of CYP2A6 activity. The ability to hydroxylate S-mephenytoin (CYP2C19) was estimated by the S-4'-OH-mephenytoin to S-mephenytoin plasma ratio at 4 h post-dose. The 4-h-\alpha-OH-metoprolol to metoprolol ratio and 6-OH-chlorzoxazone to chlorzoxazone ratio were used to evaluate the activities of CYP2D6 and CYP2E1, respectively. The concentration of 1-OH-midazolam divided by that of midazolam in the 4-h plasma sample was used to indicate CYP3A activity. A repeated ANOVA model with Gaussian random effects was applied to the log-transformed (natural base) data. Results are presented as quotients (mean, 98.75% CI) based on anti-logarithms of the contrasts for the different occasions. Quotients larger, or smaller, than unity may be associated with enzyme induction or inhibition, respectively.
L1961: **Results:** The effects of the artemisinin antimalarials on the major CYP450 enzymes are summarized in Table 18-16.

L1964: aP &lt; 0.0125 (\alpha adjusted for multiple testing).
L1965: bQuotients &gt;1 indicate increased enzyme activity, quotients &lt;1 indicate decreased enzyme activity.
L1966: The reviewer focused on the effects of AS and DHA, since these moieties are most relevant for this NDA for IV AS. As seen in Table 1, AS inhibits the metabolism of the CYP2A6 substrate, coumarin, and induce the metabolism of the CYP3A substrate, midazolam. The potential for AS to affect the other CYP enzymes was not apparent, as evidenced by the day5/day-6 ratio that centered mostly approximately 1 for the other CYP enzyme drug substrates. DHA appeared to slightly induce the CYP2A6 and CYP3A enzyme and inhibit CYP1A2 slightly.
L1967: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L1968: - This result of this study should be interpreted with caution because the drug-enzyme effect was evaluated by only one PK sample collected at 4 hours post-dose. The authors should have measured multiple concentrations and calculated the AUC of the probe drug in the presence and absence of artesunate to determine the ratio to evaluate the effect of the drugs on various enzymes.
- It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of oral artesunate on various CYP enzyme substrates. Oral artesunate is not approved in the US. Based on the results of this DDI cocktail study, there appeared to be some evidence that oral AS inhibited the metabolism of the CYP2A6 substrate, coumarin, and induced the metabolism of the CYP3A substrate, midazolam. DHA also showed some evidence to inhibit the metabolism of the CYP1A2 substrate, caffeine and induce the metabolism of the CYP2A6 substrate, coumarin and the CYP3A substrate, midazolam. These levels of respective inhibition and/or induction by AS and DHA is deemed by the reviewer to be no clinical concern. Thus, no dosage adjustments for drugs metabolized by CYP1A2, 2D6, 2C19, 2A6, 2E1 or 3A enzymes when co-administered with IV Artesunate are needed.
L1969: ## Drug-Drug Interaction Analysis of Pyronaridine/Artesunate and Ritonavir in Healthy Volunteers
L1970: *Am. J. Trop. Med. Hyg., 86(3), 2012, pp. 489–495*
L1971: Carrie A. Morris, Luis Lopez-Lazaro, Donald Jung, Janthima Methaneethorn, Stephan Duparc, Isabelle Borghini-Fuhrer, Rolf Pokorny, Chang-Sik Shin, and Lawrence Fleckenstein
L1972: A multiple dose, parallel group study was conducted to assess for a drug-drug interaction between the pyronaridine/artesunate (PA) combination antimalarial and ritonavir. Thirty-four healthy adults were randomized (1:1) to receive PA for 3 days or PA with ritonavir (100 mg twice daily for 17 days, PA administered on Days 8–10). After enrollment, subjects were randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to Arm A or Arm B.
L1973: - Subjects in Arm A were administered ritonavir soft gelatin capsules (Norvir; Lot no.: 6008303; Abbott Laboratories, Abbott Park, IL) 100 mg once on Day 1, followed by ritonavir 100 mg every 12 hours on Days 2–17; these subjects were additionally administered three or four PA tablets, depending on weight, once daily on Days 8, 9, and 10. Days 8, 9, and 10. Subjects weighing &lt; 65 kg were administered three PA tablets (180 mg pyronaridine tetraphosphate: 60 mg artesunate per tablet) per day; subjects weighing 65 kg or more were administered four tablets per day.
- Subjects in Arm B were administered three or four PA tablets, depending on weight, on Days 1, 2, and 3.
L1974: ## Blood sampling:
L1975: In Arm A, plasma samples for analysis of ritonavir concentrations were collected on Days 1 and 10 at pre-dose and 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 9, 12, 18, and 24 hours post-dose. Plasma samples for artesunate/DHA analysis were collected on Day 10 at pre-dose, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, and 12 hours post-dose. Blood samples for pyronaridine analysis were collected on Day 10 at pre-dose, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours after the last dose and at 2, 3, 5, 12, 19, 26, 33, and 40 days after the last dose. In addition, trough concentrations for all analytes were assessed on study Day 8 (pre-dose for pyronaridine and artesunate/DHA) and Day 9 before the morning dosing.
L1976: In Arm B, plasma samples for artesunate/DHA analysis were collected on Day 3 at pre-dose, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, and 12 hours post-dose. Blood concentrations for pyronaridine were collected on Day 3 at pre-dose, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours post-dose, and at 2, 3, 5, 12, 19, 26, 33, and 40 days post-dose. Trough concentrations of pyronaridine, artesunate, and DHA were assessed on study Days 1 and 2 before morning dosing.
L1977: ## Drug analysis
L1978: A validated liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry analytical method for determination of artesunate, DHA, and ritonavir was developed.
L1979: ## **Results:**

The AS and DHA pharmacokinetic parameters in the two study arms are presented in Table 18-18. L1999: AUC = area under the curve.
L2000: As seen in Table 3, the point estimates for the ratios of geometric means indicate that on average, ritonavir coadministration was associated with decreases in DHA Cmax, AUC0-t, and AUC0-inf of ~27%, 36%, and 38%, respectively. The authors concluded that ritonavir coadministration decreases exposure to DHA. This finding is of clinical relevance because currently available ACTs contain artesunate or artemether, which are metabolized to DHA, or DHA itself. The reduced exposure to DHA associated with ritonavir coadministration poses an increased risk of suboptimal antimalarial efficacy; the clinical effects of reduced exposure may be augmented by the immunocompromised state of the patient population receiving ritonavir therapy. Pharmacokinetic parameters for ritonavir are shown in Table 18-20.
L2001: **Table 18-20.** Pharmacokinetic parameters of ritonavir from Day 1 and Day 10*. Day 1 represents Ritonavir alone and Day 10 represents Ritonavir coadministered with PA.

L2003: *Cmax and area under the curve (AUC) values are adjusted for 1.4 mg/kg q12hr dose. All values given as geometric mean (geometric %CV).
L2004: Coadministration of PA resulted in substantially increased exposure to ritonavir, suggesting that either total body clearance decreased and/or the extent of ritonavir absorption increased. From *in vitro* evidence, pyronaridine is an inhibitor of CYP2D6, which is involved in the metabolism of ritonavir, and of P-gp, which is involved in the efflux transport of ritonavir. Either CYP2D6 or P-gp inhibition could produce the observed increase in exposure. If the increase in exposure stemmed primarily from CYP2D6 inhibition, some increase in ritonavir half-life on Day 10, as
L2005: compared with Day 1, would be anticipated. However, half-life decreased, suggesting that the increased ritonavir exposure may have stemmed primarily from pyronardine inhibition of P-gp mediated ritonavir efflux. It should be noted that the design of this study does not allow for effects of PA on ritonavir pharmacokinetics to be differentiated from any intrinsic time-dependency of ritonavir pharmacokinetics.
L2006: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L2007: - This study shows that coadministration of ritonavir modestly decreased the systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC) of DHA and slightly increased the systemic to artesunate (AS). However, it is to be noted that the authors evaluated oral artesunate in this study, which is not approved in the US. This effect of reduced DHA exposure oral artesunate following coadministration with ritonavir could be due to induction effect of ritonavir on DHA metabolism via CYP3A, 1A2, 2C9, 2C19 and 2B6 (US PI for ritonavir).
- The combination of pyronardine + artesunate (PA) substantially increased the PK exposure (Cmax and AUC) of ritonavir. The reviewer agrees with the authors' interpretation of the mechanism for this interaction due to pyronardine inhibition of P-gp.
- Based on published literature, ritonavir decreased the Cmax and AUC(0-inf) of DHA following co-administration with oral artesunate by approximately 27% and 38%, respectively, in healthy adult subjects. It is expected that co-administration of ritonavir with IV artesunate will also decrease the Cmax and AUC of DHA. Therefore, ritonavir should not be co-administered with IV artesunate.
L2008: ## Nevirapine-Based Antiretroviral Therapy Impacts Artesunate and Dihydroartemisinin Disposition in HIV-Infected Nigerian Adults
L2009: *AIDS Research and Treatment Volume 2012*
L2010: Fatai A. Fehintola, Kimberly K. Scarsi, Qing Ma, Sunil Parikh, Gene D. Morse, Babafemi Taiwo,2 Ibrahim Tope Akinola, Isaac F. Adewole, Niklas Lindegardh, Aphiradee Phakderaj, Oladosu Ojengbede, Robert L. Murphy, Olusegun O. Akinyinka, and Francesca T. Aweeka
L2011: The authors conducted a parallel-group comparison to explore the pharmacokinetic interactions between nevirapine (NVP) and oral artesunate taken in combination with amodiaquine in asymptomatic 21 HIV-infected Nigerian adults by evaluating the disposition kinetics of artesunate and DHA in the presence and absence of steady-state NVP. Artesunate/amodiaquine 200/600 mg was given daily for three days. Patients were recruited into two groups: (1) NVP group and (2) control group.
L2012: - 10 Patients in the NVP group were on the same anti-retroviral therapy (ART), consisting of lamivudine (3TC) 150 mg, zidovudine (AZT) 300 mg, and NVP 200 mg taken twice a day for a minimum period of 8 weeks prior to study enrolment
- 11 Patients in the control group were not yet receiving ART.
L2013: In both groups of patients, artesunate-amodiaquine 200/600 mg was given daily for three days. Measurement of drug concentrations occurred between 0 and 96 hours after the final dose. Pharmacokinetic parameters were determined using noncompartmental analysis.
L2014: ## Blood sampling:
L2015: Samples for the determination of artesunate plasma concentrations were collected according to the following schedule: predose (0h) on the third day, and 0.5 h, 1 h, 1.5 h, 2h, 3h, 4h, 6h, 8h, 10h, 12h, 24h, 48h, 72h, and 96h after the 3rd and last dose of artesunate-amodiaquine.

T1/2 (h)	1.6 (0.8)	3.2 (1.4)
L2023: Tmax is presented as median
L2024: The authors reported an increase in artesunate AUC0-96 in patients receiving NVP compared to those not on ART (105 versus 69 \mug-L/hr; respectively; p-value = 0.02) and no statistically significant difference in the DHA AUC0-96 between the two groups (603 vs 883 \mug-L/hr, p value = 0.19). There was no statistically significant difference in the Cmax between the two groups of patients for both artesunate and DHA (p-value = 0.15). Notably the variability is very high for Cmax and AUC for both AS and DHA in the control group of patients.
L2025: ## **Reviewer's Comments:**
L2026: - It is to be noted that the authors evaluated the effect of NVP on the PK of oral artesunate. Oral artesunate is not approved in the US. NVP reduced mean DHA AUC by 68% and mean DHA Cmax by 59%. This reduction is likely to result in the loss of efficacy of artesunate. Based on this reduction of systemic exposure observed in this study, IV Artesunate should not to be given with NVP. The package insert for IV Artesunate should include language recommending that NVP should not be coadministered with IV Artesunate.
L2027: ## Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics of Intravenous Artesunate During Severe Malaria Treatment in Uganda
L2028: Open Access Journal: http://www.malariajournal.com/content/11/1/132
L2029: Pauline Byakika-Kibwika, Mohammed Lamorde, Jonathan Mayito, Lillian Nabukeera, Harriet Mayanja-Kizza, Elly Katabira, Warunee Hanpithakpong, Celestino Obua, Nadine Pakker, Niklas Lindegardh, Joel Tarning, Peter J de Vries and Concepta Merry
L2030: The aim of this study was to assess the pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of IV artesunate in Ugandan adults with severe malaria. Fourteen adults with severe malaria, admitted to Mulago National Referral Hospital, Kampala, Uganda were enrolled in this trial. All participants received intravenous artesunate at a dose of 2.4 mg/kg at time 0, 2.4 mg/kg at 12 hours and 2.4 mg/kg/daily until they could tolerate oral therapy.
L2031: ## Blood sampling:
L2032: Blood for artesunate (AS) and dihydroartemisinin (DHA) assays was drawn after the initial dose of artesunate at 0 (pre-dosing), 5, 10, 15, 30, 45 minutes, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8- and 12-hours post-dosing.
L2033: ## Drug analysis
L2034: A validated liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry analytical method for determination of artesunate, and DHA was developed.
L2035: ## Results:
L2036: Pharmacokinetic parameters of AS and DHA are presented in Table 18-22. Following IV AS administration, the Cmax for AS and DHA were observed rapidly post-dose administration indicating rapid conversion of AS to DHA. Artesunate was cleared from circulation rapidly while DHA had a slightly longer elimination T_{1/2}. Although the peak concentration of AS was higher than that of DHA, the AUC of DHA was more than four times that of AS. The pharmacokinetic parameters observed in the present study are similar to findings from previous studies in malaria patients.

L2039: ## Clinical response
L2040: All study participants tolerated IV AS very well and reported very rapid recovery from symptoms and ability to take oral medication after 24 hours. No immediate adverse events were recorded. The median (range) parasite clearance time was 17 (8–24) hours. Individual plots of parasite density against time post start of treatment are shown in Figure 18-7. Parasite clearance time was comparable to that from a previous study and shorter than a median of 66 hours from other studies.

L2044: ## 18.4 Pharmacometrics review of the proposed artesunate dosing in pediatric patients
L2045: ## Introduction
L2046: The Applicant relied on published literatures of population pharmacokinetics (PK) of parenteral artesunate (intravenous or intramuscular injections) to support the proposed artesunate dose modification in patients weighing &lt;20 kg. Table 18-23 summarizes population PK studies that were independently reviewed. A brief review of the studies follows.

L2049: ## Population Pharmacokinetics of Intramuscular artesunate in African children with severe malaria (Hendriksen ICE et al., 2013)
L2050: The published article by Hendriksen ICE et al., (2013)1 describes population PK of artesunate and its metabolite dihydro-artemisinin in 70 Tanzanian children of ages 6 months to 11 years. All the children had severe malaria and received a 2.4mg/kg intramuscular injection of artesunate at 0, 12, and 24 hours. PK sampling was sparse as shown in Table 18-23. Population PK modeling using sparsely collected samples may limit identification of covariates for model parameter as information provided by the data may not be enough to characterize between subject variability (BSV) of the parameters. For the case of artesunate and dihydro-artemisinin, the 5 sparsely collected samples may be enough to characterize BSV as the 2 compounds have short half-life and follows a 1-compartment disposition kinetics.
L2051: Blood samples were adequately handled to prevent ex-vivo hydrolysis and degradation of artesunate. Artesunate is an unstable compound which, at room temperatures, undergoes hydrolysis to dihydro-artemisinin ex-vivo, and if hemolysis occurs, degrades upon contact with exposed heme. Proper sample collection tubes, immediate separation of plasma and cold storage, ensures that correct plasma exposures of artesunate and dihydro-artemisinin were assayed. The bioanalytical assay method was adequately developed and validated as reported by Hanpithakpong W. et al., (2008)2.
L2052: Population pharmacokinetic modeling followed appropriate pharmaco-statistical principles. One and 2-compartment disposition models for artesunate and dihydro-artemisinin were tested, and one compartment model adequately described the data. Principles of allometry were considered as weight was included as a covariate on clearance and volume parameters. Despite a large percent of data falling below the limit of quantitation (BLQ), omission of this data did not cause model misspecification. The final model adequately predicted the probability of BLQ data. Stepwise covariate model building was used with forward inclusion of covariates at the significance level of \alpha = 0.05 and backward elimination at the significance level of \alpha = 0.001. Model building was guided by graphical analysis of goodness of fit, visual predictive check, and physiological plausibility of covariates and precision of parameter estimates (Parameter uncertainties). In addition to body-weight, hemoglobin was the only other identified covariate; Dihydro-artemisinin clearance increased by 10% per unit (g/dL) decrease of hemoglobin. Assessment of clearance maturation did not identify age as a covariate for clearance. Based on visual predictive check, the final model described the data well and was suitable for prediction of exposures at different doses of artesunate.
L2053: The population PK model was used to simulate artesunate and dihydro-artemisinin exposure in children with different body-weights from 6-25 kg. The simulations indicated that at 2.4 mg/kg dosing, children with body weights between 6 – 10 kg had a mean reduction of 20.4% in dihydro-artemisinin exposure compared to children with body-weight between 21-25 kg. This implies that patients with a body weight between 6-10 kg require a 20% higher dose to match total drug exposure over time to heavier children weighing 21-25 kg. Hendriksen ICE et al., proposed weight-band based dosing as indicated in Table 18-24.
L2054: **Table 18-24. Proposed body weight-adjusted dosing regimen for IM artesunate.**

L2056: ## **Population Pharmacokinetics of Intravenous artesunate (Zaloumis SG et al., 2014)**
L2057: The published article by Zaloumis SG et al., (2014)3 describes the population pharmacokinetics of dihydro-artemisinin in adults and children with severe malaria after intravenous administration of artesunate. Patient data (adult and children) were obtained from 6 different studies with diverse study designs and dosing schemes4–9. The PK data were pooled from the different studies for pharmacokinetic analysis. Patients received either intramuscular (I.M), intra-rectal (I.R), or intravenous (I.V) artesunate depending on study specific designs and dosing schemes. Only PK data following I.V artesunate were used for population PK modeling; this included I.V artesunate data collected in second periods for cross-over trials.
L2058: In all studies blood samples for PK analysis were intensively sampled at times that were specific for each study. Methods for sample handling and bioassay were different among the studies as indicated in table 1. In general, samples were appropriately collected and handled to prevent ex-vivo degradation of artesunate in all the studies.
L2059: Appropriate pharmaco-statistical steps were followed in developing the population PK model. Due to limited artesunate concentration data, only dihydro-artemisinin concentrations were analyzed. The model assumed bolus administration of dihydro-artemisinin instead of modeling conversion of artesunate to dihydro-artemisinin; this was a reasonable strategy since maximum concentration of dihydro-artemisinin was observed within minutes of I.V artesunate. The structural model consisted of one-compartment disposition kinetics, allometric scaling volume of distribution, and a sub-model for allometrically scaled age-dependent maturation of enzymatic clearance. The sub-model for maturation of enzymatic clearance is supported by similar practice in literature where by a similar sub-model has been used to describe maturation of UGT mediated morphine clearance. The sub-model was also supported by a significant decrease in the objective function (\Delta OFV = 9.3). Ignoring BLQ samples did not cause model misspecification, and BLQ samples could be predicted by the final model.
L2060: In a separate sensitivity analysis, PK data collected in period 2 of cross-over trials were excluded. This resulted in identifying baseline hemoglobin and body temperature as covariates for clearance. Dihydro-artemisinin clearance increased by 4% per unit (g/dL) decrease of hemoglobin and increased by 7% per unit increase in body temperature.
L2061: The final population PK model was used to simulate dihydro-artemisinin exposure after I.V 2.4 mg/Kg to each body weight from 6-25 kg (1-kg increments). A virtual pediatric population for simulation was created by sampling age from weight-dependent uniform distributions. It was found that children 6 – 10 kg had 13.7% less exposure compared with children 21-25 Kg. Zaloumis SG et al., further simulated exposure using adjusted dosing regimens proposed by Hendriksen ICE et al. The I.M dosing regimens proposed by Hendriksen ICE et al., were found to be applicable to I.V artesunate as they resulted into similar exposures in all weight bands as shown in Figure 18-8.
L2062: **Figure 18-8.** Simulated total first dose exposures (AUC_{0-12h}) of DHA after the standard 2.4 mg/kg dosing in children at different body weights (black) and the adjusted dosing regimen proposed in Hendriksen et al. (red).
L2063: Source: Zaloumis et al., (2014), CPT Pharmacometrics Syst. Pharmacol, issue 3, e145, page 5.
Solid circles represent the median, and the error bars indicate the 25th and 75th percentiles of the 1,000 simulations at each body weight. The dashed line is the median exposure for the 25 kg weight group after the standard 2.4 mg/kg dose. DHA, dihydroartemisinin
L2064: ## Reviewer's Analysis
L2065: In both studies, data collection, handling, bioassay and pharmacokinetic modeling were appropriately performed. However, the method used by Zaloumis et al. to create the virtual population is not appropriate as it eliminates correlation between age and body weight within the weight bands. For this reason, the reviewer performed simulations investigate of the effect of weight and age on dihydro-artemisinin exposure.
L2066: Based on the proposed weight-based dosing of artesunate, the reviewer performed an independent simulation of dihydro-artemisinin exposure in children 6-25 kg using the final population PK model developed in a sensitivity analysis by Zaloumis SG et al. This model was selected as it describes dihydro-artemisinin PK in severe malaria patients before convalescence. A virtual pediatric population was created from the CDC growth charts (https://www.cdc.gov/growthcharts/percentile_data_files.htm). The charts for boys and girls (0-36 months and 0-204 months) were used to create 10,000 individuals through bootstrap resampling with replacement. Weights and heights of the individuals were obtained as follows: For each individual in the bootstrapped dataset, the 3rd and 97th percentiles (of weight or height) were transformed to normality (Box-cox transformation). Then a value within the two values was randomly sampled and back transformed to obtain a weight or height for individuals. The resulting virtual population had 3rd and 97th percentiles of weight-by-age (or height-by-age) that were similar to the CDC growth charts. Hemoglobin values were sampled from a normal distribution with mean and standard deviation of 8.5 g/dL and 2.4g/dL respectively in children with severe malaria (These parameters were reported by Kremsner et al., (2016).

Figure 2 shows the distribution of dihydro-artemisinin exposure by weight-based dosing (3 mg/kg for children 2 – 20 kg and 2.4 mg/kg for children 21 – 25 kg). The figure shows that the proposed dosing provides approximately 20% - 70% over-exposure in children 6-10 kg compared to children with 20-25 kg body weight. Children less than 6 kg had up to twice over-exposure compared to children 21-25 Kg. The simulated over-exposure is a result of age-dependent enzyme maturation effect in the simulation model. Inclusion of maturation effect in the model implies that neonates and infants have immature UGT enzymes (UGT1A9 and UGT2B7) and therefore lower clearance per body weight compared to older children. It is not clear at which age the UGT1A9 enzyme expression/activity in infants and children reaches adult level. Some literature suggests that maximal adult abundance is already reached between 4 months and 1.5 years after birth for UGT1A9 and UGT2B710-13. Other literature suggests that protein abundance of UGT1A9 and UGT2B7 reaches half-maximal by 8.2 and 2.6 years respectively14. The CDC growth chart indicates that pediatric subjects weighting 6 kg to 10 kg are in the age range of 3 month to 1 year. The minimum age in the pediatric dataset analyzed by Zaloumis was 6 months. Due to uncertainty in the age at which adult UGT1A9 activity is attained, it is unclear whether the age-dependent enzyme maturation model in Zaloumis's analysis is justified. Nevertheless, considering the potential for over-exposure with longer than 6-month enzyme maturation time, the reviewer performed simulations to quantify over-exposure predicted by the age-dependent maturation model. Although the model was developed to
L2067: describe growth and maturation of renal function22, it was used in this analysis to describe growth and maturation of total clearance of DHA. This choice is based on observation of similarity between time course of GFR-maturation and time course of clearance-maturation of drugs metabolized by UGT enzymes23. Furthermore, in population PK analyses by Hendriksen et al., and Zaloumis et al., the maturation model consistently showed improvement in model fit (although not statistically significant) over the allometric model. In addition to the empirical age dependent maturation model, the reviewer also assessed a physiological based DHA hepatic clearance model. The model was derived from literature data on invitro DHA metabolism, age dependent expression of UGT 1A9 relative to adult expression, liver microsomal protein quantity, hepatic blood flow, and plasma albumin binding. However, the model was not useful for extrapolation of exposure in neonates as it was unable to predict DHA clearance in adults: The model derived hepatic clearance in adults (V_{max} = 177 pmol/min/mg protein, K_m = 90 \muM) was about 10 times lower than the estimate by Zaloumis et al (0.073L/h/Kg vs 0.866L/h/Kg).
L2068: Taking age-dependent maturation effect into account, we performed simulation of dihydroartemisinin exposure based on studied artesunate dose of 2.4 mg/kg for all children 2 – 25 kg. As shown in figure 3, based on this simulation, children 6-10 kg are 5% - 40% over exposed compared to children 21 – 25 kg while children less than 6 kg (neonates and infants less than 3-month-old) were up to 90% over-exposed. We also simulated dihydroartemisinin exposure using a model without maturation effect. The results are shown in figure 4. As indicated in this figure, patients weighing 2-10 Kg have on average 25% less exposure compared to patients weighing 21-25Kg.
L2069: Artesunate dosing recommendation in infants depends on whether age-dependent maturation of UGT enzyme is considered. Hendriksen et al. ignored age-dependent maturation effect, and as a result recommended increasing artesunate dose in children 6 – 20 kg. However, our simulation indicates that, if age-dependent maturation is considered, artesunate dose of 2.4 mg/kg results into comparable dihydroartemisinin exposure in children 10 - 25 kg while children 2 – 9 kg are over-exposed.
L2070: 22 Hayton, W. L. Maturation and growth of renal function: Dosing renally cleared drugs in children. *AAPS PharmSci* **2**, 22–28 (2000)
L2071: 23 Anderson, B. J. &amp; Holford, N. H. G. Tips and traps analyzing pediatric PK data: Tips and traps. *Pediatr. Anesth.* **21**, 222–237 (2011)

The bar inside the box represents the median at its associated value, whiskers represent 1.5 times the interquartile range, and closed circles represent outliers. L2081: **Conclusion:** The estimated total systemic exposure (AUC) of DHA in virtual patients is sensitive to the method used to create the virtual patient population. After accounting for the correlation between age and body weight within the weight bands, simulations considering the age-dependent maturation effect do not support increasing the AS dose from 2.4 mg/kg to 3 mg/kg in children 2 kg to 20 kg based on a DHA population PK meta-analysis.
L2082: ## 18.5 Clinical Microbiology Appendix
L2083: The nonclinical studies supporting mechanism of action, activity in vitro and in malaria animal models, drug resistance and cross-resistance are summarized.
L2084: ## 18.5.1 Mechanism of Action
L2085: The precise mechanism by which artesunate, DHA and other artemisinins exhibit activity against *Plasmodium* species is not clearly understood. Studies using various derivatives of artemisinin, suggest a role of endoperoxide bridge in exhibiting anti-plasmodial activity. There appears to be an effect on the generation of superoxide anions, lipid peroxidation and protein alkylation. There is variability in uptake of the drug by the different development forms of the parasite within the red blood cells (RBCs). Some of the studies supporting the mechanism of action of artesunate, DHA, as well as artemisinin, including potential parasite targets, are summarized.
L2086: ## 18.5.1.1 Role of the endoperoxide bridge structure and activation by heme
L2087: Brossi *et al.* (Brossi et al. 1988) reported that artemisinin, DHA and arteether (with endoperoxide bridge) are more active (\ge 142-fold) than their deoxy derivatives (without the endoperoxide bridge) against the D6 strain of *P. falciparum*. Similar observations were reported in other studies. For example, Avery *et al.* (Avery et al. 1993) reported that the relative activity of the analogs of artemisinin was 1000- and &gt;10,000-fold higher compared to their deoxy derivatives against the D6 and W2 strains of *P. falciparum*; Mehra and Bhasin (Mehra and Bhasin 1993) reported that DHA was &gt;500-fold more active than deoxy-DHA against the FCD-3 strain of *P. falciparum*. Overall, the studies show that the endoperoxide bridge structure is important for the activity of artemisinins, including DHA.
L2088: The endoperoxide bridge is activated by heme resulting in the generation of free radicals, protein alkylation and cell death (Meshnick et al. 1993), (Olliaro et al. 2001). The activation of artemisinin and its derivatives appears to be due to binding with hemin released from hemoglobin in the food vacuole; the binding affinity correlated with the anti-plasmodial activity in vitro (Paitayatat et al. 1997).
L2089: Wang *et al.* (Wang et al. 2015) reported that activation of artemisinins occurred inside the parasites and not in the uninfected RBCs; the killing effect was up to 100-fold higher at the later stages (trophozoite and schizonts) than the early ring stages. At the early ring stage, activation relied primarily on the parasite's heme biosynthesis pathway and at latter stages on hemoglobin digestion; the biosynthesis of heme was lower than that released by hemoglobin digestion.
L2090: ## 18.5.1.2 Effect on oxidative stress and mitochondrial electron transport system
L2091: ## • Oxidative stress
L2092: The enzymes important in the oxidation of reactive oxygen radicals, which are by-products of metabolism and can damage nucleic acids, proteins and lipids include glutathione peroxidase (GSH-PX) and glutathione reductase (GR) in *Plasmodium* parasites as well as the host-derived superoxide dismutase (SOD) and catalase (Cat) enzymes.
L2093: The oxidative stress within the RBCs infected with the K1 strain of *P. falciparum* is increased compared to the uninfected RBCs (Ittarat et al. 2003). Pan et al. (Pan, Lin, and Zhang 1989) reported that in the presence of artesunate (concentration not specified), the activity of the antioxidant enzymes (SOD, Cat, GR, and GSH-PX) was similar in uninfected and *P. falciparum* (Hainan strain) infected RBCs. However, in another experiment, the exposure of RBCs to artesunate (961 ng/mL) resulted in ~2-fold increase in free radicals and H2O2 production in infected RBCs compared to uninfected RBCs. The activation of artemisinin by Fe2+/heme appears to be essential to release free radicals and other intermediates. Similar observations were reported by Ittarat et al. (Ittarat et al. 2003); significantly lower activity of GSH, Cat and GSH-Px enzymes was reported in parasitized RBCs, compared to uninfected RBCs, at DHA concentrations of 0.5 and 1.0 ng/mL.
L2094: Krungkrai and Yuthavong (Krungkrai and Yuthavong 1987) reported that with an increase in O2 from 3% to 30% in cultures of the K1 strain of *P. falciparum*, the artesunate 50% inhibitory concentration (IC50) values decreased from 0.11 ng/mL to 0.02 ng/mL. Synergistic activity in vitro was observed when artesunate was tested in the presence of the oxidant drugs e.g., miconazole or doxorubicin. However, the reducing agents [e.g., dithiothreitol (DTT)] as well as antioxidants (e.g., \alpha-tocopherol, sodium ascorbate, reduced glutathione, and catalase) that scavenge free radicals decreased the activity of artemisinin. Overall, the study suggests that artesunate and artemisinin act through generation of oxidative stress.
L2095: Similar observations were reported by another group of investigators (Meshnick et al. 1989) using FCR3 strain of *P. falciparum* and different antioxidants (sodium ascorbate and reduced glutathione). The antimalarial activity was determined by incorporation of 3H-hypoxanthine. The erythrocytic forms of the parasites were cultured in the presence or absence of antioxidants and various concentrations of artemisinin. The results show that ascorbic acid increased the artemisinin IC50 values suggesting that the artemisinin activity in the presence of antioxidants, is reduced.
L2096: It is known that genetically variant RBCs from patients with \alpha-thalassemia [\alpha-thal-1/\alpha-thal-2] exhibit an increase in the activity of antioxidant enzymes as well as impaired growth of *P. falciparum*. Yuthavong et al. (Yuthavong et al. 1989) reported decreased in vitro activity of artesunate and chloroquine when measured using genetically variant RBCs compared to RBCs from healthy volunteers infected with the synchronized cultures (containing schizonts) of the K1 strain of *P. falciparum*. However, the activity of pyrimethamine was not altered by the
L2097: source of RBCs. The study shows that an increase in the activity of antioxidant enzymes within the RBCs, which would lead to a decrease in oxygen radicals, can decrease the activity of artesunate.
L2098: Senok *et al.* (Senok et al. 1999) reported 2- to 3-fold increase in artemether IC50 values when in vitro sensitivity testing was conducted using thalassemic RBCs compared to normal RBCs infected with synchronous cultures (0.5% parasitemia, 0.4% hematocrit and predominantly schizont stage) of the FC27 (CQ sensitive) strain of *P. falciparum*; drugs were added to cultures at 36 hours when the parasites were in the young trophozoite stage and the activity measured after 12 hours by microscopic examination of smears. For the K1 (CQ-resistant) strain, however, changes in IC50 were \le2-fold and not statistically significant. In the presence of vitamin E (antioxidant), the activity of artemether was decreased against both the FC27 and K1 strains; however, in the presence of riboflavin (pro-oxidant), significant increase in artemether activity was reported in both thalassemic and normal parasitized RBCs.
L2099: ## - **Mitochondrial electron transport system**
L2100: Krungkrai *et al.* (Krungkrai et al. 1999) reported that artemisinin, primaquine, and mammalian mitochondrial inhibitors (that target complexes I-IV of the respiratory system) inhibit oxygen consumption by both asexual and sexual stages of the *P. falciparum* parasites.
L2101: Wang *et al.* (Wang et al. 2010) reported that artemisinin induced rapid mitochondrial depolarization and increased production of reactive oxygen species by blood stage parasites of the *Plasmodium berghei* (rodent species); such an effect was observed in vitro at a concentration of 100 nM within 30 minutes as well as in vivo within 2 hours of drug administration. Addition of iron chelator (desferrioxamine) to *P. falciparum* parasites in culture, reduced mitochondrial electron transport chain activity, artemisinin-induced production of reactive oxygen species, and the sensitivity of the parasites to artemisinin.
L2102: ## **18.5.1.3 Effect on oxidation of lipids**
L2103: Pan *et al.* (Pan, Lin, and Zhang 1989) reported that exposure of parasitized RBCs to artesunate led to oxidation of lipids; this was based on the amount of lipid peroxidation product, i.e., malonyldialdehyde (MDA), in both uninfected and infected RBCs. The increase in the MDA content after exposure to artesunate was greater in infected RBCs compared to the uninfected cells. Similar observations were reported by Meshnick *et al.* (Meshnick et al. 1989) based on measurement of thiobarbituric acid-reacting substances (TBARS; products of lipid peroxidation); it is noted that the measurements of TBARS include malonyldialdehyde.
L2104: Meshnick *et al.* (Meshnick et al. 1989) reported that the formation of lipid hydroperoxides was inhibited when the uninfected RBCs were incubated with artesunate in the presence of maltol (an antioxidant), thereby suggesting that an increase in lipid peroxidation may be due to generation of free radicals. However, similar testing was not conducted using infected RBCs.
L2105: ## 18.5.1.4 Effect on protein alkylation and proteasome
L2106: ## - **Protein alkylation**
L2107: Asawamahasakda et al. (Asawamahasakda et al. 1994) reported binding of radiolabeled DHA and other artemisinin derivatives [arteether or Ro 42-1611 (arteflene)] to different subcellular fractions of the ring and trophozoite forms of FCR3 (chloroquine-resistant) and D6 (chloroquine-sensitive) *P. falciparum* strains. Parasites were exposed to radiolabeled drugs for 6 hours and radioactivity as well as heme activity measured in host cell free parasites in different fractions by SDS-PAGE. The results show higher arteether uptake compared to DHA and Ro 42-1611; binding to parasite proteins occurred by all 3 drugs. Heme activity was observed in one of the radiolabeled subcellular fractions, representing food vacuoles of the parasite. The drug-labeled proteins appear to be associated with parasite membranes and not with hemozoin of both trophozoite and ring stages of both strains of *P. falciparum*. No proteins were labeled in the uninfected RBCs. The deoxy derivative of ^{3}\text{H}-arteether did not alkylate any proteins.
L2108: Yang et al. (Yang, Asawamahasakda, and Meshnick 1993) reported that ^{3}\text{H}-DHA and artemisinin alkylate the human serum proteins. DHA alkylated the thiol and amino moieties of the human serum protein; thiol (such as iodoacetamide or N-ethylmaleimide) and amine (succinic anhydride or N-ethylmaleimide) blocking agents reduced the binding of ^{3}\text{H}-DHA to human serum by 15-58% under the experimental conditions tested. Protein alkylation by DHA appears to be iron dependent. Binding of artemisinin to human serum was increased in the presence of iron and hemin.
L2109: Bhisutthibhan et al. (Bhisutthibhan et al. 1998) reported that one of the proteins that binds to and is alkylated by ^{3}\text{H}-DHA is a 25 kDa protein, translationally controlled tumor protein (TCTP) homologue; studies were conducted using the FCR3 and 3D7 strains of *P. falciparum*. The intensity of the 25 kDa band was stronger in trophozoites compared to the ring forms as measured by western blot using polyclonal rabbit antibodies against the recombinant TCTP. Several bands were observed for both the ring and trophozoite forms suggesting cross-reactivity of the TCTP antibodies with proteins other than 25 kDa in size.
L2110: Artemisinin binds to sites which are important for TCTP function (Eichhorn et al. 2013). The authors speculate that artemisinin disturbs TCTP's cytokine-like function in humans leading to the drug's anti-inflammatory activity. Wang et al. (Wang et al. 2015) reported that of the 124 targets identified for artemisinins, using an alkyne-tagged artemisinin analogue coupled with biotin as a probe, one of the targets was TCTP. The TCTP protein, found in eukaryotes, including protozoa and mammalian cells, is a calcium binding protein involved in a variety of cellular activities such as apoptosis and histamine release. The role of TCTP, found in the cytoplasm and food vacuole of *P. falciparum*, is unclear. Additionally, the relationship of TCTP alkylation to parasite death is not known.
L2111: Ismail et al. (Ismail et al. 2016) reported alkylation of several protein targets in situ, of the 3D7 strain of *P. falciparum*, that are important for glycolysis, hemoglobin degradation, antioxidant
L2112: defense, and biosynthesis of DNA, protein, and lipids. These biological pathways are important for parasite function and survival.
L2113: ## • **Proteasome**
L2114: DHA induced retardation of growth of erythrocytic parasites of *P. falciparum* was associated with an accumulation of ubiquitinated proteins suggesting cellular stress response (Dogovski *et al.* (Dogovski et al. 2015)). Bridgford *et al.* (Bridgford et al. 2018) reported that DHA caused protein damage and inhibited proteasome function in the trophozoite and ring stages of the 3D7 strain of *P. falciparum*, leading to accumulation of unfolded/damaged and polyubiquitinated proteins, decreasing protein synthesis, activation of endoplasmic reticulum stress response, and parasite killing. Blocking protein synthesis with a translation inhibitor or inhibiting the ubiquitin-activating enzyme, E1, reduced the level of damaged polyubiquitinated proteins, and antagonized DHA activity.
L2115: ## **18.5.1.5 Effect on phosphatidyl-inositol-3-kinase**
L2116: Mbengue *et al.* (Mbengue et al. 2015) reported that DHA can inhibit phosphatidyl-inositol-3-kinase (*PfPI3K*) of *P. falciparum*. *PfPI3K* is exported from the endoplasmic reticulum to RBCs and important for generating phosphatidyl-inositol 3-phosphate (PI3P) as well as endocytosis of hemoglobin from the host and trafficking of hemoglobin within the parasite. Furthermore, increased (~2-fold) PI3P levels were observed in resistant strains compared to sensitive strains of *P. falciparum*.
L2117: ## **18.5.1.6 Effect on sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium adenosine triphosphatase**
L2118: Eckstein-Ludwig (Eckstein-Ludwig et al. 2003) *et al.* reported that artemisinin inhibits the SERCA orthologue (*PfATP6*) of *P. falciparum*; however, desoxyartemisinin, quinine, or chloroquine were ineffective. Chelation of iron by desferrioxamine abrogated the antiplasmodial activity of artemisinin and reduced inhibition of *PfATP6*. The inhibitory activity of artemisinin against *PfATP6* appears to occur in the cytosol compartment; imaging of parasites with BODIPY-thapsigargin (used for intracellular localization that promotes Ca^{2+} release by inhibiting the endoplasmic reticulum Ca^{2+}-ATPase) labeled the cytosolic compartment and was competed by artemisinin.
L2119: Wang *et al.* (Wang et al. 2015) identified 124 protein targets for artemisinins using an alkyne-tagged artemisinin analogue coupled with biotin as a probe; one of the targets was SERCA/*PfATP6*.
L2120: ## **18.5.1.7 Uptake of dihydroartemisinin by ring and trophozoite forms**
L2121: Gu *et al.* (Gu, Warhurst, and Peters 1984) reported 100- to 180-fold higher uptake of ^{3}H-DHA (12 nM i.e., 3.41 ng/mL) in vitro by RBCs infected with the Wellcome/Liverpool/West African strain of *P. falciparum* compared to the uninfected RBCs. DHA uptake appears to depend on the type of developmental forms present in culture. For example, in cultures containing predominantly the ring form, lower amounts of DHA accumulated within 30 minutes compared
L2122: to the cultures consisting mainly of the trophozoites. The uptake of ^{3}\text{H}-DHA by parasitized RBCs was slower initially (for about 2 hours) in cultures containing predominantly the ring forms compared to the late stage developmental forms; however, after 2 hours of incubation, the uptake of ^{3}\text{H}-DHA was similar and maintained at steady level up to ~20 hours in cultures containing either predominantly early or late developmental forms. It is noted, that these experiments were conducted in medium containing high glucose and low protein concentrations. The relevance of such finding to accumulation of DHA *in vivo* is unclear.
L2123: ## **18.5.1.8 Effect on ultrastructure**
L2124: Ellis *et al.* (Ellis et al. 1985) reported ultrastructural changes in *P. falciparum* after exposure to ^{3}\text{H}-DHA in vitro and in mice infected with *P. berghei*. For in vitro assessments, DHA (10^{-5} M i.e. 28.44 ng/mL) was added to *P. falciparum* infected RBC cultures with 11% parasitemia and incubated for 1 hour. The cells were fixed and the sections examined by electron microscopy. ^{3}\text{H}-DHA was localized in the parasite limiting and the digestive vacuole membranes of infected cells but not uninfected cells.
L2125: In mice infected with asexual stage parasites of *P. berghei* and treated with a single dose of artemisinin (10 mg/kg) on Day 4 post-infection, blood was collected at different time intervals to evaluate ultrastructural changes of the parasites. The changes observed include an increase in free and bound ribosomes as well as swollen endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria within 30 minutes of treatment. By 8 to 12 hours, the parasites' organelles were increasingly disorganized; perinuclear vacuolation without and aggregation of nuclear material within the nucleus was observed. After 12 hours, progressive disintegration of the parasite into membrane whorls and vacuoles occurred. By 24 hours, almost all the parasites had disappeared. The authors state that isolated organisms observed appeared either to have been unaffected by or to have recovered from drug treatment.
L2126: Maeno (Maeno et al. 1993b) *et al.* (1993b) reported alteration in morphology of the blood stage parasites of a Brazilian clone (ItG2) of *P. falciparum* parasites incubated with ^{3}\text{H}-DHA by electron microscopic/autography. Briefly, cultures were concentrated for schizonts and suspended to 5% parasitemia, 7% hematocrit; ^{3}\text{H}-DHA was added and cultures incubated for 1 to 4 hours and processed for electron microscopy and autoradiography. The results showed localization of silver grains (representing radiolabel) of DHA in approximately 70% of the food vacuoles (mainly in malaria pigment) and 40% in the mitochondria. No radiolabel was observed in other parts of the parasite or uninfected RBCs. Similar observations were made using radiolabeled artemisinin. Overall, the study suggests that the ultrastructural changes occur sooner (2 to 4 hours) than the reduction of parasite counts in culture.
L2127: Maeno *et al.* (Maeno et al. 1993a) reported similar observations in splenectomized and non-splenectomized nonhuman primates infected with *Plasmodium coatneyi* and treated with artesunate (for details see Section 18.5.2).
L2128: Presence of ring infected erythrocyte surface antigen (RESA) cells, without intracellular *Plasmodium* parasites, was reported in the blood of patients with uncomplicated or severe malaria treated with artesunate (Angus et al. 1997); Chotivanich *et al.* 2000 (Chotivanich et al. 2000). The clearance of artesunate exposed ring stage parasites appears to be due to pitting in the spleen, leading to removal of the intraerythrocytic parasite (Chotivanich et al. 2000); (Buffet et al. 2006). The RESA cells probably represent the once infected cells (Woodrow and White 2017).
L2129: ## 18.5.2 In vitro Activity
L2130: Methods for measurement of *in vitro* sensitivity of *Plasmodium* species are not standardized and limited to testing in research laboratories. The most common *Plasmodium* species used for *in vitro* testing is *P. falciparum* and the testing is based on blood stage parasites.
L2131: Meister (Meister et al. 2011) *et al.* reported that artemisinin is not active against the liver stage parasites of *Plasmodium yoelii*. Artemisinin class of drugs are not active against the exoerythrocytic (liver) stage parasites of *Plasmodium* species (Tilley et al. 2016).
L2132: Some of the studies supporting the activity of artesunate and DHA against different stages of erythrocytic parasites of *P. falciparum* and other *Plasmodium* species are summarized.
L2133: ## 18.5.2.1 Activity against the blood stage parasites
L2134: ## - *Plasmodium falciparum*
L2135: Several studies have reported the *in vitro* activity of artesunate and DHA against the blood stage parasites of several **laboratory strains** (Krungkrai and Yuthavong 1987); (Brossi et al. 1988), (Yuthavong et al. 1989), (Alin, Björkman, and Ashton 1990); (Shmuklarsky et al. 1993); (Skinner et al. 1996); (Fivelman et al. 1999); (Brockman et al. 2000); (Moreno et al. 2001); (Ramharter et al. 2003); (Fivelman, Adagu, and Warhurst 2004); (Teja-Isavadharm et al. 2004); (Li et al. 2007); (Vivas et al. 2008); (Lim et al. 2010); (Lelièvre et al. 2012); (Duffy and Avery 2013); (Zhang et al. 2019); Study Report WRAIR 0432-16-1 (Study Report WRAIR 0432-16-1 2009) and **clinical isolates** (Mehra and Bhasin 1993); (Bustos, Gay, and Diquet 1994); (Skinner et al. 1996); (Yang et al. 1997); (Wongsrichanalai et al. 1997); (Ringwald, Bickii, and Basco 1999); (Le 1998); (Wongsrichanalai et al. 1999); (Brockman et al. 2000); (Ramharter et al. 2003); (Lim et al. 2010); (Kaewpongsri et al. 2011); (Fall et al. 2015); (Chaorattanakawee et al. 2017); (Zhang et al. 2019) of *P. falciparum*; however, the method used for testing varied. Some of the variations in the tests include the concentrations of parasitized and uninfected RBCs used for culture, source of RBCs, duration of incubation, and the method used to determine inhibition of parasite growth for example incorporation of 3H-hypoxanthine, flow cytometry, or microscopic examination of the parasites (for details see Table 18-25 and Table 18-26).
L2136: The artesunate IC50 values against the laboratory strains and clinical isolates were ≤2.14 ng/mL and ≤2.69 ng/mL, respectively; the DHA IC50 values against the laboratory strains and clinical
L2137: isolates were \le 6.54 ng/mL and \le 3.98 ng/mL, respectively (for details see Table 18-25 and Table 18-26).
L2138: Some of the clinical isolates tested were from regions known to be resistant to different antimalarial drugs; for example, isolates from India were resistant to chloroquine, Philippines to chloroquine and amodiaquine, Vietnam to chloroquine, amodiaquine, mefloquine and sulfadoxine/pyrimethamine, China-Myanmar (Burma) and China-Lao PDR borders resistant to chloroquine, Thailand region stated to be resistant to mefloquine, and Cameroon and other parts of Africa to chloroquine. The activity of both artesunate and DHA appears to be similar against the sensitive and resistant strains of *P. falciparum*. Additionally, the activity of both artesunate and DHA was similar against primary as well as recrudescence isolates, from patients in Thailand, previously treated with mefloquine + artesunate.
L2139: Studies show that in the presence of antioxidants (which would lead to a decrease in oxygen radicals) as well as in the genetically variant RBCs (such as in patients with \alpha-thalassemia), the in vitro activity of artesunate is decreased (for details see Section 18.5.1).

Isolates obtained from non-immune travelers returning from Africa to France in 1993–1996 were tested; with the exception of 26 patients returning from Comoros and Madagascar, the patients were infected in Africa, mainly in Central and West African. Most of them had not taken a correct chemoprophylactic regime. Infected RBCs were diluted with uninfected RBCs in medium containing 10% human serum, if the parasitemia exceeded 0.5% to obtain an initial parasitemia of 0.1–0.5%. Testing performed by a simplified in vitro test (hematocrit 1.5%) and a semi-microtest (the infected erythrocytes diluted with fresh uninfected erythrocytes at a parasite density 0.2–0.5% and hematocrit 0.5%). The activity was measured by incorporation of 3H-hypoxanthine at 42 h. It is unclear if the results are based on the simplified or semi-microtest method.

L2152: ## • *Plasmodium vivax*
L2153: Few studies (Kaewpongsri *et al.*, 2011; Lu *et al.*, 2011; Fernández *et al.* 2014; Chaorattanakawee *et al.*, 2017) reported the activity of artesunate and/or DHA, by the schizont maturation or *Plasmodium* lactate dehydrogenase (pLDH) tests, against the clinical isolates of *P. vivax*. The artesunate and DHA IC50 values were ≤1.2 ng/mL and ≤1.02 ng/mL, respectively (for details see Table 18-27).
L2154: ## • *Plasmodium malariae*
L2155: Siswantoro *et al.* (Siswantoro et al. 2011) reported the activity of artesunate against trophozoite (n=17) and ring (n=7) stages of clinical isolates of *P. malariae* from Indonesia. The activity was measured by the schizont maturation test. The artesunate IC50 values were ≤1.1 ng/mL (for details see Table 18-27).
L2156: ## • *Plasmodium ovale*
L2157: Siswantoro *et al.* (2011) reported the activity of artesunate against trophozoite (n=1) and ring (n=4) stages of clinical isolate(s) of *P. ovale* from Indonesia. The activity was measured by the schizont maturation test. The artesunate IC50 value was ≤ 0.5 ng/mL (for details see Table 18-27).
L2158: ## • *Plasmodium knowlesi*
L2159: Fatih *et al.* (Fatih et al. 2013) reported the activity of artesunate and DHA against 1 laboratory strain and 9 clinical isolates of *P. knowlesi* from Malaysia. The activity was measured by pLDH assay. The artesunate and DHA IC50 values were ≤0.77 and 0.45 ng/mL, respectively (for details see Table 18-27).
