L2165: ## 18.5.2.2 Stage specificity
L2166: The activity of artesunate, DHA and other artemisinins may vary against different developmental forms of the asexual forms and gametocytes of the erythrocytic parasites; *P. berghei* or *P. falciparum* parasites were used for these studies.
L2167: ## - **Asexual stage parasites of *Plasmodium berghei* and *Plasmodium falciparum***
L2168: Janse *et al.* (Janse et al. 1994) reported that the activity of DHA and artesunate decreased in older cultures containing trophozoites compared to younger cultures containing the ring stage
L2169: of *P. berghei*. The activity of DHA was 3.7-fold and 6.3-fold higher than artesunate or artemisinin, respectively.
L2170: Skinner *et al.* (1996) reported the activity of DHA and other antimalarial drugs (artemisinin, artemether, or quinine) varied against different stages of the asexual parasites of 4 clinical isolates and 2 laboratory strains of *P. falciparum*. Briefly, synchronized cultures (2% parasitemia with &gt;90% rings; 1% hematocrit) were incubated for different time points (0 hour for ring stage; trophozoites 20-24 hours; schizonts 36 hours) with concentrations equivalent to 90% inhibitory concentration (IC_{90}) values of the drugs. The percent inhibition of parasite growth was measured by incorporation of ^3H-hypoxanthine added to the cultures at the same time as the drug and cultures incubated for 0-10 hours. Data for only one isolate were shown; however, the authors state that the activity against all isolates was similar. The results show a rapid inhibition of growth (&gt;80%) of all the 3 stages within 2 – 4 hours of exposure to DHA. Artemisinin inhibited &gt;90% rings and schizonts within 4 to 6 hours and 60% trophozoites within 10 hours; artemether was the least active compared to artemisinin and DHA. Quinine was more active against the schizonts than the ring and trophozoite forms of *P. falciparum*.
L2171: ## • **Gametocytes (transmission blocking activity)**
L2172: Mehra and Bhasin (1993) reported the activity of DHA against the gametocytes of the FCD-3 isolate of *P. falciparum*. Briefly, gametocytes enriched cultures were prepared by incubating cultures containing 1% parasitemia for 8 days in flow vessels; the number of asexual and sexual stage parasites were determined and gametocytes classified into stages I to V. Gametocyte enriched cultures were incubated with DHA for 48 hours (fresh drug containing medium added at 24 hours) and the drug free medium added at 48 and 72 hours. Blood smears were prepared at 48 and 96 hours and scored for various stages of gametocytes. Complete killing of gametocytes was reported at DHA and artemisinin concentrations of 100 ng/mL at 96 hours; gametocyte inhibition at 48 hours was lower than at 96 hours. The DHA IC_{50} and IC_{90} values, based on a 96-hour reading, were 6.6 and 19 ng/mL, respectively; the artemisinin IC_{50} and IC_{90} values were 13 and 22 ng/mL, respectively. Younger stages of gametocytes (I and II), compared to late stages (III – V) were more sensitive to the drugs.
L2173: Chotivanich *et al.* (Chotivanich et al. 2006) reported that artesunate was effective in reducing the infectivity of mosquitoes fed on mature gametocytes of a AMB 47 Thai isolate incubated with the drug for 24 hours prior to mosquito feeding; the artesunate IC_{50} and IC_{90} values were 0.1 nM (0.04 ng/mL) and 0.4 nM (0.15 ng/mL), respectively.
L2174: Adjalley *et al.* (Adjalley et al. 2011) measured the activity of DHA against the gametocytes of a recombinant strain of *P. falciparum*; the DHA concentrations tested were 0.5-, 1-, and 5-times the IC_{50} value (24 nM i.e., 6.82 ng/mL) against the asexual parasites. The results show that DHA at \ge 0.5-times the IC_{50} value, against the asexual blood stage parasites, was effective in inhibiting growth of nearly 100% recombinant *P. falciparum* gametocytes at early stages (stage I-II). However, at 5-times the IC_{50} value, near complete inhibition of parasite growth was reported at gametocyte maturation stages I-III; 60% and 35% inhibition at stage IV and V gametocytes,
L2175: respectively, at the 5x concentration. A reduction in infectivity of the mosquitoes (*Anopheles stephensi*) was reported; a reduction in oocyte numbers in infected mosquitoes, by 25% was reported at the 1X, and 70% at the 5X DHA IC50 concentrations.
L2176: Lelièvre *et al.* (2012) reported that artesunate and DHA were &gt;200-fold less active (IC50s: 4163 ng/mL and 1012 ng/mL, respectively) against the mature gametocytes, compared to that against asexual forms of 3D7A strain of *P. falciparum* (Table 18-25),
L2177: Peatey *et al.* (Peatey et al. 2012) reported that artesunate inhibited the viability of the late stage gametocytes of a chimeric strain of *P. falciparum*, based on the production of ATP; the IC50 was 884.17 ng/mL.
L2178: Duffy *et al.* (2013) reported that artesunate and DHA IC50 values against the early stage (I-III) gametocytes of the transgenic NF54 Pfs 16-GFP strain, by confocal fluorescence microscopy, were 1.46 ng/mL and 5.20 ng/mL, respectively; whereas the artesunate and DHA IC50 values against the late stage (IV-V) gametocytes were 0.97 and 0.62 ng/mL, respectively.
L2179: ## 18.5.3 In vivo Activity (Animal Studies)
L2180: The suppressive activity of artesunate and/or DHA was measured in rodent and nonhuman primate malaria models including severe malaria animal models.
L2181: ## 18.5.3.1 Rodents infected with *Plasmodium berghei*
L2182: The suppressive activity of artesunate and/or DHA was reported in Swiss mice (Janse *et al.*, 1994) and Sprague-Dawley rats [(Yang et al. 1982); Janse *et al.*, 1994; (Li et al. 2003); (Xie et al. 2005); (Ohrt et al. 2014); (Study Report WRAIR-8C 2003; Study Report WRAIR-8F 2003; Study Report WRAIR-8G 2003)], infected with either synchronous or asynchronous parasites of the ANKA strain of *P. berghei*. The experimental design varied in different studies; in general, the animals were infected, either intravenously or intraperitoneally, with the parasitized RBCs and treatment with artesunate or DHA initiated after animals became parasitemic. Animals were followed for survival and/or parasitemia. Both artesunate and DHA were effective in improving survival and decreasing parasitemia. However, recrudescence was reported after treatment was discontinued (for details see Table 18-28).
L2183: One study (Yang *et al.*, 1982) reported that activity of artesunate was similar against chloroquine-sensitive and chloroquine-resistant strains.
L2184: **Table 18-28:** Suppressive activity of artesunate and DHA in mice and rats infected with the parasitized erythrocytes of *Plasmodium berghei*
L2185: Plasmodium species/ strain (Reference)	Study summary
P. berghei /CQ resistant and sensitive (strain not specified)
Mice (Strain not specified (Yang et al., 1982))	Mice were inoculated with 1.5 \times 10^7 PRBCs, IP, and artesunate administered IV or IM once daily for 3 days 24 h PI. The SD_{50} and SD_{90} doses were similar against the CQ sensitive (0.3 and 1.14 mg/kg) and resistant (0.5 and 1.14 mg/kg) strains.
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Swiss mice (Janse et al., 1994)	The activity of artesunate and DHA was measured in Swiss mice infected intraperitoneally with 10^5 PRBCs of the ANKA strain (Clone 8417) of P. berghei. Artemisinin was used as a comparator. Mice were treated, for 3 days, with artesunate or DHA (dissolved in Miglyol 812, a propylene glycol diester of saturated vegetable fatty) by the IM or SC routes once the parasitemia was between 1 and 3%. Animals were followed, up to Day 60 PI, for parasitemia at frequent intervals by thin blood films and flow cytometry. The results show that parasitemia in the vehicle group of mice increased to 40 to 50% by 12 days; 5 of the 10 mice died by Day 6 due to cerebral complications. High doses (\ge 40 mg/kg) of DHA, artesunate, and artemisinin were effective in curing mice up to Day 60 PI. At a lower dose of 10 mg/kg, parasitemia decreased, but increased rapidly in mice treated with artesunate or artemisinin; however, DHA was effective in curing 7 of the 15 mice up to Day 60. All mice treated with 0.1–10 mg/kg of artemisinin or artesunate were not cured. At a lower dose of 1 mg/kg of DHA or 1.4 mg/kg of artesunate, a significant delay in the development of parasitemia was reported in the first 4 days after drug administration; however, none of the mice were cured.
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Sprague-Dawley rats (Li et al., 2003)	Artesunate, at an IV dose of 191.2 \mu\text{M}/\text{kg} (73.4 mg/kg), was effective in clearing parasitemia with a PCT of 2.7 \pm 0.5 days (2 consecutive negative smears, one day apart) in rats (7 week-old) infected with 2 \times 10^7 PRBCs; treatment was administered daily for 3 days on Days 6, 7, and 8 PI and rats were followed to 21 days PI. Parasitemia increased gradually and peaked to ~30–40% in control group rats by Day 10–12; however, parasitemia gradually decreased by Day 15 and was cleared by Day 18–20. Lower doses (47.8 and 95.6 \mu\text{M}/\text{kg} i.e., 18.4 and 36.8 mg/kg) of artesunate were less effective and reduced parasitemia in 67% of the rats. Recrudescence was reported at all the doses within 5 to 8 days of initial parasite clearance by Day 11,
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Sprague-Dawley rats (Study Reports WRAIR-8C and 8F)	Experimental design was similar to that summarized above (Li et al., 2003). No mortality was observed in rats treated with 36.7 mg/kg artesunate once daily for 3 days. Parasitemia was cleared in 38.9% of rats. The PCT was 52.8 h in 50% of the rats. All animals showed recrudescence at Day 6.
PK parameters in infected rats were measured.DHA peak plasma concentration of 9717 \pm 858 ng/mL, at 0.17 \pm 0.04 h, were 5-fold higher than artesunate concentration. The ratio of DHA to artesunate was 5.39 of total dose.
After 1 or 2 h of IV administration, the concentration of DHA in plasma remained higher than artesunate.
The elimination half-life of artesunate was 0.36 h and DHA 0.74 h.
All 3 infected rats treated with 3 doses of 120 mg/kg artesunate had minimal thymic lymphocyte depletion; also, moderate diffused cortical depletion with marked to severe lymphocytolysis in the cortex was reported. In the non-infected rats, no lymphocyte depletion was reported however, cortical thymic depletion was observed.
L2186: Plasmodium species/ strain (Reference)	Study summary
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Sprague-Dawley rats (Study Report WRAIR-8G)	Experimental design similar to that summarized above (Li et al., 2003) except that rats with >5% parasitemia were treated with different doses of artesunate and the suppressive dose that reduced parasitemia by 50% and 100% (SD_{50} and SD_{100}, respectively) determined. The mortality in untreated rats was 16.3% whereas all the rats treated up to a dose of 240 mg/kg survived; however, 4 out of the 5 rats died at a higher dose of 480 mg/kg.
Parasitemia was cleared at doses of \ge 60 mg/kg. The SD_{50} and SD_{100} values were 16.3 mg/kg and 60 mg/kg, respectively. Recrudescence was reported in all animals with initial parasite clearance even at the dose of 240 mg/kg.
Based on the endpoints of the MTD, suppression and clearance, the therapeutic indices were calculated as 14.7 (240 mg/16.3 mg: 14.7) based on the SD_{50} data and 4 (240 mg/60 mg=4) for the SD_{100} data.
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Sprague-Dawley rats (Xie et al., 2005)	Experimental design similar to that summarized above (Li et al., 2003) except that different doses of artesunate were tested and the suppressive doses that reduced parasitemia by 50% and 100% (SD_{50} and SD_{100}, respectively) were determined. The mortality in untreated rats was 16.7% whereas all the rats treated up to a dose of 240 mg/kg survived; however, 4 of 5 rats died at dosage of 480 mg/kg.
Parasitemia was cleared at doses of \ge 60 mg/kg, and clearance time ranged between 48 and 59 h. The SD_{50} and SD_{100} values were 7.4 mg/kg and 60 mg/kg, respectively. Recrudescence was reported in all animals with initial clearance even at the MTD of 240 mg/kg.
P. berghei / ANKA strain
Sprague-Dawley rats (Ohrt et al., 2014)	Rats were inoculated with P. berghei-infected rat blood and different doses of IV artesunate administered for 3 days, when parasitemia of >4% was achieved. PCT was between 48-59 h in all rats treated with artesunate at doses of \ge 60 mg/kg.
The authors state that P. berghei is a self-curing parasite in rats: parasitemia spontaneously diminishes beginning approximately Day 12 after inoculation. In the control group of animals, parasitemia increased to approximately 10% on Day 8, reached a peak (10 and 35%) on Day 11, and then declined (self-cleared) to undetectable levels over the next seven to 11 days in the surviving animals; a 16.7% mortality from malaria was observed.
L2187: ## 18.5.3.2 Nonhuman primates infected with *Plasmodium coatneyi* and *P. cynomolgi*
L2188: The suppressive activity of artesunate was measured in splenectomized and non-splenectomized rhesus monkeys infected with *P. coatneyi* [(Maeno *et al*., 1993a); (Study Report WRAIR-8P 2004; Study Report WRAIR-8Q 2001)] and *P. cynomolgi* (Ohrt *et al*., 2014). Treatment with artesunate was initiated after animals became parasitemic. The results show that artesunate was effective in rapidly reducing parasitemia; however, recrudescence occurred.
L2189: Maeno *et al*. (1993a) reported that in monkeys infected with *P. coatneyi*, artesunate treatment altered the morphology of the parasites. Within 4 hours of treatment, enlargement of the food vacuoles and cellular degeneration with ribosomal clumping of the trophozoites was observed. By 24 hours, the sequestration of the parasitized RBC in the cerebral microvessels was reduced to &lt;0.1% in both splenectomized and non-splenectomized animals; the sequestration of
L2190: parasitized RBCs in cerebral microvessels was also reported. By electron microscopy, multiple electron-dense knobs protruding from the membranes of parasitized RBCs were observed in untreated animals; the knobs cytoadhered to the endothelial cells in cerebral microvessels and occasional endothelial cells exhibited swelling. In artesunate treated animals, very few parasitized RBCs in the cerebral microvessels and no electron-dense knobs were observed; however, some swollen endothelial cells were observed in the brain of treated animals. The authors state that cerebral edema could not be conclusively demonstrated, and cerebral hemorrhages were not seen either before or after treatment.
L2191: The Applicant conducted a study (Study Reports WRAIR-8P and 8Q) in splenectomized monkeys infected with ~5 x 106 parasitized RBCs; spleen was removed 4 weeks prior to challenge. Untreated animals developed parasitemia within 1-2 days of inoculation that increased to 106 parasites/µL (17-28% parasitemia) by Days 7-8. Early symptoms included onset of lethargy, anorexia and bloody urine; the infection was uniformly fatal if left untreated. Treatment with IV artesunate at doses of 4, 6, and 8 mg/kg for 3 days reduced mortality; such an effect was dose-dependent. Mortality was lower in artesunate treated animals (10% at doses between 4-8 mg/kg; overall 25%) compared to that in quinine (33%) treated animals. The most effective dose was 8 mg/kg and parasites were cleared in all the 3 monkeys treated with the 8 mg/kg dose of artesunate; at lower doses of 4 and 6 mg/kg, 66 and 50% reduction in parasitemia was observed. The parasite clearance time was shorter (~2.4 days) in artesunate treated animals compared to quinine (5.6 days). However, recrudescence occurred in all animals.
L2192: Similar observations were made in non-splenectomized monkeys with uncomplicated malaria.
L2193: For details see Error! Reference source not found..
L2194: Error! Reference source not found.: **Suppressive activity of artesunate in nonhuman primates infected with the parasitized erythrocytes of *Plasmodium coatneyi* or *Plasmodium cynomolgi***
L2195: Plasmodium species (Reference)	Study summary
Plasmodium coatneyi
P. coatneyi
Rhesus monkeys (Maeno et al., 1993a)	
Rhesus monkeys (n=13) in Groups 1-3, were infected IV with whole blood containing 2.2 x 108 PRBCs (13% ring forms and 87% schizonts); animals in Group 4 were infected with 1.2 x 108 PRBCs (77% ring forms and 23% schizonts). Three additional monkeys were uninfected and used for immunohistochemical staining of tissues.
Group 1: n=3 (splenectomized 2 days PI, treated 7 days PI with 3 doses: artesunate loading dose- 3.3, followed by 2 doses of 1.7 mg/kg, 8 and 24 h later).
Group 2: n=3 (no splenectomy, dosings same as Group 1).
Group 3: n=2 (no splenectomy, treated with a single dose of 3.3 mg/kg on Day 9 PI).
Group 4: n=5 (infected with lower concentration of the inoculum: untreated).
Prior to treatment, parasitemia ranged between 13.3-19.5%. Parasitemia rapidly decreased in all treated animals: In Groups 1 and 2 animals, parasitemia decreased to 2.2% and 0.1%, respectively; 50% reduction in parasitemia was between 3 and 5 h. In Group 3, parasitemia reduced to 10.6% at 3 h post-treatment with a single dose. Blood was collected for evaluating parasitemia for up to 24 h post-treatment.
L2196: Plasmodium species (Reference)	Study summary
Groups 1-3 animals were sacrificed within an hour of the last dose i.e., Days 8 or 9. Group 4 untreated animals sacrificed on Days 7-8. Tissues and blood samples were processed for light and electron microscopy.
The results show that in Group 1 and 2 animals, artesunate was effective in altering the morphology of the parasites, within 4 h of treatment, that include enlargement of the food vacuoles and cellular degeneration with ribosomal clumping of the trophozoites; by 24 h, the percentage of PRBCs sequestration in the cerebral microvessels was reduced to <0.1%. In group 3 animals, the rate of PRBC sequestration also was reduced to 2.0% within 3 h after administration of a single dose of artesunate. The sequestration of PRBC in cerebral microvessels was reported in Groups 1-3 animals.
The authors state that cerebral edema could not be conclusively demonstrated, and cerebral hemorrhages were not seen either before or after treatment; however, electron microscopy showed that untreated animals in Group 4, had multiple electron-dense knobs protruding from the membranes of RBCs; the knobs cytoadhered to the endothelial cells in cerebral microvessels and occasional endothelial cells exhibited swelling. In artesunate treated animals (Groups 1-3), very few PRBCs in the cerebral microvessels and no electron-dense knobs were observed; however, some swollen endothelial cells were observed in the brain of animals in Groups 1 and 2.
Immunohistochemical staining of tissues was performed for IgG, C3, CD36, thrombospondin (TSP), ICAM-1, and endothelial-leukocyte adhesion molecule-1 (ELAM-1). In the untreated animals (Group 4), IgG, C3, CD36, TSP, and ICAM-1 were observed in the cerebral microvessels from all five and ELAM-1 was found in 4 of 5 monkeys. In artesunate treated animals (Group 1), ELAM-1 was not detected in all three monkeys. However, IgG, C3, CD36, TSP and ICAM-1 were observed in cerebral microvessels from all eight monkeys in Groups 1-3. The brains from three uninfected monkeys were negative for IgG, C3, CD36, TSP, ICAM-1, and ELAM-1.

P. coatneyi
Rhesus monkeys
Severe malaria model
(Macaca mulatta)
(Study Report WRAIR-8P and 8Q)	
Four year old splenectomized monkeys (spleen removed 4 weeks prior to challenge) were infected with PRBCs (monkeys were infected with 1 mL of infected blood from a donor monkey with parasitemia of >50,000/\muL; about 5 x 106 RBC with 1% parasitemia); animals developed parasitemia within 1-2 days of inoculation that exceeded to 106 parasites/\muL (17-28% parasitemia) on Day 7/Day 8 and were symptomatic. Early symptoms included onset of lethargy, anorexia and bloody urine; the infection was uniformly fatal if left untreated. Moderate (<50% normal food intake) to moderately severe (<15% reduction of food intake) anorexia preceded other symptoms by 12 h. PCT90 and PCT100 were the primary endpoints.
IV artesunate, at doses of 4, 6, and 8 mg/kg administered for 3 days (when parasitemia reached ~25%), reduced mortality; such an effect was dose-dependent and parasites were cleared in all the 3 monkeys treated with the 8 mg/kg dose of artesunate; at lower doses of 4 and 6 mg/kg, 66 and 50% reduction, respectively, in parasitemia was observed. PCT was shorter (~58 hours) in animals treated with artesunate for 3 days compared to quinine (5.6 days). However, recrudescence occurred after 3 days in all animals. Mortality was lower in artesunate treated animals (10% at doses between 4-8 mg/kg; overall 25%) compared to that in quinine (33%) treated animals. The most effective dose was 8 mg/kg.
Similar observations were made in non-splenectomized monkeys with uncomplicated malaria that were treated with artesunate when parasitemia reached 3%.

Plasmodium cynomolgi
P. cynomolgi bastianellii	
Splenectomized monkeys were inoculated with 106 PRBCs; P. cynomolgi is fatal to splenectomized rhesus monkeys, if untreated. Artesunate at doses of 1 and 8 mg/kg/day, IV, for 3 days was effective in clearing
L2197: Plasmodium species (Reference)	Study summary
Rhesus monkeys (Ohrt et al., 2014)	parasitemia in between Days 1-3 post-treatment; however, recrudescence occurred between Days 7-11 days post-treatment in the monkeys treated with 1 mg/kg dose. At the higher dose of 8 mg/kg, parasitemia was cleared by Day 3, in 3 of the 4 monkeys and 1 animal died on Day 1 of treatment. Recrudescence was observed, on Days 11 and 12, in 2 monkeys and 1 animal was cured.
PCT=parasite clearance time; PI=post-infection; h=hours; PRBC=parasitized RBC
L2198: ## 18.5.3.3 Immunocompromised mice and monkeys infected with *Plasmodium falciparum*
L2199: ## - **Immunocompromised mice**
L2200: The activity of DHA was measured in immunocompromised [bg/bg xid/xid nu/nu (BXN)] mice infected with the parasitized RBCs (synchronized cultures) of the T24 strain of *P. falciparum* resistant to chloroquine and quinine (Moreno et al., 2001). The grafted mice had 85 to 99% human RBCs (HRBCs) in their peripheral blood and parasitemia was between 0.1% and 10%. Consistent and sustained parasite growth was observed that persisted as long as the HRBCs were regularly transfused and the immunomodulation maintained. Two-day oral treatment with chloroquine (mouse equivalents of the therapeutic regimens employed for human beings were used) was initiated when parasitemia was stable for at least 6 days and when ring forms were the predominant stage. Mice were followed for parasitemia on Giemsa stained thin blood smears and the parasite clearance time, parasite reduction rate after 48 hours of treatment, and effects upon different stages and parasite morphology assessed. Chloroquine was not effective in decreasing parasitemia; few if any, changes in the morphology of parasites or in the stage pattern were reported. Although a low percentage of pyknotic forms could be detected (11 to 16%), but the parasites still grew and developed normally over the following days. Mice were then treated orally with 50 mg/kg/day **DHA**, for 2 days. The results show that DHA was effective in decreasing parasitemia to below 0.005% within 24 hours; at 24 hours after initiation of treatment, pyknotic parasites (72%) and altered trophozoites (16%) were already predominant; however, small proportions of apparently healthy rings, trophozoites (1%), and schizonts (3%) were observed. Mice were cured within 2 days. At 48 hours, all of the rare forms still visible were pyknotic (for details see Table 18-29).
L2201: ## - **Aotus monkeys**
L2202: Ohrt et al. (2014) reported that artesunate dose of 8 mg/kg/day for 3 days administered on Day 5 post-infection, was effective in clearing parasitemia within 3-4 days after treatment initiation in Aotus monkeys infected with PRBCs of *P. falciparum*. Recrudescence was observed 3-9 days after initial parasite clearance (for details see Table 18-29).
L2203: ## 18.5.3.4 Rhesus monkeys infected with *Plasmodium knowlesi*
L2204: Yang et al. (1982) reported that in rhesus monkeys infected with PRBCs of *P. knowlesi*, IV artesunate (at 6, 8, and 12 mg/kg once daily for 3 days), administered when parasitemia
L2205: reached 22-30%, was effective clearing parasitemia within 16-20 hours (for details see Table 18-29).
L2206: **Table 18-29: Suppressive activity of artesunate in mice and nonhuman primates infected with the parasitized erythrocytes of *Plasmodium falciparum* or *Plasmodium knowlesi***
L2207: Plasmodium species/ strain (Reference)	Study summary
P. falciparum– mice and Aotus monkeys
P. falciparum/ T 24 strain resistant to chloroquine and quinine
Immunocompromised BXN (bg/bg xid/xid nu/nu) mice (Moreno et al., 2001)	BXN mice were infected IP with synchronized cultures of PRBCs (1 mL of 0.3% to 0.6% [parasitemia; 50% hematocrit) of the multidrug-resistant T24 (Thailand) strain. After infection, 1 mL of washed huRBC at 50% hematocrit was injected IP together with NIMP-R14 (MoAb to control neutrophils) every 3 to 4 days; dichloromethylene diphosphonate (to control macrophages) was administered every 4 to 5 days. The grafted mice had 85 to 99% huRBC in their peripheral blood and parasitemia was between 0.1 % and 10%. Consistent and sustained parasite growth was observed that persisted as long as huRBC were regularly transfused and the immunomodulation protocol continued. Two-day oral treatment with chloroquine (mouse equivalents of the therapeutic regimens employed for human beings were used) was initiated when parasitemia was stable for at least 6 days and when ring forms were the predominant stage. Mice were followed for parasitemia on Giemsa stained blood smears and the PCT, parasite reduction rate after 48 h of treatment, and effects upon each stage and parasite morphology were assessed. Chloroquine was not effective in decreasing parasitemia of the resistant T24 strain; few if any, changes in the morphology of parasites or in the stage pattern were reported. A low percentage of pyknotic forms could be detected (11 to 16%), but the strain still grew and developed normally over the following days. Mice were then treated orally with 50 mg/kg/day DHA, for 2 days. The results show that DHA was effective in decreasing parasitemia to below 0.005% within 24 h in both mice and fully disappeared within 2 days. At 24 h after initiation of the treatment, pyknotic parasites (72%) and altered trophozoites (16%) were already predominant; however, small proportions of apparently healthy rings, trophozoites (1%), and schizonts (3%) were still observed. At 48 h, all of the rare forms, still visible, were pyknotic.
P. falciparum/ FVO strain
Aotus monkeys (Ohrt et al., 2014)	Monkeys were inoculated with 5 \times 10^6 PRBCs of FVO strain of P. falciparum. FVO strain is generally fatal in Aotus monkeys. Parasitemia in all animals increased from approximately 2,000 parasites/mm3 on Day 4 to at least 50,000 parasites/mm3 on Day 5 PI. By Day 8, parasitemia in 3 control monkeys was between 600,000-800,000 parasites/mm3 and rescue therapy was administered. Artesunate at 8 mg/kg/day dose was administered for 3 days on Day 5 post-infection. Clearance of parasitemia was observed in all 3 animals, 3-4 days after treatment initiation. Recrudescence was observed 3-9 days after initial parasite clearance.
P. knowlesi - rhesus monkeys
P. knowlesi/ strain not specified
Rhesus monkeys (Yang et al., 1982)	Rhesus monkeys were infected with PRBC and artesunate administered IV, when parasitemia reached 22-30%, at 6, 8, and 12 mg/kg once daily for 3 days. Blood smears were examined every 4 h. Artesunate cleared parasitemia within 16-20 h after first IV injection.

L2208: ## 18.5.4 Drug combination
L2209: Few studies reported an interaction between artemisinins and antiretroviral drugs in vitro. For example, He *et al.* (He et al. 2010) reported antagonistic activity between artemisinins (artesunate, DHA, and artemether) and antiretroviral protease inhibitors (ritonavir, saquinavir, and indinavir), against the 3D7 (chloroquine sensitive) and Dd2 (multi-drug resistant) *P. falciparum* strains. However, another study (Mishra et al. 2010) reported synergistic activity between artemisinin and indinavir or nelfinavir against 3D7 and RKL303 (chloroquine resistant) *P. falciparum* strains. The reasons for differences in activity between the two studies are unclear.
L2210: ## 1.1.1. Drug Resistance
L2211: The nonclinical and clinical studies supporting a potential for development of resistance to artesunate, DHA, and other artemisinin derivatives are summarized.
L2212: ## 18.5.4.1 Nonclinical studies
L2213: - *In vitro**Laboratory strains and clinical isolates of P. falciparum*
L2214: Jiang (Jiang 1992) reported the development of resistance by the FCR3 strain of *P. falciparum* to artesunate; the artesunate IC50 values increased by about 3-fold after 13 days of exposure to the drug. However, such an effect was reversible after culture in drug-free medium.
L2215: Lim *et al.* (2010) reported lower in vitro sensitivity (i.e., higher IC50 values) of *P. falciparum* isolates from Western Cambodia (multi-drug resistant malaria region), to artesunate and other antimalarials drugs, compared to the isolates from Eastern Cambodia; there was a positive correlation between artesunate and mefloquine, chloroquine, as well as quinine based on the IC50 values. The IC50s for isolates from participants who failed artesunate + mefloquine therapy, i.e., recrudescence isolates (n=37), were 2-fold higher than those from patients (n=558) who were cured (P &lt; 0.001). Some of the isolates (n=851) were genotyped for single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) in four genes (*pfdhfr*, *pfcr*t, *pfmdr1*, and *pfatpase6*). The distributions of the SNPs differed between Eastern and Western Cambodia, suggesting different genetic backgrounds of the parasite populations. Although, there was no statistically significant correlation between increased *pfmdr1* copy numbers and increased artesunate IC50s, *pfmdr1* codon Y184F appeared to be associated with an increased artesunate IC50s (n=113), but only for Eastern Cambodia. For chloroquine, however, there was a significant association between the IC50 values and *pfmdr1* codon Y184F (n = 114) or *pfdhfr* codon I64L (n = 162). The clinical significance of these findings is not known.
L2216: Witkowski *et al.* (Witkowski et al. 2010) reported a selection of artemisinin tolerant line (F32-ART), after long-term artemisinin pressure *in vitro*, of the parasitized RBCs of the F32-Tanzania strain of *P. falciparum*. The F32-ART strain was tolerant to artesunate, although artemisinin was used for inducing drug pressure, but not to chloroquine. A subpopulation of ring stages was in
L2217: developmental arrest that survived the higher concentrations (7000-fold initial IC50) of the drug; no trophozoites or schizonts were observed. No mutations in the *PfATP6* gene and variations in the *pfdm1* copy number were observed; the transcriptomic modifications observed include an overexpression of heat shock and RBC surface proteins and the down expression of a cell cycle regulator and a DNA biosynthesis protein. Within 24-30 hours of removal of the drug, the ring stages returned to normal cell cycle and trophozoites and schizonts developed. No quiescent rings were observed in the parent strain under drug pressure. The authors suggest increased tolerance to artemisinin to be mediated by a quiescence mechanism.
L2218: Dogovski *et al.* (2015) reported that incubation of synchronized cultures of resistant *P. falciparum* isolates, from Pailin, Cambodia, with DHA was associated with lower levels of ubiquitinated proteins and delayed onset of cell death compared to the cultures of the sensitive strains.
L2219: Birnbaum *et al.* (Birnbaum et al. 2020) reported that inactivation of 8 proteins in the kelch13 defined compartment rendered the ring stage parasites resistant to artemisinin; however, adding wild type kelch13 rendered the resistant parasites fully sensitive to DHA. The kelch13 proteins were associated with endocytosis of hemoglobin from the host cell.
L2220: ## 18.5.4.2 *Plasmodium vivax* drug resistance markers
L2221: Chaorattanakawee *et al.* (2017) reported *P. vivax* multi-drug resistance (*Pvmdr1*) Y976F and F1076L mutations in 78% (17/23) and 83% (19/23) of the clinical isolates, respectively, from Cambodia; of the 23 isolates tested, 74% (n=17) were double mutants, 9% (n=2) single F1076L mutants, and 17% (n=4) wild type isolates. None had *Pvmdr1* amplification. The artesunate and DHA IC50 values were low (\le 1.23 ng/mL). The clinical relevance of these findings is not known.
L2222: ## - ***In vivo* (Animal studies):**
L2223: Studies in rodent and nonhuman primate malaria animal models demonstrate recrudescence/recurrence after discontinuation of treatment with artesunate, DHA or other artemisinin derivatives (for details see Section 18.5.2). A potential for development of resistance in malaria animal models was measured using *Plasmodium* species that infect rodents as well as *P. falciparum*.
L2224: ## 18.5.4.3 *Plasmodium* species infecting rodents - *Plasmodium berghei* and *Plasmodium yoelii*
L2225: Chawira *et al.* (Chawira, Warhurst, and Peters 1986) evaluated the development of resistance to artemisinin against the chloroquine-resistant NS strain of *P. yoelii*. Briefly, the TFW male mice were inoculated intravenously with 107 parasitized RBCs of the NS strain of *P. yoelii* and treated with a single subcutaneous dose of artemisinin (100 mg/kg). Serial passage was performed by inoculating mice with parasitized RBCs from the artemisinin treated mice in the previous passage. After 50 passages (Day 149), about 35-fold increase (11.5 mg/kg to 400 mg/kg) in artemisinin 90% suppressive dose (SD90) was observed. The resistance to artemisinin
L2226: was lost when the drug pressure was removed. However, the same level of resistance was observed by reapplying the drug pressure, in 3 passages. Resistance to artemisinin against the chloroquine-sensitive N strain of *P. berghei* was not observed when tested under similar experimental conditions.
L2227: Liu *et al.* (Liu and Ren 1987) reported the development of resistance by blood stage parasites of *P. berghei* to artesunate in mice; after 21 serial passages, the SD90 increased by 29-fold. Resistance remained stable until 80 passages.
L2228: ## o *Plasmodium falciparum*
L2229: Tyagi *et al.* (Tyagi *et al.* 2018) reported a potential for development of resistance by *P. falciparum* to artesunate in vivo, in humanized immunocompromised [NOD/SCIDIL-2Ry-/- (NSG)] mice, and ex vivo. Briefly, animals were administered clodronate-containing liposomes to induce modulation of innate defenses; chimerism with human RBCs was maintained at ~78% and monitored by flow cytometry. Mice were infected with asynchronous cultures of *P. falciparum* (Uganda Palo Alto Marburg (FUP/CB or PAM) strain and parasitemia monitored by Giemsa stained smears. Artesunate was administered IV as single-doses (2.4 to 240 mg/kg) or a 2-day regimen administered 2-days apart [2.4 mg/kg/injection (total dose 4.8 mg/kg) up to 80 mg/kg/injection]; doses were progressively increased as parasites were recovered. Parasitemia was monitored every 24 hours and allowed to recover to the pre-treatment level before administering the next higher dose.
L2230: The normal day-to-day fluctuation of parasitemia in 13 untreated mice was 18.3% (95% confidence interval was 12.5–27%); based on this, the threshold for resistance, was the dose at which parasitemia failed to drop &gt;27% the next day. The mean reduction in parasitemia of the PAM-sensitive strain, the day after a single dose administration, was 78.4 \pm 18.2\%; the sensitive response was defined as parasitemia greater than 60.2% that corresponded to the mean (1 SD).
L2231: A **single dose** of 0.15 mg/kg artesunate was established as the lowest effective dose; parasitemia was reduced within 24 to 48 hours and pyknotic parasites observed. However, recrudescence occurred by 72 hours post-treatment. After parasitemia recuperated back to pre-treatment levels (APC), the next higher dose of artesunate was administered. Artesunate resistant (ART-R) parasites were passed through 7 generations. The results show, an increase in the number of mice with ART-R parasites at increasing doses. Stability of the resistant parasites was observed in increasing number of mice at a dose of 60 mg/kg in the 4th generation parasites and 120 mg/kg in the 5th generation; in the 7th generation, resistance was stable, in all 6 mice (ART-R240) after sub-inoculation to mice treated with 240 mg/kg artesunate; this represents a 3200-fold decrease in sensitivity to artesunate in immunocompromised mice. The sensitive progenitors of the PAM strain, used as controls, remained sensitive at all doses for all the generations.
L2232: Studies for **2-day dosing** were started at a dose of 2.4 mg/kg with an ART-R30 strain (i.e., resistant at a single dose of 30 mg/kg). Resistance was observed after 1 APC. The parasites were
L2233: passaged through 4 generations and increasing doses administered until reproducible resistance at doses of 80 mg/kg (i.e., 160 mg/kg total) was observed.
L2234: The ex vivo sensitivity of the ART-R strains was measured by a double-site enzyme-linked pLDH immunodetection and ^{3}\text{H}-hypoxanthine incorporation (for confirmation) assays. Briefly, the synchronous ring stage cultures with parasitemia of 0.05% and 2% hematocrit were incubated with and without drug for 72 hours. The artesunate and DHA \text{IC}_{50} values for the sensitive strain were 10.7 nM i.e., 4.11 ng/mL and 13.8 nM i.e., 3.92 ng/mL, respectively. Although there was a trend (based on slope of the curve) towards an increase in \text{IC}_{50} values for ART-R strains collected at lower doses, significantly higher artesunate and DHA \text{IC}_{50} values (82.5 nM i.e., ng/mL and 54.6 nM i.e., 31.71 and 15.5 ng/mL, respectively) were reported for ART-R_{120} strain (after single dose); for the ART-T_{240} strain, the artesunate and DHA \text{IC}_{50} values were 100.3 nM and 100.3 nM (i.e., 38.56 and 28.52 ng/mL), respectively. Similar shift in \text{IC}_{50} values was observed for parasites from the 2-day dosing regimen.
L2235: The resistant parasites (ART-R) were processed for isolation of DNA, PCR/restriction fragment length polymorphism (RFLP) analyses to assess polymorphisms in the 451 PCR fragment^{24}; RFLP assessment revealed no mutation in the ART-R_{240}, ART-R_{\text{DD}38.4}, or the parent PAM strain.
L2236: PCR-sequencing was performed for the *RAD5*, *cNBP*, *RPB9*, *PK7*, *FP2A*, *Pfg27*, *chloroquine resistance transporter* (*Pfcrt*), *Na^{+}/H^{+} exchanger* (*Pfnhe*), two fragments overlapping the *kelch13* propeller domain, and *multidrug resistant* (*Pfmdr1*) genes of *P. falciparum*. There was no difference in the sequencing of the putative *Kelch13* propeller domain between control (3D7, PAMwt) and ART-R strains; also, there were no sequence changes in the 611 bp *PfNHE* fragment gene, flanking the DNNND repeat, which is related to quinine resistance. The changes observed include the following:
L2237: - A mutation identified in the *RAD5* homolog, in all of the ART-R parasites recovered from mice, that were resistant to single doses of 38.4 mg/kg, 120 mg/kg, and 240 mg/kg artesunate, and to a 2-day regimen of 80 mg/kg/day artesunate; the *RAD5* mutation was not identified in the wild type parent strain and in any of 4 control clinical *P. falciparum* isolates collected from Brazil, Senegal, Comoro Islands, and Thailand in the late 1990s.
- An amplification of the *Pfmdr1* gene copy number from 1 to 2 copies as well as *N86Y* mutation after in vivo artemisinin drug pressure.
L2238: Plasma concentration of DHA, measured at 1- and 2-hour after administration of 120 mg/kg artesunate in four ART-R_{120}-infected mice, were between 1573-3219 ng/mL; the DHA half-life was between 20.9-53.2 minutes.
L2239: ^{24} This is the orthologous gene in *P. falciparum* (PF3D7_0104300) identified based on studies with *Plasmodium chaubaudi* (PCHAS020720)), a rodent malaria parasite. A non-synonymous point mutation of *ubp1* in *P. chaubaudi* (PCHAS020720) was reported by others as being a marker of ART resistance in a rodent model.
L2240: Overall, the studies suggest a potential for development of resistance to artesunate in the blood stage parasites of *Plasmodium* species. Studies in the *P. falciparum* humanized murine immunocompromised model suggest that delay in parasite clearance time was not associated with increased IC50 values; however, in stable resistant strains, there was an association with an increase in the IC50 values as well as mutation in the *RAD5* but not in the *Kelch13* gene.
L2241: ## 18.5.4.4 Clinical studies
L2242: ## - *P. falciparum*
L2243: Resistance to artemisinins, including artesunate and DHA, by the *P. falciparum* parasites is clinically manifested as recrudescence and/or delay in parasite clearance time [(Bunnag et al. 1991); (Alin et al. 1995); (Looareesuwan et al. 1997); (Krudsood et al., 2000); (Krudsood et al. 2003); (Dondorp et al. 2009); (Ashley et al. 2014); (Takala-Harrison et al. 2015); (Ménard et al. 2016); (Thanh et al. 2017)]. This is thought to be due to short half-life of the artemisinin drugs. Therefore, artemisinin drugs are administered in combination with long-acting drugs such as lumefantrine.
L2244: There is no standardized assay to evaluate the in vitro sensitivity of the isolates to artemisinins, including artesunate and DHA. Recent studies (Witkowski et al., 2013; Klonis et al., 2013) suggest that a ring-stage survival assay may be useful for testing sensitivity to artemisinins; there was a good correlation between in vitro sensitivity and a delay in parasite clearance time. Thanh et al. (2017) reported that recrudescence was associated with a decrease in the ex vivo response to DHA as measured by the ring survival assay.
L2245: A delay in parasite clearance time and/or recrudescence has been shown to be associated with alterations in some genetic regions, such as *Kelch13* and *Pfmdr1*, of *P. falciparum* isolates from some of the regions.
L2246: ## - *Mutations in Kelch13*
L2247: A delay in parasite clearance time has been reported in Cambodia, Laos, Myanmar, Thailand, China, Vietnam, Bangladesh, Cambodia and Africa (Dondorp et al., 2009; Ashley et al., 2014; Takala-Harrison et al., 2015; Ménard et al., 2016; Thanh et al., 2017). In some of the countries, such as Bangladesh, Cambodia, Laos, Myanmar, Thailand, and Vietnam, *P. falciparum* parasites with mutation(s) in any of the *Kelch13* domains were associated with longer parasitic clearance time compared to parasites with wild type (WT) alleles; several mutations were identified, with C580Y being the most prevalent [(Ariey et al. 2014); (Takala-Harrison et al., 2015); (Thanh et al., 2017)]. Based on the surveillance studies in 59 countries, the *Kelch13* Artemisinin Resistance Multicenter Assessment (KARMA) consortium reported 108 non-synonymous *Kelch13* mutations with regional differences; e.g., in Cambodia-Vietnam-Laos the dominant mutation was C580Y whereas in Thailand-Myanmar-China the dominant mutation was F446I (Tilley et al., 2016). In sub-Saharan Africa, however, no *Kelch13* mutations and changes in vitro sensitivity of the parasites were reported [(Taylor et al. 2015); Fall et al., 2015; (Kremsner et al. 2016)].
L2248: Based on the surveillance studies conducted by the CDC in the US, no *Kelch13* resistant marker was reported in 77 of the 78 patients from Africa; also, no resistance marker was identified in any of the 5 patients from Central America (Mace *et al.*, 2018). Similar observations were reported recently (Mace, Arguin, and Tan 2018); of the 146 patients from Africa, A578S polymorphism was reported in 1 subject and not in any of the 2 subjects from Central America and the Caribbean.
L2249: The mechanism by which *PfKelch13* mutations/inactivation of kelch compartment proteins produce delay in parasite clearance is unclear. Studies suggest that *Kelch13* mutation may protect the resistant parasites from oxidative damage by reducing the levels of ubiquitinated and unfolded proteins ([Dogovski *et al.*, 2015]; (Mok *et al.* 2015)] and/or reduce endocytosis of hemoglobin from the host cell (Birnbaum *et al.*, 2020). Mbengue *et al.* (2015) reported an increased PfPI3K level was associated with the C580Y mutation in *Kelch13* of the resistant strain of *P. falciparum*; PfPI3K binding to C580Y *Kelch13* mutants, measured by an immunoprecipitation assay, was reduced in the resistant strains compared to the wild-type strain without *Kelch13* mutation. Mutations in *Kelch13* may decrease the degradation of PfPI3K leading to an increase in PfPI3K levels; this may allow ring-stage *P. falciparum* parasites to survive the oxidative stress induced by artemisinins by entering the quiescent stage.
L2250: Parasites may extend their ring-stage. Based on in vitro studies, Witkowski *et al.* (2010) showed that parasite is arrested at the ring-stage under drug pressure; however, cell cycle arrest was reversible after culture in drug free medium.
L2251: ## o *Mutations or increased copy number of pfmdr1 gene*
L2252: Treatment with a combination of artemether + lumefantrine selected for *pfmdr1* N86 ([Somé *et al.* 2010); (Ngasala *et al.* 2011); (Malmberg *et al.* 2013)] and Y184 (Somé *et al.*, 2010) alleles. Venkatesan *et al.* (Venkatesan *et al.* 2014) reported that increased copy number of *pfmdr1* and the presence of *pfmdr1* N86 were a significant independent risk factors for recrudescence in patients treated with Coartem; almost 50% samples from Thailand had ≥2 copies of *pfmdr1*, but this is rarely observed in African samples. Kremsner *et al.* (2016) reported that a delay in parasite clearance was associated with the N86Y *Pfmdr1* genotype in subjects treated with artesunate.
L2253: ## • *P. vivax, P. malariae, P. ovale and P. knowlesi*
L2254: No information available.
L2255: ## 1.1.2. Cross-resistance
L2256: ## 18.5.4.5 In vitro studies
L2257: In vitro activity of artesunate and DHA against chloroquine sensitive and resistant strains of *P. falciparum* was similar (Alin 1990; Le Bras, 1998; Ringwald 1999, Vivas 2008; Wongsrichanalai *et al.*, 1999; for details see Section 18.5.2).
L2258: Wongsrichanalai *et al.* (1999) reported higher artesunate IC_{50} values for isolates collected during 1991-1997, from the Thai-Myanmar and Thai-Cambodian borders compared to those from Southern Thailand. There was a strong correlation between the artesunate and mefloquine as well as artesunate and quinine IC_{50} values.
L2259: Brockman *et al.* (2000) reported a positive correlation between the *in vitro* sensitivity to artesunate and quinine (r=0.43, P&lt;0.001), mefloquine (r=0.46, P&lt;0.001), and halofantrine (r=0.51, P &lt; 0.001). It is noted that the isolates tested were recrudescent isolates, collected between March 1995 and July 1999 from patients presenting with falciparum malaria within 42 days of treatment with artesunate + mefloquine.
L2260: ## **18.5.4.6 *In vivo (Animal studies)***
L2261: Tyagi *et al.* (2018) reported that immunocompromised mice infected with an artesunate resistant strain of *P. falciparum* (ART-R240) and treated with either 3 doses of quinine (73 mg/kg every 8 hours, IV), 4 doses of halofantrine (1 mg/kg, every 24 hours), and/or one dose of amodiaquine (73 mg/kg, oral) were resistant to quinine, amodiaquine, and halofantrine both in vivo and ex vivo; also artesunate plus amodiaquine were ineffective in vivo. Additionally, ART-R120 and ART-R240 strain had an amplified *pfm* gene. However, there was no change in IC_{50} values for other drugs (chloroquine, mefloquine, lumefantrine, and pyrimethamine); also, artesunate plus mefloquine were effective in vivo. The parasites of the parent sensitive strain (PAM) were sensitive to all drugs.
L2262: ## 18.6 Clinical Review of Specific Studies/Clinical Trials with IV Artesunate
L2263: ## 18.6.1 SEAQUAMAT
L2264: Primary outcome of mortality in SEAQUAMAT was analyzed for baseline characteristics including demographics, clinical status and laboratory evaluations for this study - See Section 8.1.1.
L2265: ## 18.6.1.1 *Study Design and Analysis*
L2266: Results for SEAQUAMAT submitted in the NDA consisted of a reanalysis of legacy datasets obtained from the principle investigator, Arjen Dondorp, MD., PhD. Datasets were analyzed by the [REDACTED] (b) (4). Because the original analysis dataset collected clinical events (adverse events) and concomitant medications in a single text field, coding was performed to split the variables into adae.xpt and adcm.xpt files. The CSR was drafted by the [REDACTED] (b) (4).
L2267: **Figure 18-12: SEAQUAMAT – Diagram of Study Structure**
L2268: Data safety monitoring committee
L2269: Source: SEAQUAMAT Protocol p. 20
L2270: ## **18.6.1.2 Study Results – Safety**
L2271: The primary objective of SEAQUAMAT was to evaluate efficacy of IV artesunate in the treatment of severe malaria, with the primary endpoint in-hospital mortality. Secondary outcomes included the incidence of neurological sequelae, combined death or neurological sequelae, recovery times (time to eat, speak, sit and discharge), and development of severe complications. Beyond these outcome measures, the CRF fields included a baseline assessment at the time of admission that assessed the presence or absence of clinical conditions associated with severe malaria, including coma, severe prostration, convulsions, severe anemia, anuria/oliguria, hypotension, suspected metabolic acidosis, respiratory insufficiency, blackwater fever, clinical jaundice, hypoglycemia, and a free text field. The CRF also collected data on Glasgow or Blantyre coma scales at baseline, vital signs and clinical history including previously administered medications. In addition to blood smears, parasite counts, limited laboratory evaluations were performed on admission, including anion gap, base excess, BUN, chloride, venous CO2, glucose, Hb, Hct, potassium, sodium, venous pH and total CO2. Laboratory evaluations following admission were not routinely reported.
L2272: The CRF also collected “complications not present on admission” which were coded as CETERM “After Admission” in the ADAE dataset. These clinical events included the following: ICU admission; coma, not present on admission or deterioration in GCS; convulsions not present on admission, shock (systolic blood pressure less than 80 mmHg); vasopressor drugs required, respiratory insufficiency or mechanical ventilation, renal failure (BUN &gt; 60 mg/dL or urine output &lt; 400 mL/dL; severe anemia (Hct &lt; 20%); severe bleeding (requiring blood transfusion); blackwater fever/hemoglobinuria; hypoglycemia (glucose &lt; 40 mg/dL; severe jaundice; suspected sepsis; antibiotics; in case of pregnancy: still birth or spontaneous abortion during admission; and a free text box for other complications.
L2273: The outcome was recorded as either the patient died (at home or in hospital and date of death) or survived (date of discharge). The duration of symptoms (in days) was recorded in time to speak, time to sit independently, and time to eat independently.
L2274: The following neurological sequelae were specified on the CRF, with the investigator instructed to tick if “yes”: cortical blindness, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia, loss of balance, extrapyramidal rigidity, hemiplegia/pareisis/neuropsychiatric (like psychosis), and other significant neurological sequelae on discharge with a free text field to provide details.
L2275: The CRF also captured “other medical problems on discharge” in a free text field.

L2299: ## On Discharge
L2300: If the patient survived, limited information on the clinical status of the patient was collected at discharge. The CRF collected information on the duration of symptoms by recording the days required for the following:
L2301: - Time to speak
- Time to sit independently
- Time to eat independently
L2302: The CRF also captured information on neurological and other sequelae including the following tick boxes to check if present (more than one tick was permitted):
L2303: - Cortical blindness
- Cranial nerve palsies
- Ataxia
- Loss of balance
- Extrapyramidal rigidity
- Hemiplegia/pareisis
- Neuropsychiatric (like psychosis)
- Other significant neurological sequelae on discharge; if ticked specify:
- Other significant medical problems; if ticked specify:
- Comments
L2304: See Section 8.1.1 for discussion of secondary endpoints including neurological outcomes. "Significant neurological sequelae" were reported in 4 (0.5%) of artesunate recipients compared with zero quinine recipients; hemiplegia/pareisis and ataxia were also observed in 2 artesunate recipients each versus no quinine recipients. In addition to neurological sequelae, at the time of discharge patients were evaluated for other significant medical problems with specific information collected in a free text field. Of note, more events were observed in the "significant medical problem" category in artesunate recipients: 36 (4.6%) in artesunate group versus 28 (3.8%) in the quinine group.
L2305: When querying the "other significant medical problems" category, multiple descriptions were provided included. For artesunate recipients these included the following observations (note: some patients were reported with more than one condition):
L2306: - anemia (7 patients, Hb range 3.6 – 5%);
- left hospital against medical advice (6 patients);
- HIV positive patient- (5 patients);
- acute or persistent renal failure (4 patients);
- pulmonary TB (3 patients)
- diabetes type 1 (2 patients, 1 with diabetic ketoacidosis), Diabetes type 2 (2 patients);
L2307: - elevated BUN/Creatinine (2 patients);
- vertigo but no objective neuro deficit;
- pregnancy 24-25 weeks;
- persistent, intermittent hiccups;
- vertigo and weakness;
- keratitis due to pre-existing lagophthalmos
- benign prostrate hypertrophy;
- mitral regurgitation;
- collagen disease (? SLE);
- aplastic (hypoplastic) anemia;
L2308: The single patient reported with aplastic anemia on discharge (Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was a 17 years old male from Myanmar who had received four doses of artesunate. He had a positive blood smear for *P. f.* malaria, parasitemia &lt; 10%, and discharge on day 6. No hematology labs were found.

L2314: ## **18.6.2 CDC-060**
L2315: Title: Retrospective Abstraction of Data Collected During IND Protocol CDC-060 – Intravenous Artesunate for the Treatment of Severe Malaria in the United States.
L2316: ## **18.6.2.1 Study Objectives**
L2317: To evaluate the safety, tolerability, and potential clinical benefits of IV artesunate for patients in United States hospitals with severe and complicated malaria who do not have access to quinidine, do not tolerate that drug, or whose infection does not respond to quinidine treatment.
L2318: ## **18.6.2.2 Study Design and Endpoints**
L2319: Data reported were retrospectively abstracted from medical records of subjects with malaria in the US enrolled in a treatment IND application from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) from August 2007- December 2010. Subjects were to receive four doses of
L2320: IVAS over 48 hours. Data were collected on the safety and the clinical course of the disease from administration of the first dose of IV artesunate until the subject transitioned to follow-on oral therapy (or a maximum of 7 days). These data were collected on a data abstraction template (DAT) that was developed by the US Army. The data were quality assured and then presented before a Clinical Benefits Endpoint Committee (CBEC), with a majority vote used to make decisions on inclusion of clinical benefit and safety outcomes.
L2321: **Table 18-33: CDC-060 – Schedule of Events**

L2325: Source: CDC-060 Protocol, Table 3, p. 30
L2326: ## **18.6.2.3 Criteria for Evaluation (Endpoints)**
L2327: ## **Safety**
L2328: - Physical examination
- Vital signs
- Clinical laboratory tests (chemistry, hematology, malaria microscopy)
- Adverse events (AEs)
L2329: ## **Clinical Benefit (Efficacy)**
L2330: - Percentage of subjects who completed IVAS therapy as defined by the protocol
L2331: - Percentage of subjects who were able to receive a follow-on oral antimalarial therapy
- Time to negative parasitology
- Time to start of a follow-on oral therapy
- Time to discharge from the intensive care unit (ICU)
- Serial parasite density
- Change in clinical signs, laboratory parameters, and symptoms of severe malaria, including an evaluation of the improvement, worsening, development of, no change, or never present status of:Parasitemia
- Impaired consciousness
- Seizures
- Circulatory collapse/shock
- Pulmonary edema or acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS)
- Acidosis
- Acute renal failure
- Abnormal bleeding or disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)
- Jaundice
- Severe anemia
- Ability to take oral medication
L2332: ## **18.6.2.4 Key Inclusion Criteria**
L2333: Subjects eligible for IV artesunate treatment were those for whom there was a microscopy-confirmed diagnosis of severe malaria that required parenteral treatment, a strong clinical suspicion of severe malaria for which a timely, reliable microscopic diagnosis was not available, or the presence of uncomplicated malaria but an inability to take oral treatment. The administration of IV artesunate was requested because quinidine was either unavailable or could not be accessed in a timely manner, the patient experienced quinidine failure or intolerance, or the patient had contraindications to quinidine.
L2334: ## **18.6.2.5 Statistical Analysis Plan**
L2335: Continuous variables were summarized with descriptive statistics [n, mean, standard deviation (SD), median, minimum (min), and maximum (max)]. When appropriate, graphical summaries were used to describe distributions of numeric variables. Frequency tables (counts, percentages) were used to summarize categorical data. Confidence intervals (CI) (95%) for outcomes and AE rates were reported. Analysis populations were defined as follows:
L2336: - Enrolled analysis population (n=128): All subjects who had a CDC subject ID, and for whom IV artesunate was shipped.
- Safety analysis population (n=102): All subjects in the analysis population who received at least one dose of IV artesunate
- Evaluable analysis population (n=87): All subjects in the safety analysis population who met at least one of the criteria in Section A (malaria diagnosis) and Section B (need for IV treatment) of the Treatment IND.
L2337: - Inclusion criteria met and had microscopic confirmation of *P. falciparum malaria*.
- Per protocol analysis population (n=76): All subjects in the safety analysis population who met at least one criterion in Section A (malaria diagnosis), Section B (need for IV treatment), and Section C (IV artesunate desirable) of the Treatment IND Inclusion criteria.
L2338: Additional subgroup analyses included:
L2339: - Level of renal impairment, as measured by the estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), calculated using the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease (MDRD) formula
- Level of hepatic impairment, as measured by Model for End-Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score
- Subjects' concurrent antimalarial use during IVAS administration
- Quinidine exposure (any time prior to or during IVAS administration)
- Presence of seizures or impaired consciousness or ventilator support at baseline

L2376: **Reviewer Comments:** In CDC-060 2 of 92 (90%) patients received four 2.4 mg/kg doses of artesunate intravenously at 0, 12, 24, and 48 hours. 9 patients received less than 4 doses; 5 died before the regimen could be completed. Only one patient (# (b) (6)) received 5 doses. According to the patient narrative, this patient received 1.2 mg/kg/dose for the first 4 doses for unexplained reasons and 4.2 mg/kg/dose for the fifth dose.

L2397: *SAEs were not specifically recorded for this study. Grade 4 AEs (life-threateningAEs) were reported in lieu of SAEs.
L2398: Additional TEAEs that were considered life-threatening (coded in dataset as Grade 5) include the following: anemia (5%), elevated transaminases (3%), thrombocytopenia (2%), respiratory distress (1%), cholecystitis (1%), *C. difficile* colitis (1%), acute renal failure (1%), epistaxis (1%), petechiae (1%), mental status change (1%)
L2399: **Reviewer Comments:** Per the study protocol, safety and outcome data were collected until subject's death or discharge, for a maximum of seven days. Review of patient narratives revealed an additional patient who likely experienced a poor outcome. Subject (b) (6) was a 61-year old Asian male with a history of travel to Ghana who presented with fever and headache. His clinical status worsened over the next day and he developed coma and was intubated and treated with IV antibacterial drugs for sepsis. On the day following admission, a malaria smear was positive for *P. falciparum* and he was started on IV quinidine. His clinical status continued to deteriorate with renal failure and hypotension, and he was treated with pressors and received exchange, packed RBCs and platelet transfusions. On hospital day 4 he was started on IV artesunate and received a total of 3 doses. Over the following 4 weeks the patient's neurological status did not improve and he was discharged to a palliative care facility. No further information was provided.
L2400: ## 18.6.2.6.3 Treatment Emergent Adverse Events
L2401: **Reviewer comments:** Limited safety data were collected under the original IND so additional data were retrieved by the US Army and CDC from patient medical records. Datasets included information on mortality, adverse events, and laboratory data were reported only until death or discharge for a maximum of 7 days. Some additional information was available in patients' narratives, for example, on pregnancy outcomes.
L2402: The lack of a comparator arm in CDC-060 makes it difficult to ascribe causality to the adverse reactions observed. Most of the reported adverse events in this study may be attributable to the condition under treatment, further complicating interpretation of safety as it was not possible to determine which AEs would have occurred more commonly with Artesunate for Injection than
L2403: *with the comparator if one were used.*

L2409: ## *Transfusions*
L2410: Forty-nine subjects received a total of 102 transfusions of packed RBCs from day -1 to day 8. Since data on transfusions with blood products and laboratory evaluations were provided only until hospital discharge or day 8, information on transfusions after day 8 was not reported. LDH and haptoglobin levels were not obtained; therefore, it was not possible to evaluate the occurrence delayed hemolysis after artesunate therapy.

L2434: ## 18.6.3 WRAIR Study 1168 (Phase 2 Uncomplicated Malaria)
L2435: Title: A Phase II, Open Label, Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Efficacy and Pharmacokinetics of Intravenous Artesunate in Adults with Uncomplicated Malaria
L2436: ## **18.6.3.1 Study Objectives**
L2437: - Determine the full pharmacokinetic profile for cGMP intravenous artesunate formulation in adults with uncomplicated *falciparum* malaria
- Determine the effectiveness of the new GMP intravenous artesunate formulation to clear *Plasmodium falciparum* parasites from the blood
- Evaluate the safety of intravenous artesunate in Kenyan adult populations with uncomplicated falciparum malaria
L2438: ## **18.6.3.2 Study Design**
L2439: This was an unblinded, non-randomized, phase 2 clinical study of intravenous artesunate for the treatment of uncomplicated *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria in adults in Kenya. This study enrolled 30 subjects, age 18 to 65 years who received intravenous artesunate (2.4 mg/kg) once a day for 3 day, followed by Malarone for parasitologic cure. Pharmacokinetic samples were obtained on Day 0 at 0, 5 min, 10 min, 20 min, 40 min, 1 h, 2 h, 4 h, and 6 h) on Day 0. Safety laboratories were performed on Day 3 and repeated on Days 7 and 14 if abnormal. Adverse events were observed at each visit. Malaria blood films were obtained at enrollment at twice daily for the first 3 days, then once daily on Days 3, 7, and 14.
L2440: **Table 18-49: Study 1168 – Schedule of Assessments**

L2450: ## **18.6.3.3 Endpoints**
L2451: - Pharmacokinetics: pharmacokinetic analysis of the 2.4 mg/kg dose in adults over 6 hours
- Efficacy: 99% parasite reduction at 48 hours, parasite clearance times (PCTs), fever clearance times (FCTs)
- Safety: the number and severity of adverse events (AE) and serious adverse events (SAE).
L2452: ## **18.6.3.4 Statistical Analysis Plan**
L2453: ## **Efficacy**
L2454: Parasite reduction ratio at 48 hours was determined using the following formula:
L2455: Peak parasitemia – Parasitemia at 48 hours / Peak parasitemia X 100%
L2456: Parasite clearance time was determined as the time (hours) parasites are first not detected if followed by 2 negative smears. Fever clearance time was the time (hours) to afebrile (&lt;37.5°C) if sustained for 24 hours.
L2457: ## **Safety**
L2458: Safety parameters, which included vital signs, clinical signs or symptoms, and clinical laboratory measurements, were presented using counts and percentages for categorical data and continuous data were presented using the mean, SD, and number of subjects. Adverse events were coded using the Medical Dictionary of Regulatory Affairs (MedDRA) preferred terms within each system, organ, and class (SOC) grouping. Frequencies were presented by counting the number of subjects who reported the AE daily. Multiple reports in the same subject were only counted once per subject.
L2459: Efficacy results: all subjects received 100% parasite clearance by the end of Day 2.
L2460: ## **18.6.3.5 Study Results – Safety**
L2461: ## **18.6.3.6 Key Inclusion Criteria**
L2462: - Adults and non-pregnant females, 18-65 years
- Fever &gt;37.5 C during present illness
- Falciparum malaria, greater than or equal to 200 parasites/\muL
L2463: Key exclusion criteria included mixed malarial infection (malaria other than *falciparum* malaria monoinfection as detected by screening blood smear) and severe *falciparum* malaria as defined by the WHO.

L2465: All subjects (N=30) received 3 doses of IV artesunate and completed the study. A

L2475: ## **18.6.3.6.2 Deaths and Serious Adverse Events**
L2476: There were no deaths in Study 1168 and one SAE, in a 54 year old female subject (Subject # (b) (6) who developed Stevens-Johnson syndrome (verbatim term bullous erythema multiforme) and was hospitalized. Symptoms of pruritis developed on Day 3, following completion of 3 days of IV artesunate at 2.4 mg/kg/day and within one hour 40 minutes of receiving the second dose of concomitant Malarone. The prescribing information for Malarone notes that Stevens-Johnson syndrome has been reported as a post-marketing adverse event.25 Given the concomitant administration of Malarone which, it is not possible to determine an unequivocal association with IV artesunate alone. The following patient summary was provided:
L2477: After the second dose of Malarone on Day 3 ( (b) (6) the subject complained of pruritis and rash. The subject was treated with chlorpheniramine (4 mg) and prescribed a topical steroid cream (Temoate). The final dose of Malarone was not given. On Day 4, the subject had elevated temperatures (max 39.9), labile blood pressures, and a worsening rash. The subject also complained of increased pain at the rash sites which were noted to have progressed to bullous lesions with a dusky erythematous background. The subject was re-admitted to the hospital, administered IV fluids (1000 L NS) and 250 mg hydrocortisone. The subject improved over the following 4 hours (Day 5, (b) (6)). Aspiration of bullous fluid revealed mixtures of eosinophils, lymphocytes and occasional neutrophil. Gram stain was negative for bacteria. Dermatological assessments revealed lesions suggestive of bullous erythema multiforme likely resulting from Malarone administration combined with occult parasitic infection, and autoimmune disease. An allergist at Walter Reed Army Medical Center was consulted and the subject was placed on ranitidine adjunctive therapy and a tapered steroid course (oral prednisone). Tramadol was used for effective pain control, and the subject remained in the ward for rash and symptom management.
L2478: The subject improved over the following 10 days with no further progression of the bullous lesions. On Day 16 (b) (6) the patient was discharged with instructions to follow-up within 48 hours. On Day 17 (b) (6) study staff went to the home of the subject for follow up and were informed that the subject had been admitted to another hospital. The subject was finally located walking in town without difficulty. On Day 18 (b) (6) study staff once again went the subject's residence, and at that time the subject refused any further follow up. [From NDA 213035 SN1, Module 5, Section 5.3.4 2, Clinical Study Report Study 1168.]

L2504: ## 18.6.4 WRAIR Study 1263ab (Phase 2 Uncomplicated Malaria)
L2505: Study Title: A Phase II, Randomized, Open-label, Dose-ranging Study of Intravenous Artesunate Therapy for the Treatment of Acute, Uncomplicated *Plasmodium falciparum* Malaria
L2506: ## 18.6.4.1 Study Objectives
L2507: Primary:
L2508: - Compare 4 regimens of WRAIR formulation of intravenous artesunate for the treatment of uncomplicated *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria using the pharmacodynamic endpoints of parasite clearance to identify the most efficacious treatment regimen.
L2509: Secondary:
L2510: - Evaluate the tolerability of these different intravenous artesunate regimens, in order to gain information to support dose selection for future studies of intravenous artesunate
L2511: ## 18.6.4.2 Study Design
L2512: This was a randomized, open-label, dose-ranging study of four treatment regimens of intravenous artesunate in male and non-pregnant female subjects with symptomatic uncomplicated *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria. The study was conducted in 200 subjects, 100 from each study site (Thailand and Kenya). Fifty subjects were initially randomly placed in treatment cohorts 1 and 2 – Cohort 1: 2.4 mg/kg dose IV artesunate once daily for 3 days, followed by 25 mg/kg oral mefloquine (Thailand) or Malarone (Kenya); and Cohort 2: 2.4 mg/kg/dose of IV artesunate initially, 12 hours later on the same day (Day 0) and then once daily on Days 1 and 2, followed by oral mefloquine (Thailand) or Malarone (Kenya). After these cohorts were treated and the safety data from the 8 mg/kg dose given in the Phase I study 1142 were reviewed, 50 additional subjects were randomly placed into Cohorts 3 and 4 – Cohort 3: 1.2 mg/kg IV artesunate given once daily for 3 days followed by mefloquine (Thailand) or Malarone (Kenya); Cohort 4: 4.8 mg/kg of IV artesunate given once daily for 3 days followed by oral mefloquine (Thailand) or Malarone (Kenya).
L2513: The following table lists the baseline and screening assessments and the study schedule through Day 28.

L2517: Source: Adapted from Study 1163ab CSR Appendix 11.1.1.1, 7.1 Study design and plan
L2518: ## 18.6.4.3 Endpoints
L2519: ## Efficacy:
L2520: - Efficacy was defined by parasite clearance quantified using 3 outcome measures: the ability to clear at least 90% of asexual parasites from the peripheral blood by 48 hours after commencing IV artesunate treatment using the Giemsa-stained blood films for malaria as the diagnostic method of parasitemia quantification, the time to 90% and 100% parasite clearance denoted as parasite clearance times (PCT90 and PCT100) and the parasite reductions ratios at 12 and 24 hours after initial drug treatment (PRR12h and PRR24h), and the area under the curve (AUC) of quantifiable parasitemia.
L2521: ## Safety:
L2522: - Safety and tolerability of the treatment regimens were assessed through rates of adverse events and laboratories (hematology and chemistry). Fever clearance time, defined as the time from study drug administration until a subject's oral temperature first falls and then falls below 37.5°C and remains there for more than 24 hours, was also evaluated.
L2523: ## 18.6.4.4 Key Inclusion Criteria
L2524: ## Thailand:
L2525: - Acute symptomatic *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria infection with a parasite density of ≥5000 asexual parasites/µL as determined by malaria smear
- Male and non-pregnant female subjects from 18 to 65 years of age
L2526: ## Kenya:
L2527: - Acute symptomatic *Plasmodium falciparum* malaria infection with a parasite density of ≥5000 asexual parasites/µL as determined by malaria smear
L2528: - Male and non-pregnant female subjects from 5 to 65 years of age.
L2529: **Exclusion criteria** included pregnancy, clinical evidence of severe malaria, mixed malaria infection by malaria smear.
L2530: ## **18.6.4.5 Statistical Analysis Plan**
L2531: The primary endpoint was a discrete variable (yes/no) denoting the efficacy to clear at least 90% of asexual *P. falciparum* parasites from the peripheral blood by 48 hours after administration of IV artesunate. A point estimate (Chi-Square or similar discrete variable analysis) and 95% confidence interval was constructed, and pair-wise comparisons were made employing a Bonferroni adjustment for the multiple comparisons across the groups. Summary statistics (mean, standard deviation, minimum, maximum, n, and coefficient of variation) were calculated for parasite clearance times (PCT90 and PCT100) and parasite reduction ratios (PRR12h, PRR24h, PRR48h, and PRR 72h). Comparisons between groups were made using an ANOVA or a similar non-parametric approach if data were not normally distributed. Secondary outcomes included determination of fever clearance times, and descriptive summary of adverse events, especially hematologic, associated with intravenous administration of artesunate. A temperature versus time curve was produced for each subject, and summary statistics were calculated for fever clearance time

L2548: **Efficacy Results:** There were no subject deaths in this study.
L2549: All dose groups had 90% parasite clearance by 48 hours and 100% clearance by 72 hours. In Kenya, parasite clearance was significantly reduced in the 4.8 mg/kg once daily for three days compared to the 2.4 mg/kg twice on day 0 and once daily for 2 more days. The Applicant postulates that this effect may have been due to artemisinin resistance, as this effect was not observed in Kenya, which at the time of the study was considered artemisinin-naïve.
L2550: **Figure 18-25: Study 1263ab – Fever Clearance by Study Dose**
L2551: Source: Clinical Reviewer (JReview)
L2552: All patients enrolled in this study had *P. falciparum*. Despite an exclusion criterion of mixed infection, two subjects in Kenya were enrolled having a mixed infection with *P. falciparum* and *P. ovale*. Patient #(b)(6) was a 13-year old male who received IV artesunate at 1.2 mg/kg once daily for three days. Patient #(b)(6) was a 15-year old female who received IV artesunate at 4.8 mg/kg once daily for three days. Both patients, became smear negative for *P. falciparum* by Day
L2553: 3 of treatment. Clearance of *P. ovale*, occurred by Day 2. See discussion of Study 1263ab in Section 9.2.

L2576: ## 18.6.4.6.3 Laboratory Findings
L2577: White blood cells and neutrophils showed a general decline during IV artesunate therapy and then showed a strong response on Day 3, recovering to normal by Day 7. Higher dose groups appeared to take longer to recover to baseline.

L2590: ## 18.6.5 WRAIR Study 1128
L2591: ## Title
L2592: A Phase 1a Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Dose Escalation Study of the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Single Intravenous Doses of Artesunate Administered to Healthy Subjects
L2593: ## 18.6.5.1 Study Objectives
L2594: ## Primary:
L2595: - To determine the safety of a single dose administration of artesunate in doses that bracket the anticipated compassionate use dose of 2.4 mg/kg of artesunate by measuring adverse events (AEs) and cardiovascular responses [heart rate (HR), blood pressure (BP), and electrocardiogram parameters (ECG)].
L2596: ## Secondary:
L2597: - To determine the safety and tolerability of the compassionate use anticipated dose of 2.4 mg/kg of artesunate given intravenously (IV).
- To assess the pharmacokinetics of artesunate and its major metabolite Dihydroartemisinin (DHA) in healthy volunteers.
L2598: ## 18.6.5.2 Study Design
L2599: This study was a Phase 1a, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, inpatient, ascending single IV dose study to determine the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics of Intravenous artesunate in healthy male and non-pregnant, non-lactating female subjects aged 18 to 55 years old.
L2600: Five cohorts of 8 volunteers per cohort were studied. Within each cohort, 6 subjects received a single dose of Intravenous Artesunate infused over a period of 2 minutes at one of 5 specified dose levels, 0.5 mg/kg, 1.0 mg/kg, 2.0 mg/kg, 4.0 mg/kg, and 8.0 mg/kg, and 2 subjects in each cohort received a similar infusion of placebo (mannitol in phosphate buffer). Subjects underwent an outpatient screening visit for medical history, vital signs, physical exam (with a standardized neurologic assessment), hematology parameters including complete blood cell (CBC) count with platelets and differentials, coagulation indices, blood chemistries, urinalysis, urine drug screen, and, if female, a serum pregnancy test. Within 21 days of initial screening visit, eligible subjects were scheduled for a 6-hour outpatient visit in which
L2601: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213036
## Artesunate for Injection
L2602: pre-dose ECGs and vital signs were obtained over the same period of time to compare with those obtained during investigational product dosing on Day 1.
L2603: On Day 0, subjects were admitted to the CPU to begin the inpatient phase of the study, and underwent a brief physical exam. On Day 1, pre-dose vital signs and ECG were performed. Subjects then received investigational product. Subjects were closely monitored by evaluating vital signs (pre-dose, approximately 5 min, 20 min, 40 min, 1 h, 2h, 4 h, 6h, 8 h, and 24 h after completion of infusion), ECG (pre-dose, and approximately 5 min, 20 min, 40 min, 1 h, 2 h, 4 h, 6 h, and 24 h after completion of infusion, and assessment of spontaneously reported AEs over the next 24 hours. Blood was drawn for pharmacokinetic pre-dose, approximately 5 min, 20 min, 40 min, 1 h, 2 h, 4 h, 6 h, and 24 h post-dose, and when directed by AEs. Blood for hematology, chemistry, and coagulation marker analysis was collected 6 h and 24 h post dose. Urinalysis was also performed 6 h and 24 h post dose. On Day 2, subjects were discharged. Outpatient follow-up included Day 4 vital signs, ECG, hematology, blood chemistry, and urinalysis, Day 8 hematology, vital signs, and ECG, and Day 15 hematology, vital signs, ECG, urine pregnancy test, and physical exam.
